Boundary value problems and Hardy spaces for singular Schrödinger equations with block structure Thanks: This work was supported by the Royal Society (IES \ \backslash R3 \ \backslash 193232). Thanks: No data were created or analysed in this study.
Arnaud Dumont, Andrew J. Morris

Abstract
. We obtain Riesz transform bounds and characterise operator-adapted Hardy spaces to solve boundary value problems for singular Schrödinger equations − div ( A ∇ u ) + a V u = 0 -\textup{div}(A\nabla u)+aVu=0 in the upper half-space ℝ + 1 + n \mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+} with boundary dimension n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 . The coefficients ( A , a , V ) (A,a,V) are assumed to be independent of the transversal direction to the boundary, and consist of a complex-elliptic pair ( A , a ) (A,a) that is bounded and measurable with a certain block structure, and a non-negative singular potential V V in the reverse Hölder class RH q ​ ( ℝ n ) \textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) for q ≥ max ⁡ { n 2 , 2 } q\geq\max\left\{\frac{n}{2},2\right\} . This block structure is significant because it allows for coefficients that are not symmetric but for which L 2 ​ ( ℝ n ) \mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) -solvability persists due to recently obtained Kato square root type estimates. We find extrapolation intervals for exponents p p around 2 on which the Dirichlet problem is well-posed for boundary data in L p ​ ( ℝ n ) \mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) , and the associated Regularity problem is well-posed for boundary data in Sobolev spaces 𝒱 ˙ 1 , p ​ ( ℝ n ) \dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) that are adapted to the potential V V , when p > 1 p>1 . The well-posedness of these Dirichlet problems and related estimates then allow us to solve the corresponding Neumann problem with boundary data in L p ​ ( ℝ n ) \mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) . The results permit boundary data in the Dziubaǹski–Zienkiewicz Hardy space H V 1 ​ ( ℝ n ) \textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) and adapted Hardy–Sobolev spaces H ˙ V 1 , p ​ ( ℝ n ) \dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) when p ≤ 1 p\leq 1 . We also obtain comparability of square functions and nontangential maximal functions for the solutions with their boundary data.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35J25 (Primary) 35J10, 35J25, 42B37, 47D06, 47A60 (Secondary)

1.
Introduction

We consider singular Schrödinger equations
-\textup{div}(A\nabla u)+\widetilde{V}u=0
in the upper half-space
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}:=\left\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:t>0\right\}
, with boundary
\partial\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is the prototypical domain for understanding solvability of associated boundary value problems on Lipschitz domains. The consideration of
t
-independent coefficients
A(t,x)=A(x)
and
\widetilde{V}(t,x)=\widetilde{V}(x)
is also significant for allowing minimal regularity coefficients, as the necessary transversal regularity determined by Caffarelli–Fabes–Kenig in
[
22
]
is then typically reached via perturbation. Amid recent efforts to characterise solvability for such equations when the principal coefficient matrix
A
is not symmetric and the potential
\widetilde{V}\equiv 0
, results for block structure coefficients

(1.1)

A=\begin{bmatrix}A_{\perp\perp}&0\\
0&A_{\parallel\parallel}\end{bmatrix}\text{, where $A_{\perp\perp}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}$ and $A_{\parallel\parallel}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}$},

have been indispensable (e.g.,
[
27
,
43
]
). In that case, solvability for
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-data and extrapolation to
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-type data are both deeply connected to the resolution of the Kato square root conjecture by Auscher–Hofmann–Lacey–McIntosh–Tchamitchian in
[
12
]
. A comprehensive treatment of the state of the art can be found in the monograph by Auscher–Egert
[
11
]
, which unifies and improves a variety of techniques to treat Dirichlet, Regularity and Neumann problems for many boundary data spaces, including those of
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-type, in ranges likely to be the best possible.

These boundary value problems are far less understood in the presence of a non-zero singular potential
\widetilde{V}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
when the Schrödinger equation can be expressed in the form

(1.2)

-\sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n}\partial_{i}\left(A_{ij}\partial_{j}u\right)\left(t,x\right)+\widetilde{V}(x)u(t,x)=0,

where
\partial_{0}:=\partial_{t}
and
\left(\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{n}\right)=\nabla_{x}
on
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
. The
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-solvability for Regularity and Neumann problems was recently obtained by Morris–Turner in
[
51
]
for a class of reverse Hölder potentials. The approach once again exploited the connection with the Kato conjecture but the results showed how the presence of a singular potential fundamentally changes the approach. This paper extrapolates those results to obtain
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-type solvability for Dirichlet, Regularity and Neumann problems, utilising
tools for singular potentials from
[
51
]
to extend the extrapolation framework in
[
11
]
.

More specifically, the principal coefficients
A
are assumed to be
t
-independent and measurable with the block structure (
1.1
). The potential
\widetilde{V}
is assumed to be
t
-independent and given by
\widetilde{V}(t,\cdot)=aV
for some measurable function
a:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
and non-negative function
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. This allows us to express (
1.2
) in the form

(1.3)

\mathscr{H}u:=-\partial_{t}\left(A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u\right)-\textup{div}_{x}\left({A_{\parallel\parallel}\nabla_{x}u}\right)+aVu=0.

We assume that there are constants
0<\lambda\leq\Lambda<\infty
with the bound

(1.4)

\max\left\{\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}))},\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\right\}\leq\Lambda

and ellipticity

(1.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{Re}{{\int}}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\bigg(A(x)\begin{bmatrix}f(x)\\
\nabla_{x}g(x)\end{bmatrix}\cdot\overline{\begin{bmatrix}f(x)\\
\nabla_{x}g(x)\end{bmatrix}}&+a(x)V(x)\left|g(x)\right|^{2}\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\\
&\geq\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\bigg(\left|f(x)\right|^{2}+\left|\nabla_{x}g(x)\right|^{2}+V(x)\left|g(x)\right|^{2}\bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\end{split}

for all
f
,
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Finally, we assume that the potential
V
belongs to the reverse Hölder class
\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
. This means that there is
C>0
such that

(1.6)

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V

for every ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where the left-hand side of (
1.6
) is understood as
\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B)}
when
q=\infty
.

There is a growing literature devoted to the study of boundary value problems for such perturbations of divergence form elliptic equations (and systems) with complex coefficients. One of the earliest results in this direction was obtained by Shen
[
54
]
, who used the method of layer potentials to prove well-posedness of the
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-Neumann problem for the Schrödinger equation
-\Delta u+Vu=0
above a Lipschitz graph for
p\in(1,2]
and potentials
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. This was improved by Tao–Wang
[
61
]
to include potentials
V\in\textup{RH}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Tao
[
60
]
has also considered the corresponding Regularity problem. More recently, Morris–Turner
[
51
]
proved well-posedness of the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-Regularity and Neumann problems for the Schrödinger equation (
1.3
) on the upper half-space
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
for potentials
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
when
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
n\geq 3
. The introduction of
[
51
]
contains a more detailed review of the literature concerning square integrable boundary data. For
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-data in a related context, we also highlight the recent series of papers by Bortz–Hofmann–Luna García–Mayboroda–Poggi
[
19
,
20
]
, which develops a theory of abstract layer potentials to prove well-posedness of the
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-Dirichlet, Regularity and Neumann problems on the upper half-space for equations with
t
-independent coefficients and lower-order terms in certain Lebesgue-critical spaces. Finally, for real, symmetric and
t
-independent coefficients in the region above a Lipschitz graph, we mention the recent work of Geng–Xu
[
34
]
proving unique solvability of the
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-Regularity and
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-Neumann problems for Schrödinger equations with potentials
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Solutions of (
1.3
) have to be understood in a weak sense, which we will now make precise. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{1+n}
be an arbitrary open set. Following
[
51
]
, we utilise the
V
-adapted Sobolev space
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
, defined as the space of functions
u\in\textup{W}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
such that
V^{1/2}u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega).

These spaces are studied in Section
2.3
. For
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
, we say that a function
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=g
in
\Omega
if

(1.7)

\iint_{\Omega}\left(A\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{t}u\\
\nabla_{x}u\end{bmatrix}\cdot\overline{\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{t}\varphi\\
\nabla_{x}\varphi\end{bmatrix}}+aVu\overline{\varphi}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\iint_{\Omega}g\overline{\varphi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

for all smooth compactly supported functions
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
.

In order to pose the relevant boundary value problems on
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
, we need to introduce the following maximal function, which measures the size of solutions on conical nontangential approach regions. For a function
F\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+};\mathbb{C}^{m})
, we consider the nontangential maximal function
N_{*}(F):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,\infty]
defined as

N_{*}(F)(x):=\sup_{t>0}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|F(s,y)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\right)^{1/2}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
W(t,x):=(\frac{t}{2},2t)\times B(x,t)
is a Whitney box (or cylinder) of scale
t>0
above the ball
B(x,t):=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\left|y-x\right|<t\right\}
. We also require the conical square function
S(F):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,\infty]
defined as

S(F)(x):=\left(\iint_{\Gamma_{1}(x)}\left|F(s,y)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}{s^{1+n}}\right)^{1/2}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\Gamma_{a}(x):=\left\{(s,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}:\left|x-y\right|<as\right\}
is a cone of aperture
a>0
with vertex at
x
.

Our main results require a systematic treatment and new characterisations for the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-based versions of the Hardy spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, for
p\in(0,\infty)
, introduced by Dziubański–Zienkiewicz
[
30
]
and adapted to the Schrödinger operator
H_{0}:=-\Delta+V
on the boundary
\partial\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This is the focus of Section
3
. In particular, we consider the pre-completed versions of these spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and in Section
3.1.2
we also prove that the completion of
\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
can be realised as a subspace of
\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. In Theorem
3.14
, we prove a new square function characterisation for
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in the full range
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},\infty)
. This enables us to obtain the interpolation result for these spaces in Lemma
3.20
, which is crucial for adapting the extrapolation framework in
[
11
]
.

We may now state the boundary value problems treated in this paper. For the Dirichlet problem, we consider boundary data
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
p\in(1,\infty)
, and
f\in A_{\perp\perp}^{-1}\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for the endpoint case
p=1
(see (
1.8
) below).
For
p\in[1,\infty)
, the Dirichlet problem
\left(\mathcal{D}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
is to find a function
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
such that

\left(\mathcal{D}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}\hskip 14.22636pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathscr{H}u=0\text{ weakly in }\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+},\\
N_{*}(u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\\
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)-f(x)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0\text{ for a.e. }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{array}\right.

The convergence of the solution
u
towards the boundary data
f
required here is a weak type of non-tangential convergence. Indeed, note that
\Gamma_{1}(x)\subseteq\cup_{t>0}W(t,x)\subseteq\Gamma_{2}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

For the Regularity problem, we distinguish the cases
p>1
and
p\leq 1
. For
p\in(1,\infty)
, we consider boundary data
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, meaning that
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is such that

\nabla_{\mu}f:=\begin{bmatrix}\nabla_{x}f\\
V^{1/2}f\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1}).

These homogeneous
V
-adapted Sobolev spaces are defined and studied in Section
2.3
. The density of smooth compactly supported functions
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is crucial for our results, and when
p=n
we find that it relies on the full strength of the improved Fefferman–Phong inequality obtained by Auscher–Ben Ali in
[
9
, Lemma 2.1]
(see Propositions
2.8
and
2.9
).

For
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},1]
, we consider boundary data
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which is a Hardy–Sobolev space defined as a completion of the normed space

\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\left\{f\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2}):\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}:=\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}<\infty\right\}.

The spaces
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
are studied in Section
3.2
, and we construct their completion
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as a subspace of
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in Section
3.2.2
, where
p^{*}
is the upper Sobolev exponent of
p
in dimension
n
(see Section
2.1.1
).
For
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},\infty)
, the Regularity problem
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
is to find a function
u\in\mathcal{V}_{\textup{loc}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
such that

\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}\hskip 14.22636pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathscr{H}u=0\text{ weakly in }\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+},\\
N_{*}(\nabla_{\mu}u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\\
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)-f(x)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0\text{ for a.e. }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{array}\right.

We also study the corresponding Neumann problem
\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
for
p\in[1,\infty)
. Given boundary data
g\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
if
p>1
, or
g\in\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
if
p=1
, the Neumann problem
\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
is to find a function
u\in\mathcal{V}_{\textup{loc}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
such that

\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}\hskip 14.22636pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathscr{H}u=0\text{ weakly in }\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+},\\
N_{*}(\nabla_{\mu}u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\\
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|(A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u)(s,y)-g(x)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0\text{ for a.e. }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.\end{array}\right.

The boundary value problems
\left(\mathcal{D}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
,
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
, and
\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
are called

solvable
if for each boundary datum there exists at least one solution
u
with the respective
properties specified above. In the case
V\not\equiv 0
, they are called
uniquely posed
if for each boundary datum there exists at most one such solution
u
in
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
. In the case
V\equiv 0
, it is instead required that there exists at most one such solution
u
in
W^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
modulo constants. The boundary
value problems are called
well-posed
if they are solvable and uniquely posed.

We also note that
[
51
, Theorem 1.1]
implies that the Regularity problem
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{2}
is well-posed if
V\not\equiv 0
is such that
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
q\geq\max\left\{\frac{n}{2},2\right\}
and
n\geq 3
. While the type of convergence of the solution towards the boundary data obtained in the aforementioned reference is
a priori
different to that considered here, it can be shown to imply the convergence required in
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{2}
. We provide details at the start of Section
9.2
.

Before stating the main results of the paper, we shall give an overview of the method that we will employ to solve the boundary value problems
\left(\mathcal{D}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
,
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
and
\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
, adapting the machinery of Auscher–Egert
[
11
]
to treat the Schrödinger equation.

The first step is the following reduction.
Taking
g=0
in the ellipticity assumption (
1.5
), we see that
\operatorname{Re}{{A_{\perp\perp}(x)}}\geq\lambda>0
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As a consequence,
b(x):=A_{\perp\perp}^{-1}(x)
exists with

(1.8)

\left|b(x)\right|\leq\lambda^{-1}\text{ and }\operatorname{Re}{{b(x)}}\geq\lambda\Lambda^{-2}

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The
t
-independence of
A_{\perp\perp}
shows that the equation (
1.3
) can be written in the form
\partial_{t}^{2}u=Hu
, where
H
is the differential operator formally defined as

H:=-b\textup{div}_{x}\left({A_{\parallel\parallel}\nabla_{x}}\right)+baV

on the boundary
\partial\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}\cong\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This suggests that solutions
u
to the boundary value problems can be constructed, at least when
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, using the holomorphic functional calculus for the operator
H
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to define the Poisson semigroup solution
u(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
for
t>0
. This will be made precise in Theorem
7.2
.

Some operator theoretic properties of the operator
H
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
are needed to support this strategy. In the case
V\equiv 0
, the solution of the Kato square root conjecture is essential for solving the Regularity problem for boundary data in
\dot{\textup{W}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Those results were extended recently to Schrödinger operators by Morris–Turner in
[
51
]
. They used the first-order approach, originally developed for boundary value problems by Auscher–Axelsson–McIntosh in
[
8
]
, to prove quadratic estimates for so-called
DB
-type operators (see
[
51
, Theorem 3.2]
) and applied those to obtain well-posedness results for
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-Regularity and Neumann boundary value problems for the Schrödinger equation (
1.2
) on the upper half-space
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
when
n\geq 3
.
Importantly for our purposes, these quadratic estimates imply that
H
has a bounded holomorphic functional calculus on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with square root domain
\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})=\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and estimate

(1.9)

\left\lVert H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\eqsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

for all
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, when
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
(see
[
51
, Corollary 3.21]
).

Four critical numbers
p_{\pm}(H)
and
q_{\pm}(H)
govern the
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-extrapolation of solvability for (
1.3
).
Analogous to
[
11
, Section 4]
, we define
(p_{-}(H),p_{+}(H))
and
(q_{-}(H),q_{+}(H))
to be the largest open intervals of exponents
p\in\left(\frac{n}{n+1},\infty\right)
for which the respective family

\left\{A_{\perp\perp}(1+t^{2}H)^{-1}A_{\perp\perp}^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-1}A_{\perp\perp}^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}

is uniformly bounded on
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(recall that
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
when
p>1
).
These intervals are studied in Section
4
. We note that they both contain
p=2
, whilst estimates for the endpoints in some prototypical cases are obtained in Remark
4.6
. It is also important to note that the use of the letter
q
in both
q_{\pm}(H)
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is an unfortunate consequence of the literature, which we keep for consistency.

One of the main tools used in
[
11
]
to establish the non-tangential maximal function and conical square function
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounds for Poisson semigroup solutions is a class of
H
-adapted Hardy spaces
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
defined for
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in(0,\infty)
. The introduction of
[
28
]
provides a useful overview of the history of such operator-adapted spaces.

An essential step in the extrapolation theory for
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-type solvability is then the identification of the
H
-adapted Hardy spaces
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\mathbb{H}^{0,p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with “concrete” function spaces for boundary data, such as
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
when
p>1
. To identify these spaces when
p\leq 1
, we introduce a new class of molecules for
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
which have supports concentrated on cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of arbitrary size. This is in contrast to the
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-molecules considered previously, such as in
[
21
, Section 3.2]
, which typically restrict the size of such cubes according to the critical radius function in (
2.3
). Instead, we impose a cancellation condition determined only by the scaling in the Fefferman–Phong inequality (see Definition
3.11
and Proposition
2.8
). This substitute is vital to the proof of Lemma
6.7
. An important ingredient for these identifications when
p>1
is the
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-boundedness of the
Riesz transform

R_{H}:=\nabla_{\mu}H^{-1/2}
. The square-root estimate (
1.9
) shows that this operator has a bounded extension mapping from
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
into
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})
. The
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-extrapolation of these bounds is investigated in Section
5
. Our main result in this direction is the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

If
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then the Riesz transform
R_{H}
extends to a bounded operator on
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
p\in\left(p_{-}(H),q_{+}(H)\right)\cap(1,2q]
.

This result is a significant extension of
[
11
, Theorem 7.3]
.
The
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounds for
R_{H}
in the range
p\in(1,2]
follow from a modification of the arguments used therein to treat the case
V\equiv 0
.
The
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounds in the range
p\in(2,\infty)
are more subtle, however, because difficulties arise from the absence of the conservation property
(1+t^{2}H)^{-1}1=1
, which is exploited when
V\equiv 0
(see
[
11
, Corollary 5.4]
) but fails when
V\not\equiv 0
. These difficulties materialise as an additional term that we estimate by combining the local Fefferman–Phong inequality (see Proposition
2.8
), with a novel “cancellation” bound (
5.12
) that controls averages of
t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(1)
on balls of radius
t
for sufficiently large integers
k\geq 1
. This is achieved by utilising an extension of the aforementioned conservation property that holds even when
V\not\equiv 0
(see Lemma
5.2
) as well as certain off-diagonal estimates for
\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}\right\}_{t>0}
(see Lemma
5.5
.(ii)). The later estimates don’t appear to have a precedent in the literature. Altogether, this allows us to prove Theorem
1.1
in Section
5.2
.

Once Theorem
1.1
is at hand, we can proceed to identify the
H
-adapted Hardy spaces
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in a range of exponents
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},\infty)
determined by the critical numbers
p_{\pm}(H)
and
q_{\pm}(H)
. More specifically, if
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
q>\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
\delta:=2-\frac{n}{q}>0
, then we obtain the following identifications with equivalent
p
-(quasi)norms in Theorem
6.1
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),&\textup{ if }p\in(p_{-}(H)\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}\,,\,p_{+}(H));\\
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),&\textup{ if }p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*},q_{+}(H))\cap(1,2q];\\
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),&\textup{ if }p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}\,,1]\cap\mathcal{I}(V).\end{array}

Note that
p_{-}(H)_{*}
is the lower Sobolev exponent of
p_{-}(H)
in dimension
n
(see Section
2.1.1
).
We recall that the subscript “pre” in the notation
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is used to indicate that these are pre-completed spaces, which are subspaces of
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, respectively. Moreover, we note that
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with equivalent
p
-norms when
p>1
(see Lemma
3.3
).

These identifications provide an analogue of
[
11
, Theorem 9.7]
. A significant difference is the presence of two new endpoints in the intervals of identification, namely
\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}=(1+\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{2q})^{-1}
and
2q
, which depend only on the reverse Hölder property of
V
through the exponent
q
and dimension
n
. Another distinction is the introduction of an additional subset of exponents
\mathcal{I}(V)
, which is used to obtain an identification of
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
when
p\leq 1
. An exponent
p\in\left(\frac{n}{n+1},1\right]
is defined to belong to
\mathcal{I}(V)
when

(1.10)

\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\gtrsim\left\lVert(-\Delta+V)^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}

for all
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the property that each of the
n+1
components of
\nabla_{\mu}f
is in
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. This set allows us to circumvent the need to establish atomic decompositions of
\nabla_{\mu}f
with atoms belonging to the range of
\nabla_{\mu}
. Instead, we establish Theorem
3.18
as a substitute, after having defined
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
accordingly using the fractional power
H_{0}^{1/2}
in place of
\nabla_{\mu}
, when
p\leq 1
. These reverse Riesz transform bounds on
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
are studied in Section
4.2
, where a non-trivial class of potentials
V
satisfying (
1.10
) is exhibited (see Theorem
4.10
and Section
4.2.3
).
It is important to note, however, that the set
\mathcal{I}(V)
is not required for the
well-posedness
of the Regularity problem in Theorem
1.3
below. This is because the only relevant embedding for the associated existence result is the continuous inclusion
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subseteq\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which is shown to hold when
{p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+1},1]}
in Lemma
6.10
.

Once Theorem
6.1
is established, we obtain estimates for Poisson semigroup solutions when the boundary data is in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\textup{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, for each relevant boundary data space
\textup{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, in Section
8
. In contrast to
[
11
, Sections 17.1 and 17.2]
, significant difficulties arise when proving the reverse nontangential maximal function estimates when
p\leq 1
and these ultimately restrict our consideration to potentials
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in that case
(see Propositions
8.4
and
8.9
). This is because the presence of the potential
V
implies that the maximal operator used in the definition of the Hardy space
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
may no longer be associated with convolution operators and must instead be treated using the functional calculus of
H_{0}=-\Delta+V
. We circumvent this challenge by combining the finite propagation speed property of
H_{0}
(in the sense
[
44
, Definition 3.3]
) with pointwise bounds on its fundamental solution (see Lemma
8.3
).

We then extend the estimates of Section
8
by density, utilising the approach from
[
11
, Section 17.3]
, to prove solvability of
\left(\mathcal{D}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
and
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
for general boundary data in Section
9
. To prove uniqueness for the Dirichlet problems, we follow the method from Chapters 20 and 21 of
[
11
]
, which originates in
[
10
]
. This is the content of Section
10
. Finally, we treat the Neumann problem in Section
11
.

Our main result for the Dirichlet problem is the following theorem.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Suppose that
p\geq 1
and

p_{-}(H)<p<p_{+}(H)^{*}.

If
p>1
, then

\left(\mathcal{D}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
is well-posed and the solution
u
for data
f
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies

\left\lVert N_{*}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert A_{\perp\perp}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

If
p=1
, then
\left(\mathcal{D}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
is well-posed and the solution
u
for data
f
in
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies

\left\lVert N_{*}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}\lesssim\left\lVert A_{\perp\perp}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}.

Moreover, the reverse nontangential maximal function bound holds when
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

We obtain the existence and further properties of solutions to the Dirichlet problem, including non-tangential convergence of
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-averages to the boundary data, and uniform
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-estimates, in Theorem
9.1
below. The uniqueness is contained in Theorem
10.1
.

Our main result for the Regularity problem is the following theorem.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Suppose that

p_{-}(H)_{*}<p<q_{+}(H)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{n}{n+1}<p\leq 2q.

If
p>1
, then
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
is well-posed and the solution
u
for data
f
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies

\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert S(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

If
p\leq 1
, then
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
is well-posed and the solution
u
for data
f
in
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies

\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lVert S(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}.

Moreover, the reverse square function bound holds when
{p\in\left(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2},1\right]\cap\mathcal{I}(V)}
, whilst the reverse nontangential maximal function bound holds when
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

We obtain the existence and further properties of solutions to the Regularity problem in Theorems
9.2
and
9.3
below. The uniqueness is contained in Theorem
10.2
.

Our main result for the Neumann problem is the following theorem.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Suppose that

p_{-}(H)<p<q_{+}(H)\quad\text{and}\quad 1\leq p\leq 2q.

If
p>1
, then
\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
is well-posed and the solution
u
for data
g
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies

\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert S(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

If
p=1\in\mathcal{I}(V)
, then
\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
admits a solution
u
for data
g
in
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfying

\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}\lesssim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert S(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}.

Moreover, the reverse nontangential maximal function bound holds when
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

We deduce these results for the Neumann problem as an application of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
in Section
11
below. Our method provides a new self-contained proof even in the case
V\equiv 0
treated by Auscher–Egert
[
11
]
, since it no longer relies on the results in
[
16
]
and
[
17
]
.
The open question of uniqueness for
\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
is discussed in Remark
11.2
after the proof of Theorem
1.4
.

We stress that throughout the paper
V
will always denote a
non-negative
locally integrable function. Theorems
1.1
,
1.2
,
1.3
and
1.4
are proved in
[
11
]
in the case
V\equiv 0
. This means that we can restrict our attention to the case
V\not\equiv 0
, or equivalently
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V\in(0,\infty]
,
which often allows us to avoid working modulo constants.

The paper is organised as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce preliminary material that will be used throughout the paper. Section
2.2
collects some important properties of reverse Hölder potentials
V
, whilst Sobolev spaces adapted to singular potentials
V
are studied in Section
2.3
. The rest of Section
2
serves as a brief overview of the holomorphic functional calculus for the (bi)sectorial operators that we will consider, along with the construction of the corresponding adapted Hardy spaces. In Section
3
, we introduce the Hardy and Hardy–Sobolev spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which are adapted to the Schrödinger operator
H_{0}:=-\Delta+V
. Topics covered include atomic decompositions (Sections
3.1.4
and
3.2.3
), interpolation, and duality (Section
3.3
). Section
4
is concerned with properties of the critical numbers
p_{\pm}(H)
and
q_{\pm}(H)
, as well as presenting a class of potentials
V
for which the set
\mathcal{I}(V)
is non-empty. Theorem
1.1
is proved in Section
5
, and the identifications of
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
are established in Section
6
. Some elementary properties and interior estimates for weak solutions of
\mathscr{H}u=0
are collected in Section
7
, before proving the required estimates for Poisson semigroup solutions in Section
8
. Finally, Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
follow from Section
9
(existence and estimates) and Section
10
(uniqueness), and Theorem
1.4
is proved in Section
11
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this first section, we introduce the notation that we will be using, the definitions and properties of the function spaces that we need, as well as some operator-theoretic objects needed to carry out the strategy outlined in the introduction.

2.1.
Notation and function spaces

In this section, we set out the notation that we will use throughout the text. We also recall the definitions of some well-known function spaces.

2.1.1.
Exponents and order relations

For
p\in\left[1,\infty\right]
, we denote the Hölder conjugate
p^{\prime}\in\left[1,\infty\right]
such that
\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{p}=1
, with the convention that
\frac{1}{\infty}=0
. For
p_{0},p_{1}\in(0,\infty]
and an interpolating index
\theta\in[0,1]
, we define
[p_{0},p_{1}]_{\theta}\in(0,\infty]
by the relation
\frac{1}{[p_{0},p_{1}]_{\theta}}:=\frac{1-\theta}{p_{0}}+\frac{\theta}{p_{1}}
. For
p\in\left(0,\infty\right)
, we let

p^{*}\in\left(0,\infty\right]
be the Sobolev exponent for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined as
p^{*}:=\infty
if
p\geq n
, and
p^{*}:=\frac{np}{n-p}
if
p<n
. For
p\in\left(0,\infty\right]
, we let
p_{*}\in\left(0,\infty\right)
be the lower Sobolev exponent defined as
p_{*}:=\frac{np}{n+p}
.

We shall also be using the notation
a\vee b:=\max\left\{a,b\right\}
and
a\wedge b:=\min\left\{a,b\right\}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
. We say that
x\lesssim y
if there exists
C>0
, independent of
x
and
y
, such that
x\leq Cy
. We define
x\gtrsim y
in a similar way, and use the notation
x\eqsim y
to express that both
x\lesssim y
and
x\gtrsim y
hold.

2.1.2.
Geometry in
\mathbb{R}^{n}

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we let
\left|x\right|:=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|x_{i}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}
denote the Euclidean norm of
x
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r>0
, we let

B(x,r):=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\left|y-x\right|<r\right\}

be the ball centred at
x
with radius
r
. The distance between two subsets
E,F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
\textup{dist}(E,F):=\inf\left\{\left|x-y\right|:x\in E,y\in F\right\}
. For cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
or balls
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and any real
r>0
, the set
rQ
(or
rB
) denotes the cube (or the ball) with same centre as
Q
, and with sidelength (radius) equal to
r
times the sidelength of
Q
(or the radius of
B
). For a given cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we let
\ell(Q)
denote its sidelength. For cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
(or balls
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
), and integers
j\geq 1
, consider the annular regions
C_{j}(Q)
(or
C_{j}(B)
), defined as
C_{1}(Q):=4Q
and
C_{j}(Q):=2^{j+1}Q\setminus 2^{j}Q
for all
j\geq 2
. A collection
\left\{A_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathcal{I}}
of subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is said to have bounded overlap if
\sum_{k\in\mathcal{I}}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{k}}(x)\lesssim 1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
(we let
\mathbbm{1}_{A}
denote the indicator function of a set
A
).

2.1.3.
Operators and quasinormed spaces

Given quasinormed spaces
X
and
Y
, we let
\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
denote the space of all bounded linear operators
T:X\to Y
equipped with the usual operator quasinorm
\left\lVert T\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(X,Y)}
. The domain, range and null-space of an operator
T
are denoted respectively as
\mathsf{D}(T)
,
\mathsf{R}(T)
and
\mathsf{N}(T)
. We also consider the intersection
X\cap Y
with the quasinorm
\|z\|_{X\cap Y}:=\max\left\{\|z\|_{X},\|z\|_{Y}\right\}
for all
z\in X\cap Y
.

2.1.4.
Lebesgue spaces and distribution spaces

We shall occasionally use the notation
\mathrm{L}^{p}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for the space of compactly supported functions in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{C})
. If the Lebesgue measure of
A
, denoted
\left\lvert A\right\rvert
, is finite, we let
\textup{av}_{A}f:=\left(f\right)_{A}:=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{A}f:=\frac{1}{\left\lvert A\right\rvert}\int_{A}f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
denote the average of a measurable function
f
over the set
A
.

For an open subset
O\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n},
we let
C^{\infty}_{c}(O)
denote the space of smooth functions with compact support in
O
. The space of Schwartz functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is denoted by
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Both spaces are equipped with the usual Fréchet topology, and their topological duals are the distribution spaces denoted by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, respectively.

For
1\leq p\leq\infty
, the Sobolev space
\textup{W}^{1,p}(O)
is the space of all functions
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(O)
with distributional gradient
\nabla f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(O;\mathbb{C}^{n})
, equipped with the usual norm

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{W}^{1,p}(O)}:=\left(\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{p}_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(O)}+\left\lVert\nabla f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(O)}^{p}\right)^{1/p}.

The corresponding local Sobolev spaces are denoted by
\textup{W}_{\textup{loc}}^{1,p}(O)
. The homogeneous Sobolev space
\dot{\textup{W}}^{1,p}(O)
is the space of all distributions
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(O)
(or equivalently functions
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(O)
) such that
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(O;\mathbb{C}^{n})
, equipped with the seminorm
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{W}}^{1,p}}:=\left\lVert\nabla f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(O)}
.

2.1.5.
Tent spaces
\textup{T}^{s,p}

These function spaces have been introduced by Coifman–Meyer–Stein
[
23
]
. We refer the reader to
[
1
]
and
[
2
]
for detailed proofs. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we introduce the cone of unit aperture with vertex at
x
,
\Gamma(x):=\left\{(t,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}:\left|x-y\right|<t\right\}
.

For a measurable function
F:\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
, we define the corresponding standard conical square function
S(F):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,\infty]
by

S(F)(x):=\left(\iint_{\Gamma(x)}\left|F(t,y)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}{t^{1+n}}\right)^{1/2}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in(0,\infty)
, the tent space
\textup{T}^{s,p}
consists of all functions
F\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
such that
\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{s,p}}:=\left\lVert S\left(t^{-s}F\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
.
The maps
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{s,p}}
are quasinorms (norms when
p\geq 1
), and all tent spaces are quasi Banach spaces, or Banach spaces when
p\geq 1
(see
[
1
, Proposition 3.5]
). Their topology is finer than that of
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
.
When
s=0
, we simply write
\textup{T}^{0,p}=:\textup{T}^{p}
. The space
\textup{T}^{p}
coincides with the space
\textup{T}^{p}_{2}
introduced in
[
23
]
.

It follows from Fubini’s theorem that
\textup{T}^{2}
and
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+};\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t})
are equal as sets, with equivalent norms. When
1<p<\infty
, the
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+};\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t})
inner-product

\langle F,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+};\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t})}:=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}}F(t,x)\overline{G(t,x)}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}

identifies the dual of
\textup{T}^{s,p}
with
\textup{T}^{-s,p^{\prime}}
, see
[
2
, Proposition 1.9]
. In particular, there is a
C>0
such that

(2.1)

\left|\langle F,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+};\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t})}\right|\leq C\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{s,p}}\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{-s,p^{\prime}}}

for all
F,G\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+},\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t})
.

When
s=0
and
0<p\leq 1
, the tent spaces
\textup{T}^{p}
admit atomic decompositions. A
\textup{T}^{p}
-atom associated with a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a measurable function
A:\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
with support in
Q\times(0,\ell(Q))
such that

\left(\int_{0}^{\ell(Q)}\int_{Q}\left|A(s,y)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)^{1/2}\leq\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}
.
The atomic decomposition
[
23
, Proposition 5]
says that there is
C\geq 0
(depending only on
n
and
p
) such that every
F\in\textup{T}^{p}
can be written as
F=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}A_{i}
with unconditional convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, where each
A_{i}
is a
\textup{T}^{p}
-atom and
\left\lVert(\lambda_{i})_{i}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}}\leq C\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}
. Moreover, if
F\in\textup{T}^{p}\cap\textup{T}^{2}
, then the atomic decomposition also converges in
\textup{T}^{2}
and thus in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+},\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t})
. The unconditional convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
is not explicitly stated in
[
23
, Proposition 5]
, but follows from the construction. Indeed, the decomposition has the form
F=\sum_{i\geq 0}F\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}}
, where
\left\{\Delta_{i}\right\}_{i\geq 0}
is a family of disjoint measurable subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
such that
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\Delta_{i}
, see equation (4.5) in
[
23
]
. This also justifies convergence of the atomic decomposition of
F\in\textup{T}^{p}
in the
\textup{T}^{p}
norm.

For
p\in(0,\infty)
, we shall occasionally need the tent space
\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
defined as the set of functions
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
with finite (quasi)norm
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}}:=\left\lVert N_{*}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}<\infty
. Again, these are (quasi)Banach spaces whose topologies are finer than that of
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
.

2.1.6.
Fefferman–Stein Hardy spaces
\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

For
p\in(0,1]
, we let
\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
denote the real Hardy space of Fefferman–Stein (see, e.g.,
[
59
, Chapter III]
). It is useful to think of these spaces in terms of atomic decompositions. We restrict ourselves to the range
\frac{n}{n+1}<p\leq 1
. An
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-atom for
\textup{H}^{p}
is a measurable function
a:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
supported in a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, such that
\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{-1/p}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a=0
.
The atomic decomposition of the space
\textup{H}^{p}
(see
[
59
, Section III.3.2]
) states that every
f\in\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
can be decomposed as
f=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}a_{k}
,
where the sum converges absolutely in
\textup{H}^{p}
, the
a_{k}
are
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-atoms for
\textup{H}^{p}
, and the scalars
\left\{\lambda_{k}\right\}_{k\geq 1}
satisfy

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left|\lambda_{k}\right|^{p}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{p}_{\textup{H}^{p}}
. It is useful to note that if
f\in\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then the decomposition can be taken to also converge in the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
norm (see
[
11
, Section 2.4]
and the references therein).

2.2.
Reverse Hölder weights

For
n\geq 1
and
q\in(1,\infty)
, the reverse Hölder class
\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the vector space consisting of locally integrable functions
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\left[0,\infty\right)
for which there exists
C\geq 0
such that

(2.2)

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V^{q}\right)^{1/q}\leq C\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V

for every ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The infimum of all such
C\geq 0
will be denoted by
\llbracket V\rrbracket_{q}
. The reverse Hölder class
\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined by replacing the left-hand side of (
2.2
) with
\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B)}
. Note that replacing balls with cubes, or indeed any family of sets with bounded eccentricity, gives the same class
\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by item (ii) of Lemma
2.1
below.
It follows from Hölder’s inequality that
\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subseteq\textup{RH}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
1<p\leq q\leq\infty
. As examples of reverse Hölder weights, we mention that
x\mapsto\left|x\right|^{-\alpha}
belongs to
\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\alpha>0
such that
1<q<\frac{n}{\alpha}
, and that for any polynomial
P:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
and
a>0
, it holds that
\left|P\right|^{a}\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(see Proposition
2.3
).
The self-improvement, doubling and two other well-known properties of reverse Hölder weights are collected below.

Lemma 2.1
.

If
n\geq 1
,
q>1
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then the following properties hold:

(i)

There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
V\in\textup{RH}^{q+\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(ii)

There exists
\kappa>0
such that for all balls
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
it holds that

\int_{2B}V\leq\kappa\int_{B}V
.

(iii)

For each
s\in(0,1)
it holds that
V^{s}\in\textup{RH}^{\frac{1}{s}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(iv)

There exists
C>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
0<r<R
it holds that

\frac{1}{r^{n-2}}\int_{B(x,r)}V\leq C\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)^{\frac{n}{q}-2}\frac{1}{R^{n-2}}\int_{B(x,R)}V.

Proof.

See 6.6(a) in Section 6 of
[
59
, Chapter V]
for (i), Section 1 of the same reference for (ii),
[
9
, Proposition 11.1]
for (iii), and
[
55
, Lemma 1.2]
for (iv).
∎

The following singularity property for reverse Hölder weights
V
that are not equal to
0
almost everywhere, henceforth denoted
V\not\equiv 0
, is essential in the proof of Proposition
2.7
below.

Lemma 2.2
.

If
n\geq 1
,
q>1
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
V\not\equiv 0
and
0<p<q
, then
\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}=\infty
.

Proof.

Since
V\not\equiv 0
, there are
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r_{0}>0
such that
\int_{B(x_{0},r_{0})}V>0
. Therefore, for
R>r_{0}
, item (iv) from Lemma
2.1
gives a
c>0
such that

\frac{1}{R^{n-2}}\int_{B(x_{0},R)}V\geq c^{-1}\left(\frac{r_{0}}{R}\right)^{\frac{n}{q}-2}\frac{1}{r_{0}^{n-2}}\int_{B(x_{0},r_{0})}V=c_{0}R^{2-\frac{n}{q}},

where
c_{0}:=c^{-1}r_{0}^{\frac{n}{q}-n}\displaystyle\int_{B(x_{0},r_{0})}V
. If
p\geq 1
, then we obtain

c_{0}R^{-\frac{n}{q}}\leq\frac{1}{R^{n}}\int_{B(x_{0},R)}V\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x_{0},R)}V^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

If
p\in(0,1)
, then item (iii) of Lemma
2.1
shows that
V^{p}\in\textup{RH}^{1/p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, hence

c_{0}R^{-\frac{n}{q}}\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x_{0},R)}V\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x_{0},R)}V^{p}\right)^{1/p}.

In either case, this proves that there are
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
C>0
and
r_{0}>0
such that

R^{n\left(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\right)}\leq C\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}\left(B(x_{0},R)\right)}

for all
R>r_{0}
. The conclusion follows by letting
R\to\infty
.
∎

Now suppose that
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\frac{n}{2}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Shen
[
55
]
introduced the
critical radius function

{\rho(\cdot,V):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,\infty]}
defined by

(2.3)

\rho(x,V):=\sup\left\{r>0:r^{2}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}V(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\leq 1\right\}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. It follows from items (i) and (iv) of Lemma
2.1
that
\rho(\cdot,V)
is well-defined since the set defining it is not empty. Moreover, we have
0<\rho(x,V)<\infty
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
when
V\not\equiv 0
. It is customary to denote
m(x,V):=\rho(x,V)^{-1}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. When it is clear from the context, we shall abbreviate
\rho(x):=\rho(x,V)
, and similarly for
m(x,V)
. We shall often use the elementary properties of
\rho(\cdot,V)
listed in
[
55
, Lemma 1.4]
. Important for us is the “slowly-varying” property, whereby for every
C>0
, whenever
|y-x|\leq C\rho(x)
it holds that

(2.4)

\rho(x)\eqsim\rho(y),

where the implicit constant depends on
C
.
Also, applying item (iv) of Lemma
2.1
with
R=\rho(x)
and setting
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}
shows that

(2.5)

r^{2}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x,r)}V\lesssim\left(\frac{r}{\rho(x)}\right)^{\delta}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r\in(0,\rho(x)]
.

The following result is stated without proof in
[
56
, Estimate (0.8)]
, and in
[
55
, Section 0]
for non-negative polynomials
P
and
p=1
. We provide a proof for completeness.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
p\in(0,\infty)
. If
P:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
is a polynomial of degree
d\geq 0
, then
\left|P\right|^{p}\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

(2.6)

m(x_{0},\left|P\right|^{p})\eqsim\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|\partial^{\alpha}P(x_{0})\right|^{\frac{p}{\left|\alpha\right|p+2}}

for all
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where the implicit constants only depend on
d
,
n
and
p
.

Proof.

Let
d\geq 0
be a fixed integer, and let
X
be the finite dimensional complex vector space of all polynomials
Q:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
of degree at most
d
. Observe that the mappings

Q\mapsto\begin{cases}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(0,1)}\left|Q\right|^{p},&\text{ if }p\in(0,1);\\
\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(0,1)}\left|Q\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p},&\text{ if }p\in[1,\infty),\end{cases}

and

Q:=\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}a_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\mapsto\begin{cases}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|a_{\alpha}\right|^{p},&\text{ if }p\in(0,1);\\
\left(\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|a_{\alpha}\right|\right)^{1/p},&\text{ if }p\in[1,\infty),\end{cases}

both define norms on
X
, and are therefore equivalent. Combining this with a change of variable, this implies that there exists
C\geq 1
(depending only on
d
,
n
and
p
) such that for any polynomial
Q(x):=\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}a_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
it holds that

(2.7)

C^{-1}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|a_{\alpha}\right|^{p}r^{\left|\alpha\right|p}\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(0,r)}\left|Q\right|^{p}\leq C\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|a_{\alpha}\right|^{p}r^{\left|\alpha\right|p}

for all
r>0
, and therefore
\left\lVert\left|Q\right|^{p}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B(0,r))}\lesssim\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|a_{\alpha}\right|^{p}r^{\left|\alpha\right|p}\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(0,r)}\left|Q\right|^{p}
.

Now, given
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a polynomial
P:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
of degree
d
, we note that
Q(x):=P(x_{0}+x)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, defines a polynomial of degree
d
, and therefore

\left\lVert\left|P\right|^{p}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B(x_{0},r))}=\left\lVert\left|Q\right|^{p}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B(0,r))}\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(0,r)}\left|Q\right|^{p}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x_{0},r)}\left|P\right|^{p},

which means that
\left|P\right|^{p}\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

For a polynomial
Q(x)=\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}a_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
, we let
M_{p}(x_{0},Q):=\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|\partial^{\alpha}Q(x_{0})\right|^{\frac{p}{\left|\alpha\right|p+2}}
for all
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and note that

(2.8)

M_{p}(0,Q)=\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|\partial^{\alpha}Q(0)\right|^{\frac{p}{\left|\alpha\right|p+2}}=\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}(\alpha!\left|a_{\alpha}\right|)^{\frac{p}{\left|\alpha\right|p+2}}\eqsim\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|a_{\alpha}\right|^{\frac{p}{\left|\alpha\right|p+2}}.

Moreover, observe that the definition (
2.3
) and the two-sided estimate (
2.7
) show that

\rho(0,\left|Q\right|^{p})=\sup\left\{r>0:\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|a_{\alpha}\right|^{p}r^{\left|\alpha\right|p+2}\leq C\right\},

and therefore
\rho(0,\left|Q\right|^{p})\eqsim\min_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|a_{\alpha}\right|^{-\frac{p}{\left|\alpha\right|p+2}}
(with the understanding that
0^{-\frac{p}{\left|\alpha\right|p+2}}=\infty
). Combined with (
2.8
), this shows that
m(0,\left|Q\right|^{p})\eqsim\max_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq d}\left|a_{\alpha}\right|^{\frac{p}{\left|\alpha\right|p+2}}\eqsim M_{p}(0,Q)
. As above, for any given
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, applying this with
Q(x):=P(x_{0}+x)
proves (
2.6
).
∎

2.3.
Sobolev spaces adapted to singular potentials

We consider a class of function spaces obtained by adapting the usual Sobolev spaces
\textup{W}^{1,p}\left(\Omega\right)
for
p\in\left[1,\infty\right)
and
n\geq 1
to account for non-negative potentials
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

on open sets
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
has a weak (distributional) derivative
\nabla_{x}f=\left(\partial_{1}f,\ldots,\partial_{n}f\right)\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{n})
, then
\nabla_{\mu}f:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
is the measurable function defined for all
x\in\Omega
by

\nabla_{\mu}f(x)=\begin{bmatrix}\nabla_{x}f(x)\\
V(x)^{1/2}f(x)\end{bmatrix}.

The adapted Sobolev space
\mathcal{V}^{1,p}\left(\Omega\right)
is defined to be the set

(2.9)

\mathcal{V}^{1,p}\left(\Omega\right):=\left\{f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega):\nabla_{\mu}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}\left(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\right)\right\}

with the norm

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{V}^{1,p}(\Omega)}:=(\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega)}^{p}+\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\Omega,\mathbb{C}^{n+1})}^{p})^{1/p}

for all
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,p}(\Omega)
.
It follows from
[
51
, Lemma 2.1]
that
\mathcal{V}^{1,p}(\Omega)
is a Banach space and that
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\Omega)
is a Hilbert space with the inner-product

\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\Omega)}:=\int_{\Omega}(f\overline{g}+\nabla_{\mu}f\cdot\overline{\nabla_{\mu}g})

for all
f,g\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\Omega)
. We shall also consider the local variants
\mathcal{V}^{1,p}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
defined as the space of functions
f\in\textup{W}^{1,p}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
for which
V^{1/2}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
equipped with the natural locally convex topology. We also define the homogeneous adapted Sobolev space
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as the set

(2.10)

\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\left\{f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\nabla_{\mu}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\right)\right\}

equipped with the norm

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}:=\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})}

for all
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
when
V\not\equiv 0
.

Remark 2.4
.

We note that when
n\geq 3
, the space
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
given by (
2.10
) may be larger than that given by (2.5) in
[
51
]
, as here we require
f
to be in
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
instead of
\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Proposition
2.7
below shows that these definitions coincide when
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
q\geq\frac{n}{2}
and
n\geq 3
, which is the only case when we will need to use these spaces. The definition of
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
given by (
2.10
) is chosen here because it is more suited to our purposes when
p\neq 2
.

The following properties of
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
are important considerations given the remark above.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be non-negative with
V\not\equiv 0
. If
p\in[1,\infty)
, then
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a Banach space. In addition,
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a Hilbert space with the inner-product

\langle f,g\rangle_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}:=\langle\nabla_{\mu}f,\nabla_{\mu}g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})}

for all
f,g\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove completeness with respect to the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.
We begin with a general observation. Let
Q_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an arbitrary cube. Then, if
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
has a weak gradient
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})
, the Poincaré inequality lets us estimate, for all larger cubes
Q\supseteq Q_{0}
,

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\left|f-(f)_{Q_{0}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}{\left\lvert Q_{0}\right\rvert}\right)\int_{Q}\left|f-(f)_{Q}\right|\leq c_{n}\left(1+\frac{\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}{\left\lvert Q_{0}\right\rvert}\right)l(Q)\int_{Q}\left|\nabla_{x}f\right|

\displaystyle\leq c_{n}\left(1+\frac{\left\lvert Q\right\rvert}{\left\lvert Q_{0}\right\rvert}\right)l(Q)\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left\lVert\nabla_{x}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

We can now proceed with the proof. Let
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\left(\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}\right)
. Since
(\nabla_{x}f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
forms a Cauchy sequence in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
, the observation above implies that the sequence
(g_{k})_{k\geq 1}:=\left(f_{k}-\left(f_{k}\right)_{Q_{0}}\right)_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
g\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
g_{k}\to g
in
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
k\to\infty
. Since the sequence
(\nabla_{x}f_{k})_{k\geq 1}=(\nabla_{x}g_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
, there is
F\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})
such that
\nabla_{x}f_{k}=\nabla_{x}g_{k}\to F

in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
as
k\to\infty
. A standard argument (see, e.g.,
[
51
, Lemma 2.1]
) now implies that
g
has a weak gradient in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
, given by
\nabla_{x}g=F
. Moreover, since the sequence
\left(V^{1/2}f_{k}\right)_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
, there is a function
G\in\mathrm{L}^{p}
such that
V^{1/2}f_{k}\to G
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
, as
k\to\infty
. In particular, there is a subsequence
(k_{l})_{l\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{N}
such that
V(x)^{1/2}f_{k_{l}}(x)\to G(x)
as
l\to\infty
, for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, we can extract a further subsequence
(k_{l_{m}})_{m\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{N}
such that
g_{k_{l_{m}}}(x)\to g(x)
as
m\to\infty
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As a consequence, we obtain that

V(x)^{1/2}\left(f_{k_{l_{m}}}\right)_{Q_{0}}=V(x)^{1/2}f_{k_{l_{m}}}(x)-V(x)^{1/2}g_{k_{l_{m}}}(x)\to G(x)-V(x)^{1/2}g(x)

as
m\to\infty
, for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since
V\neq 0
on a set of positive measure, this implies that the subsequence of averages
\left(\left(f_{k_{l_{m}}}\right)_{Q_{0}}\right)_{m\geq 1}
converges as
m\to\infty
, towards a constant that we shall denote
c
. Consequently, the subsequence
\left(f_{k_{l_{m}}}\right)_{m\geq 1}\subset\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
converges towards
\tilde{g}:=g+c
in
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and

V(x)^{1/2}f_{k_{l_{m}}}(x)=V(x)^{1/2}\left(g_{k_{l_{m}}}(x)+\left(f_{k_{l_{m}}}\right)_{Q_{0}}\right)\to V(x)^{1/2}\tilde{g}(x)

as
m\to\infty
, for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Hence,
V^{1/2}\tilde{g}=G\in\mathrm{L}^{p}
, and
V^{1/2}f_{k}\to V^{1/2}\tilde{g}
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
, as
k\to\infty
. Finally, we have
\nabla_{x}\tilde{g}=\nabla_{x}g=F\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})
, and
\nabla_{x}f_{k}\to\nabla_{x}\tilde{g}
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
as
k\to\infty
. This proves that
\tilde{g}\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and that
f_{k}\to\tilde{g}
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}
, hence
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}
is complete.
∎

The remainder of this section is dedicated to proving a density result for the homogeneous spaces
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
which is analogous to that recorded below for
\mathcal{V}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
from
[
51
, Lemma 2.2]
.

Lemma 2.6
.

If
n\geq 1
,
p\in[1,\infty)
and
V^{p/2}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is dense in
\mathcal{V}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover, if
p_{0},p_{1}\in[1,p]
, then
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is dense in
\mathcal{V}^{1,p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Such a density result for
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
cannot hold under the mere assumption that
V^{p/2}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Indeed, if
n\geq 3
,
p\in[1,n)
, and we choose
V\not\equiv 0
such that
V^{p/2}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then clearly
V^{p/2}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(but
V\not\in\textup{RH}^{\frac{n}{2}}
by Lemma
2.2
). Let
X
denote the closure of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. It follows from the Sobolev embedding theorem and the fact that
V\not\equiv 0
that
\textup{X}\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{p*}
. However,
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, but
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\notin\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. This shows that
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is not dense in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

The following result will allow us to prove in Proposition
2.9
below that it does hold when
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\frac{n}{2}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which is sufficient for our main results.

Proposition 2.7
.

Suppose that either
n\geq 3
and
q\geq\frac{n}{2}
, or
n=2
and
q>1
. If
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
V\!\not\equiv 0
and
p\in[1,n)
, then
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\!\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}\!(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}\!}\!\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{x}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

Since
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}
, there is a sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\nabla_{x}f_{k}\to\nabla_{x}f
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
as
k\to\infty
. In fact, a standard regularisation argument shows that we can assume that
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\dot{\textup{W}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Next, for
R>0
, consider the annulus
C_{R}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:R<\left|x\right|<2R\right\}
and the bump function
\eta_{R}(x):=\eta\left(\frac{x}{R}\right)
, where
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B(0,2))
is such that
\eta\geq 0
and
\eta=1
on
B(0,1)
. The sequence
f_{k}:=(f-(f)_{C_{k}})\eta_{k}
,
k\geq 1
has the required property (see, e.g.,
[
40
, Theorem 4]
).

The Sobolev embedding theorem applied to
f_{k}-f_{j}
,
j,k\geq 1
implies that the sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
. If
g\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
is the limit of this sequence in
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
, then
\nabla_{x}g=\nabla_{x}f
in the distributional sense, hence
f=g+c
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}
. It suffices to prove that
c=0
. We trivially have
cV^{1/2}=V^{1/2}f-V^{1/2}(f-c)
, hence for all balls
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
it holds that

(2.11)

\displaystyle\left|c\right|\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B)}\leq\left\lVert V^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B)}+\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{n}(B)}\left\lVert f-c\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(B)},

by Hölder’s inequality. If
n\geq 3
, then since
V\in\textup{RH}^{\frac{n}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we have

\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{n}(B)}=\left(\int_{B}V^{n/2}\right)^{\frac{1}{n}}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{n}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The same estimate trivially holds for
n=2
. Now, if
p\in[2,n)
, then
p/2\geq 1
, so by Hölder’s inequality,

(2.12)

\displaystyle\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{n}(B)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{n}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{1/2}\leq\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{p}}\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B)}.

If
p\in[1,2)
, then
p/2\in(0,1)
, so by Lemma
2.1
.(iii) we have
V^{p/2}\in\textup{RH}^{\frac{2}{p}}
. We therefore obtain the same estimate as (
2.12
) up to a multiplicative constant. Combining (
2.11
) and (
2.12
), we get

\displaystyle\left|c\right|\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B)}\leq\left\lVert V^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}+\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{p}}\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B)}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}.

If
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is sufficiently large to have
||V^{1/2}||_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B)}>0
, this yields the estimate

\left|c\right|\lesssim\frac{\left\lVert V^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}}{\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B)}}+\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{p}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}.

Finally, note that
\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(B)}=\left\lVert V\right\rVert^{1/2}_{\mathrm{L}^{p/2}(B)}
. If
n\geq 3
, then
V\in\textup{RH}^{\frac{n}{2}}
, with
\frac{p}{2}<\frac{n}{2}
, hence Lemma
2.2
implies that
c=0
by letting the radius of
B
tend to infinity. Similarly, if
n=2
, then
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}
for
q>1
and it follows that
0<\frac{p}{2}<\frac{n}{2}=1<q
, which allows us to conclude using Lemma
2.2
.
∎

We also need a Fefferman–Phong inequality proved by Auscher–Ben Ali in
[
9
, Lemma 2.1]
.

Proposition 2.8

(Fefferman–Phong)
.

If
n\geq 1
,
p\in[1,\infty)
,
q\in(1,\infty)
and
V^{p/2}\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then there exists
\beta\in(0,1)
such that for all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,p}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
it holds that

m_{\beta}\left(l(Q)^{p}\textup{av}_{Q}V^{\frac{p}{2}}\right)\int_{Q}\left|f\right|^{p}\lesssim l(Q)^{p}\int_{Q}\left|\nabla_{\mu}f\right|^{p},

where
m_{\beta}(x):=x
for
0\leq x\leq 1
, and
m_{\beta}(x):=x^{\beta}
for
x\geq 1
.

Proof.

We follow the arguments in the proof
[
51
, Proposition 2.3]
to extend
[
9
, Lemma 2.1]
.
∎

We can now prove the following density results for the homogeneous spaces
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proposition 2.9
.

If
V\not\equiv 0
and either
(i)
,
(ii)
or
(iii)
holds, then
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is dense in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
:

(i)

n\geq 3
,
q\geq\frac{n}{2}
,
p\in[1,n]
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
;

(ii)

n\in\left\{1,2\right\}
,
q>1
,
p\in[1,n]
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
;

(iii)

n\geq 1
,
p\in(n,\infty)
and
V^{p/2}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Moreover, if
p_{0},p_{1}\in[1,\infty)
and either
(i)
,
(ii)
or
(iii)
hold with both
p=p_{0}
and
p=p_{1}
, then
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is dense in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

We will closely follow the argument of
[
51
, Lemma 2.2]
. Let us first remark that our assumptions imply that
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
1\leq p<\infty
. Indeed, since
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}
implies that
V\in\mathrm{L}^{q}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, this always yields
V^{1/2}\in\mathrm{L}^{n}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Let
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By approximating separately the real and imaginary parts of
f
, we may assume that
f
is real valued. For each
N\geq 1
, we consider the truncation

f_{N}(x):=\begin{cases}N,&\text{ if }f(x)>N;\\
f(x),&\text{ if }\left|f(x)\right|\leq N;\\
-N,&\text{ if }f(x)<-N,\end{cases}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. It is well-known that
f_{N}\in\dot{\textup{W}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
\left|\nabla_{x}f_{N}(x)\right|\leq\left|\nabla_{x}f(x)\right|
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Moreover,
\left|f_{N}(x)\right|\leq\left|f(x)\right|
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, hence
f_{N}\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
\left|\nabla_{\mu}f_{N}(x)\right|\leq\left|\nabla_{\mu}f(x)\right|
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Moreover, it is known that
\nabla_{x}f_{N}\to\nabla_{x}f
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
as
N\to\infty
. Similarly, by dominated convergence we deduce that
V^{1/2}f_{N}\to V^{1/2}f
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
as
N\to\infty
. Consequently,
f_{N}\to f
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
N\to\infty
.

Next, let
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be
[0,1]
-valued, with support in
B(0,2)
, and such that
\eta(x)=1
for
\left|x\right|\leq 1
. Then, for all
N\geq 1
and
R\geq 1
, we consider the approximant

f_{N,R}(x):=\eta\left({x}/{R}\right)f_{N}(x)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. First, observe that
V^{1/2}f_{N,R}\to V^{1/2}f_{N}
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
R\to\infty
, by dominated convergence. Then, the product rule shows that
f_{N,R}\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with

\nabla_{x}f_{N,R}-\nabla_{x}f_{N}=R^{-1}\left(\nabla_{x}\eta\right)\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)f_{N}+\left(\eta\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)-1\right)\nabla_{x}f_{N}.

Again, dominated convergence shows that
\left(\eta\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)-1\right)\nabla_{x}f_{N}\to 0
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
as
R\to\infty
. The first term needs to be handled differently depending on the value of
p
.

First, if
1\leq p<n
(and thus
n\geq 2
), Proposition
2.7
shows that
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Therefore, we also have
f_{N}\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. As a consequence, Hölder’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\left\lVert R^{-1}\left(\nabla_{x}\eta\right)\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)f_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq R^{-1}\left\lVert f_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(R\leq\left|x\right|\leq 2R)}\left(\int_{R\leq\left|x\right|\leq 2R}\left|\nabla_{x}\eta\left(\frac{x}{R}\right)\right|^{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/n}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert f_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(R\leq\left|x\right|\leq 2R)}\to 0

as
R\to\infty
.

Next, if
p>n
, then we simply obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert R^{-1}\left(\nabla_{x}\eta\right)\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)f_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim R^{\frac{n}{p}-1}\left\lVert f_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\left\lVert\nabla_{x}\eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\to 0

as
R\to\infty
, since
\frac{n}{p}-1<0
.
Finally, if
p=n
, we note that our assumptions imply that there is always some
r>1
such that
V^{\frac{n}{2}}\in\textup{RH}^{r}
. Indeed, if
n\geq 3
, then
V\in\textup{RH}^{\frac{n}{2}+\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
and we can take
r=\frac{n/2+\varepsilon}{n/2}
. If
n=2
and
q>1
is such that
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}
, then we may take
r=q
. Similarly, if
n=1
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}
, we may take
r=2
thanks to Lemma
2.1
.(iii). We may therefore apply the Fefferman–Phong inequality (Proposition
2.8
) to
f_{N}\in\mathcal{V}^{1,n}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, to find some
\beta\in(0,1)
and
C>0
such that

(2.13)

m_{\beta}\left(\int_{Q}V^{n/2}\right)\int_{Q}\left|f_{N}\right|^{n}\leq Cl(Q)^{n}\int_{Q}\left|\nabla_{\mu}f_{N}\right|^{n}

for all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
N\geq 1
. Therefore, if
\left\{Q_{R}\right\}_{R\geq 1}
is a family of cubes such that
B(0,2R)\subseteq Q_{R}
for all
R\geq 1
, Lemma
2.2
applied to
w:=V\in\textup{RH}^{\frac{n}{2}+\varepsilon}
shows that
\int_{Q_{R}}V^{n/2}>1
for all sufficiently large
R
. Applying (
2.13
) with
Q:=Q_{R}
, we get

\displaystyle\left\lVert R^{-1}\left(\nabla_{x}\eta\right)\left(\frac{\cdot}{R}\right)f_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{n}}\leq\left(\int_{Q_{R}}\left|f_{N}\right|^{n}\right)^{1/n}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{n}}\left(\int_{B(0,2R)}V^{n/2}\right)^{-\frac{\beta}{n}}.

Finally, Lemma
2.2
shows that the right-hand side tends to
0
as
R\to\infty
. This proves that, for any
p\in[1,\infty)
and
N\geq 1
,
f_{N,R}\to f_{N}
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
R\to\infty
.
We can now conclude using the mollification argument used in the proof of
[
51
, Lemma 2.2]
. The second part of the lemma follows, since the construction of the approximating sequence does not depend on
p
.
∎

The approximating sequence used in the proof of Proposition
2.9
above is the same as that constructed in the proof of
[
51
, Lemma 2.2]
. Moreover, by dominated convergence and well-known properties of mollifiers, it is easily shown to converge to the original function
f
in every
