\mathrm{L}^{p}
space that
f
belongs to. We therefore obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 2.10
.

Suppose that
V\not\equiv 0
and either
(i)
,
(ii)
or
(iii)
from Proposition
2.9
holds:

(1)

If
r\in[1,\infty)
and
V^{r/2}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is dense in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(2)

If
r\in[1,\infty)
, then
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is dense in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

2.4.
Holomorphic functional calculus and Hardy spaces for (bi)sectorial operators

2.4.1.
Sectorial and bisectorial operators

We consider the open sectors defined as

\displaystyle S_{\omega}^{+}

\displaystyle:=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:z\neq 0\text{ and }\left|\arg{z}\right|<\omega\right\}

for any
\omega\in\left(0,\pi\right]
. We also let
S_{0}^{+}:=(0,\infty)
, even though it is not open. We can then define the open bisector
S_{\mu}:=S_{\mu}^{+}\cup\left(-S_{\mu}^{+}\right)
for
\mu\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
. We say that a (necessarily closed) operator
T
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is sectorial of type
S_{\omega}^{+}
, or of angle
\omega\in\left[0,\pi\right)
, if the spectrum
\sigma(T)\subseteq\overline{S^{+}_{\omega}}
and for each
\mu\in(\omega,\pi)
, there is
C_{\mu}>0
such that

\left\lVert\left(z-T\right)^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})}\leq C_{\mu}\left|z\right|^{-1}

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{S_{\mu}^{+}}
. Bisectorial operators of type
S_{\omega}
, for
\omega\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, are defined similarly, upon replacing sectors with bisectors. We refer the reader to
[
38
, Section 2.1]
for some well-known properties of these operators.

2.4.2.
Classes of holomorphic functions on sectors

Let
\mu\in(0,\pi)
. We say that a holomorphic function
\varphi:S^{+}_{\mu}\to\mathbb{C}
belongs to
\mathcal{F}(S_{\mu}^{+})
if there are
C,s>0
such that
\left|\varphi(z)\right|\leq C\left(\left|z\right|^{s}+\left|z\right|^{-s}\right)
for all
z\in S^{+}_{\mu}
. For
\alpha,\beta>0
, we say that
\varphi\in\Psi^{\alpha}_{\beta}(S_{\mu}^{+})
if there is a
C>0
such that

\left|\varphi(z)\right|\leq C\left(\left|z\right|^{\beta}\wedge\left|z\right|^{-\alpha}\right)

for all
z\in S^{+}_{\mu}
. We shall also write
\textup{H}^{\infty}(S^{+}_{\mu})
for the class of all bounded holomorphic functions on
S^{+}_{\mu}
. We also consider the classes
\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\mu}^{+}):=\bigcup_{\alpha,\beta>0}\Psi_{\beta}^{\alpha}(S_{\mu}^{+})
and
\Psi^{\infty}_{\infty}(S_{\mu}^{+}):=\bigcap_{\alpha,\beta>0}\Psi_{\beta}^{\alpha}(S_{\mu}^{+})
. We shall in general suppress the reference to the sector when it is irrelevant or implicit. We note that
\textup{H}^{\infty},\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}
and
\Psi^{\infty}_{\infty}
are commutative algebras of functions under pointwise multiplication. Similarly, we define the classes
\Psi^{\alpha}_{\beta}(S_{\omega})
,
\textup{H}^{\infty}(S_{\omega}),\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\omega})
and
\Psi^{\infty}_{\infty}(S_{\omega})
on bisectors
S_{\omega}
for
0<\omega<\frac{\pi}{2}
.

2.4.3.
Holomorphic functional calculus

In this section, we briefly describe the holomorphic functional calculus for (bi)sectorial operators. Good references include
[
38
]
(for sectorial operators) and
[
33
, Chapter 3]
(focusing on bisectorial operators).

Let
\omega\in[0,\pi)
and let
T
be an injective sectorial operator of type
S^{+}_{\omega}
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then,
T
has dense domain and dense range. For each
\mu\in(\omega,\pi]
, there is a map sending any
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}(S_{\mu}^{+})
to a closed operator
\varphi(T)\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
. This map is called the
\mathcal{F}(S_{\mu}^{+})
-functional calculus for
T
. See
[
51
, Theorem A.1]
for a summary of some important properties of this mapping. We mention that if
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}
, then
\varphi(T)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
and has the usual Cauchy integral representation.
If
T
is a sectorial operator of type
S^{+}_{\omega}
on
\mathcal{H}
which is not necessarily injective, then we can consider its injective part
T|_{\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}}
, which is defined as the restriction of
T
to the closure of its range. It is an injective sectorial operator of type
S^{+}_{\omega}
on the Hilbert space
\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
. If
\mu\in(\omega,\pi]
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}(S^{+}_{\mu})
, we
define

\varphi(T):=\varphi(T|_{\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}})\in\mathcal{C}(\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)})
.

We say that
T
has a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus of angle
\mu\in(\omega,\pi)
on
\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
if there is
C\geq 0
such that
\left\lVert\varphi(T)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)})}\leq C\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\infty}
for all
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}(S^{+}_{\mu})
.
Let
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}(S^{+}_{\mu})
for some
\mu\in(\omega,\pi)
. We say that
T
satisfies quadratic estimates with auxiliary function
\varphi
if there is
C\geq 1
such that
C^{-1}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\varphi(tT)f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\leq C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
for all
f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
.
The usual modifications can be made to treat bisectorial operators. We also note that a positive self-adjoint operator is sectorial of type
S_{0}^{+}
and has a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus that is compatible with its Borel functional calculus.

The equivalence between bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus and quadratic estimates is a fundamental theorem due to McIntosh
[
49
]
; see also
[
38
, Theorem 7.3.1]
.

Theorem 2.11

(McIntosh’s theorem)
.

Let
T
be a sectorial operator of angle
\omega_{T}\in[0,\pi)
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then, the following statements are equivalent.

(i)

The operator
T
satisfies quadratic estimates with some (equivalently any non-trivial) auxiliary function
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}(S^{+}_{\mu})
and some
\mu\in(\omega_{T},\pi)
,

(ii)

The operator
T
has a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus of some (equivalently arbitrary) angle
\mu\in(\omega_{T},\pi)
on
\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
.

This also holds for bisectorial operators
T
, upon replacing sectors with bisectors, and
\pi
with
\frac{\pi}{2}
.

We shall also need the following useful tool, see
[
38
, Theorem 5.2.6]
for a proof.

Theorem 2.12

(Calderón–McIntosh reproducing formula)
.

Let
T
be a sectorial operator of angle
\omega_{T}\in[0,\pi)
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Let
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}(S^{+}_{\mu})
be such that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}=1
. Then, for all
f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
it holds that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}\varphi(tT)f\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}=f,

with convergence in the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}
.
The result also holds for bisectorial operators upon replacing sectors with bisectors and provided
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}(S_{\mu})
satisfies
\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(\pm t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}=1
.

2.4.4.
Adjoints and similarity

Let
T
be a sectorial operator of angle
\omega_{T}\in[0,\pi)
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Since
T
is densely defined, it has an adjoint operator
T^{*}
. As is well-known,
T^{*}
is also a sectorial operator of angle
\omega_{T}
. Moreover,
T^{*}
is injective if and only if
T
is injective.

For a meromorphic function
\varphi:S^{+}_{\mu}\to\mathbb{C}
and some
\mu\in(0,\pi)
, the complex conjugate of
\varphi
is the function
\varphi^{*}:S^{+}_{\mu}\to\mathbb{C}
, defined as
\varphi^{*}(z):=\overline{\varphi(\overline{z})}
for all
z\in S^{+}_{\mu}
. The operation of complex conjugation preserves the classes of holomorphic functions introduced in Section
2.4.2
. For all
\mu\in(\omega_{T},\pi)
and
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}(S^{+}_{\mu})
, the adjoint of the bounded operator
\varphi(T)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
is
\varphi(T)^{*}=\varphi^{*}(T^{*})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
. The same result also holds for bisectorial operators up to the usual modifications.

2.4.5.
Off-diagonal estimates

We will be working with operators which cannot generally be represented as integral operators which have pointwise integral kernel decay estimates. As substitutes for such estimates we will use the weaker
off-diagonal estimates
(or Davies–Gaffney estimates). The following definition is taken verbatim from
[
11
, Definition 4.5]
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
p,r\in[1,\infty]
, let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
, and let
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
be finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. A family
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
of bounded linear operators mapping
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};W_{1})
into
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};W_{2})
is said to satisfy
\mathrm{L}^{p}-\mathrm{L}^{r}
off-diagonal estimates of order
\gamma>0
if

\left\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{F}T(z)\left(\mathbbm{1}_{E}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}\lesssim\left|z\right|^{\frac{n}{r}-\frac{n}{p}}\left(1+\frac{\textup{dist}(E,F)}{\left|z\right|}\right)^{-\gamma}\left\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{E}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all measurable subsets
E,F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, all
z\in\Omega
and all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p}
. If there is a
c>0
such that the stronger estimate

\left\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{F}T(z)\left(\mathbbm{1}_{E}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}\lesssim\left|z\right|^{\frac{n}{r}-\frac{n}{p}}\exp{\left(-c\frac{\textup{dist}(E,F)}{\left|z\right|}\right)}\left\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{E}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

holds, then the family is said to satisfy
\mathrm{L}^{p}-\mathrm{L}^{r}
off-diagonal estimates of exponential order. When
p=r
, we simply speak of
\mathrm{L}^{p}
off-diagonal estimates.

2.4.6.
Hardy spaces
\mathbb{H}_{T}^{s,p}

In this section, we recall the definition of Hardy spaces adapted to a given (bi)sectorial operator, following the construction outlined in
[
11
, Chapter 8]
.
Let
W
be a finite dimensional Hilbert space, and let
T
be an operator on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};W)
satisfying the following standard assumptions:

(i)

T
is a bisectorial operator on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};W)
, of angle
\omega_{T}\in\left[0,\pi/2\right)
,

(ii)

T
has a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus on
\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
,

(iii)

The family
\left\{\left(1+itT\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{0\right\}}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order.

For sectorial operators, we replace
\frac{\pi}{2}
by
\pi
in (i), and require instead that
\left\{\left(1+t^{2}T\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates in (iii).
For an arbitrary (bi)sectorial operator
T
satisfying these standard assumptions, the
T
-adapted spaces are constructed as follows. For a given
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}
on any appropriate (bi)sector, we consider the associated extension operator

(2.14)

\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi,T}:\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}\longrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})),

defined for all
f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
and
t>0
as
\left(\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi,T}f\right)(t):=\varphi\left(tT\right)f
if
T
is bisectorial, or
\left(\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi,T}f\right)(t):=\varphi\left(t^{2}T\right)f
if
T
is sectorial.
For
p\in(0,\infty)
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the pre Hardy–Sobolev space
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{\varphi,T}
of smoothness
s
and integrability
p
, adapted to
T
, is then defined to be the set

\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{\varphi,T}:=\left\{f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}\,:\,\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi,T}f\in\text{T}^{s,p}\cap\text{T}^{0,2}\right\}

equipped with the quasinorm
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{\varphi,T}}:=\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi,T}f\right\rVert_{\text{T}^{s,p}}
.
Notice that if
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}
is non-degenerate, then
\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi,T}
is defined on
\mathrm{L}^{2}
, and for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}
there is an equivalence
\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi,T}f\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{0,2}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
by Fubini’s theorem and that of McIntosh.
As a consequence, the condition
\mathbb{Q}_{\psi,H}f\in\textup{T}^{0,2}
is redundant in the definition of
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{\varphi,T}
in that case. Moreover, this also shows that
\mathbb{H}^{0,2}_{\varphi,T}=\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
with equivalent norms. Finally, we mention that, up to equivalent norms, the space
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{\varphi,T}
does not depend on the particular choice of
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}
as long as it non-degenerate and has enough decay at
0
and at
\infty
. In fact, we need
\varphi\in\Psi^{\tau}_{\sigma}
with the following conditions on the decay parameters:

Table 1.

T
is bisectorial

T
is sectorial

If
p\leq 2

\tau>-s+\left|\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}\right|
and
\sigma>s

\tau>-s/2+\left|\frac{n}{4}-\frac{n}{2p}\right|
and
\sigma>s/2

If
p\geq 2

\tau>-s
and
\sigma>s+\left|\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}\right|

\tau>-s/2
and
\sigma>s/2+\left|\frac{n}{4}-\frac{n}{2p}\right|

See
[
11
, Proposition 8.2]
and references therein.
We denote
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{T}:=\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{\varphi,T}
for any suitable choice of
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}
. When
s=0
, we simply write
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}:=\mathbb{H}^{0,p}_{T}
.

2.4.7.
Molecular decompositions for
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}

If
p\leq 1
, the adapted Hardy space
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}
admits a molecular decomposition
[
11
, Theorem 8.17]
. In fact, provided
M
is large enough depending on
p
and
n
, for any
\varepsilon>0
, an arbitrary function
f\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}
admits a decomposition
f=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}m_{k}
with unconditional convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
, where the
m_{k}
are so-called
(\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T},\varepsilon,M)
-molecules and
\left\lVert(\lambda_{k})_{k\geq 1}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{N})}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}}
. The
(\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T},\varepsilon,M)
-molecules are defined in
[
11
, Definition 8.14]
. For
M=1
, their definition is recalled in Lemma
6.7
below.

2.4.8.
Extension and contraction operators associated to an injective sectorial operator

Let
T
be an injective sectorial operator on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of type
S_{\omega}^{+}
for some
\omega\in[0,\pi)
. Let us assume that
T
has a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For
\mu\in(\omega,\pi)
and any non-trivial auxiliary function
\psi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}(S^{+}_{\mu})
, we observe that (since
\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}=\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
) the extension operator
\mathbb{Q}_{\psi,T}
from (
2.14
) has the following mapping property:

\mathbb{Q}_{\psi,T}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\rightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2}((0,\infty),\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t};\mathrm{L}^{2}).

This operator is bounded by McIntosh’s theorem since
T
has a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Indeed, for
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
it holds that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{\psi,T}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,\infty,\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t};\mathrm{L}^{2})}^{2}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\psi(t^{2}T)f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}.

This implies that
\mathbb{Q}_{\psi,T}
has a bounded adjoint
\left(\mathbb{Q}_{\psi,T}\right)^{*}:\mathrm{L}^{2}((0,\infty),\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t};\mathrm{L}^{2})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}
, referred to as a
contraction operator
. Let
\mathbb{C}_{\psi,T}:=(\mathbb{Q}_{\psi^{*},T^{*}})^{*}
. Then, for all
F\in\mathrm{L}^{2}((0,\infty),\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t};\mathrm{L}^{2})
and
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}
, the operator
\mathbb{C}_{\psi,T}
acts as

\langle\mathbb{C}_{\psi,T}F,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle\int_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\psi(t^{2}T)\left[F(t)\right]\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t},g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}},

and the limit exists by McIntosh’s theorem. It follows from the Calderón–McIntosh reproducing formula that the constant
c:=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\psi^{2}(t^{2})\frac{dt}{t}\right)^{-1}
is such that
f=c\mathbb{C}_{\psi,T}(\mathbb{Q}_{\psi,T}f)
for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

2.5.
Schrödinger operators
H
and first-order operators
BD
and
DB

Here we consider
n\geq 3
and non-negative
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The bound (
1.4
) and ellipticity (
1.5
) imply that the sesquilinear form
h:\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathbb{C}
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, defined for all
f,g\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
by

(2.15)

h(f,g):=\left\langle A_{\parallel\parallel}\nabla_{x}f,\nabla_{x}g\right\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})}+\left\langle aV^{1/2}f,V^{1/2}g\right\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

is densely defined, closed, accretive and continuous. Following
[
51
, Section 3]
, there is a unique maximal accretive operator
\mathsf{H}:\mathsf{D}(\mathsf{H})\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
h(f,\varphi)=\left\langle\mathsf{H}f,\varphi\right\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
f\in\mathsf{D}(\mathsf{H})\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We then define
H:=b\mathsf{H}
with
\mathsf{D}(H):=\mathsf{D}(\mathsf{H})
, since
b:=A_{\perp\perp}^{-1}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, so that formally
H=-b\textup{div}_{x}(A\nabla_{x})+baV
.

We now consider the first-order operators of the type introduced by Auscher–McIntosh–Nahmod
[
15
]
from which the second-order operator
H
can be constructed as in
[
51
, Section 3]
. Let
\nabla_{\mu}:\mathsf{D}(\nabla_{\mu})\subset\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\longrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})
denote the unbounded operator with domain
\mathsf{D}(\nabla_{\mu}):=\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and let
\nabla_{\mu}^{*}
denote its adjoint. The self-adjoint operator
D:\mathsf{D}(D)\subset\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})\longrightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
is defined as

Du:=-\begin{bmatrix}0&\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\\
\nabla_{\mu}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}u_{\perp}\\
\left(u_{\parallel},u_{\mu}\right)\end{bmatrix}=-\begin{bmatrix}-\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\left(u_{\parallel},u_{\mu}\right)\\
-\nabla_{\mu}u_{\perp}\end{bmatrix}

for all
u=\left(u_{\perp},u_{\parallel},u_{\mu}\right)\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})=\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\oplus\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})\oplus\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in the domain

\mathsf{D}(D):=\left\{u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2}):u_{\perp}\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\text{ and }\left(u_{\parallel},u_{\mu}\right)\in\mathsf{D}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*})\right\}.

It follows from
[
51
, Lemma 3.1]
that the closure of the range of
D
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
is

(2.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{\mathsf{R}(D)}&=\left\{u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2}):u_{\perp}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\text{ and}\left(u_{\parallel},u_{\mu}\right)=\nabla_{\mu}g\text{ for }g\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right\}.\end{split}

The bounded multiplication operator
B:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})\rightarrow\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
is defined pointwise by

(2.17)

(Bu)(x):=B(x)u(x)\quad\text{ and }\quad B(x):=\begin{bmatrix}b(x)&0&0\\
0&A_{\parallel\parallel}(x)&0\\
0&0&a(x)\end{bmatrix}

for all
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
and almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and it is strictly accretive on
\mathsf{R}(D)
of angle

\omega(B):=\sup_{v\in\mathsf{R}(D)\setminus\{0\}}\left|\arg\left\langle Bv,v\right\rangle\right|\in[0,\pi/2).

The unbounded operators
BD
and
DB
are then understood on the domains
\mathsf{D}(BD)=\mathsf{D}(D)
and
\mathsf{D}(DB)=\left\{u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2}):Bu\in\mathsf{D}(D)\right\}
.
The operators
BD
and
DB
are densely defined bisectorial operators of type
S_{\omega(B)}
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
(see
[
51
, Section 3]
and the references therein).
Moreover, we have the topological direct sum decompositions

\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})=\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}\oplus\mathsf{N}(DB)=\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}\oplus\mathsf{N}(BD)

and the following identities:
\mathsf{R}(DB)=\mathsf{R}(D)
,
\mathsf{R}(BD)=B\mathsf{R}(D)
,
B\mathsf{N}(DB)=\mathsf{N}(D)
and
\mathsf{N}(BD)=\mathsf{N}(D)
.
This and the accretivity of
B
imply that
B\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}=B\overline{\mathsf{R}(D)}=\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}
, and that the restriction
B|_{\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}}:\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}
is an isomorphism.

The operators
\left(BD\right)^{2}
and
\left(DB\right)^{2}
are sectorial of type
S_{2\omega(B)}^{+}
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
.
The block structure of the operators
B
and
D
shows that we can define operators
\widetilde{H}
,
M
and
\widetilde{M}

such that

(2.18)

(BD)^{2}=\begin{bmatrix}H&0\\
0&M\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{ and }\quad(DB)^{2}=\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{H}&0\\
0&\widetilde{M}\end{bmatrix}.

The operators
H
and
\widetilde{H}
are injective sectorial operators of type
S_{2\omega(B)}^{+}
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
M
and
\widetilde{M}
are sectorial operators of type
S_{2\omega(B)}^{+}
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})
. Moreover, if
T\in\left\{DB,BD\right\}
, then bisectoriality of
T
implies that
\mathsf{N}(T^{2})=\mathsf{N}(T)
and therefore that
\overline{\mathsf{R}(T^{2})}=\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
(see the bisectorial counterpart of
[
38
, Proposition 2.1.1 e)]
). As a consequence, (
2.16
) shows that

(2.19)

\overline{\mathsf{R}(\widetilde{H})}\times\overline{\mathsf{R}(\widetilde{M})}=\overline{\mathsf{R}((DB)^{2})}=\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}=\mathsf{R}(D)=\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\overline{\mathsf{R}(\nabla_{\mu})}

and

(2.20)

\overline{\mathsf{R}(H)}\times\overline{\mathsf{R}(M)}=\overline{\mathsf{R}((BD)^{2})}=\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}=B\overline{\mathsf{R}(D)}=\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times A\overline{\mathsf{R}(\nabla_{\mu})}.

2.5.1.
Block functional calculus

Let
\mu\in(2\omega(B),\pi)
, and
\psi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}(S_{\mu}^{+})
. Then, the function
\varphi(z):=\psi(z^{2})
satisfies
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}(S_{\mu/2})
, and
[
33
, Theorem 3.2.20]
shows that for
T\in\left\{DB,BD\right\}
it holds that
\varphi(T)=\psi(T^{2})
,
where the left-hand side is defined by the
\mathcal{F}(S_{\mu/2})
-functional calculus for the bisectorial operator
T
, and the right-hand side is constructed through the
\mathcal{F}(S^{+}_{\mu})
-functional calculus for the sectorial operator
T^{2}
. The block decomposition
(
2.18
) is preserved by the functional calculus, with

(2.21)

\varphi(BD)=\psi((BD)^{2})=\begin{bmatrix}\psi(H)&0\\
0&\psi(M)\end{bmatrix},

and a similar identity for
DB
. A central result obtained by Morris–Turner
[
51
]
is the following.

Theorem 2.14

(
[
51
, Theorem 3.2]
)
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. If
{T\in\left\{DB,BD\right\}}
, then the bisectorial operator
T
has a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus of angle
\omega(B)
on
\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
.

It follows from Theorem
2.14
and McIntosh’s theorem (Theorem
2.11
) that the sectorial operators
H
and
\widetilde{H}
have a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus of angle
2\omega(B)
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, whereas
M
and
\widetilde{M}
have a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus of angle
2\omega(B)
on
\overline{\mathsf{R}(M)}
.

2.5.2.
Intertwining relations

Let
\mathcal{H}:=\overline{\mathsf{R}(D)}=\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}
. We know that
B|_{\mathcal{H}}:\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}
is an isomorphism. Consequently, we have
BD|_{\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}}=B|_{\mathcal{H}}(DB)|_{\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}}\left(B|_{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{-1}
on
\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}
. By similarity, see
[
33
, Proposition 3.2.10]
), we deduce that

(2.22)

\varphi(BD)=B\varphi(DB)\left(B|_{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{-1},

as closed (bounded) operators on
\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}
, whenever
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}(S_{\mu})
,
\omega(B)<\mu<\pi/2
.
The following so-called
intertwining relations
are obtained by following the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 3.6]
.

Lemma 2.15

(Intertwining relations)
.

Let
\mu\in(2\omega(B),\pi)
, and
\psi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}(S_{\mu}^{+})
. Let
\varphi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}(S_{\mu/2})
be defined as
\varphi(z):=\psi(z^{2})
for all
z\in S_{\mu/2}
. Then,

D\varphi(BD)u=\varphi(DB)Du

for all
u\in\mathsf{D}(D)\cap\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}
. Moreover, if
f_{\perp}\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
(f_{\parallel},f_{\mu})\in\mathsf{D}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*})\cap\overline{\mathsf{R}(M)}
, then

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\psi(M)\begin{bmatrix}f_{\parallel}\\
f_{\mu}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\psi(\widetilde{H})\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\begin{bmatrix}f_{\parallel}\\
f_{\mu}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\psi(H)f_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\psi(\widetilde{M})\nabla_{\mu}f_{\perp}.

2.5.3.
Adjoints and similarity

It is useful to introduce the operator
H^{\sharp}:\mathsf{D}(H^{\sharp})\subset\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, formally identified as

(2.23)

H^{\sharp}:=-b^{*}\textup{div}_{x}\left({A_{\parallel\parallel}^{*}\nabla_{x}}\right)+b^{*}a^{*}V.

Its coefficients have exactly the same ellipticity and boundedness properties as those of
H
. Note that
\mathsf{D}(H^{*})=\left\{g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):b^{*}g\in\mathsf{D}(H^{\sharp})\right\}
, and
H^{\sharp}\left(b^{*}g\right)=b^{*}H^{*}g
for all
g\in\mathsf{D}(H^{*})
. In other words, the adjoint operator
H^{*}
is similar to
H^{\sharp}
under conjugation with
b^{*}
. The functional calculus preserves this similarity. In fact, if
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}(S_{\mu}^{+})
for some
\mu\in(2\omega_{B},\pi)
, then
\varphi(H^{*})f=(b^{*})^{-1}\varphi(H^{\sharp})(b^{*}f)

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(see
[
33
, Proposition 3.2.10]
). In the same way, the operator
\widetilde{H}
is similar to
H
under conjugation with
b
.

2.5.4.
Mapping properties of adapted Hardy spaces

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
T\in\left\{BD,DB\right\}
. We obtain the uniform bound

\left\lVert\left(1+itT\right)^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
because
T
is bisectorial. It follows from
[
51
, Proposition 3.7]
and the references therein that the family
\left\{\left(1+itT\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{0\right\}}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates of exponential order.
Following the argument of the proof of
[
11
, Corollary 3.13]
, this implies that for any
S\in\left\{H,\widetilde{H},M,\widetilde{M}\right\}
, the families
\left\{(1+t^{2}S)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
and
\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
both satisfy
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates of exponential order. With Theorem
2.14
, this shows that
T
and
S
satisfy the standard assumptions from Section
2.4.6
(on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
and
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, respectively). This means that the adapted Hardy spaces
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{T}
and
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{S}
can be constructed as in Section
2.4.6
above.

This rest of this section is concerned with establishing the following important diagram connecting these different adapted Hardy spaces; compare with
[
11
, Figure 9.1]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{p}_{BD}\cap\mathsf{R}([BD])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{R}(H^{1/2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{p}_{M}\cap\mathsf{R}(M^{1/2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{BD}\cap\mathsf{D}(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{M}\cap\mathsf{D}(M^{1/2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}\cap\mathsf{R}(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{p}_{\widetilde{H}}\cap\mathsf{R}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{p}_{\widetilde{M}}\cap\mathsf{R}(\nabla_{\mu})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{p}_{BD}\cap\mathsf{R}(BD)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{R}(b\nabla_{\mu}^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{H}^{p}_{M}\cap\mathsf{R}(A\nabla_{\mu})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[BD]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1/2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\nabla_{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{1/2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\nabla_{\mu}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

Figure 1.
Relations between various adapted Hardy spaces

All spaces appearing on the diagram are intersections of some operator-adapted Hardy space with one of its dense subsets, for both the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
norm and the corresponding Hardy space norm. Moreover, each arrow is a bijection which is bounded from above and below for the correponding
p
-(quasi)norms.

We shall follow the construction outlined in
[
11
, Section 8.6]
, starting with the first column.
Let
T\in\left\{BD,DB\right\}
. We know that
T
is a bisectorial operator of angle
\omega(B)\in\left[0,\pi/2\right)
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
, with a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus on
\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
. It follows from equation (4.8) in
[
51
]
that the operators
T
and
[T]
have the same domain and the same range. Let
p>0
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
. By following the argument of
[
11
, Corollary 8.11]
, we also get that they are bijections mapping
\mathbb{H}^{s+1,p}_{T}\cap\mathsf{D}(T)
to
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{T}\cap\mathsf{R}(T)
and satisfying

(2.24)

\left\lVert Tf\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{T}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s+1,p}_{T}}\eqsim\left\lVert\left[T\right]f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{T}}

for all
f\in\mathbb{H}^{s+1,p}_{T}\cap\mathsf{D}(T)
. Moreover, we may follow the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 8.34]
and use the intertwining relations from Section
2.5.2
to obtain that the map
{B:\mathbb{H}_{DB}^{s,p}\cap\mathsf{R}(D)\to\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{BD}\cap\mathsf{R}(BD)}

obtained by restriction of the operator
B
, is bijective and
\left\lVert Bf\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{BD}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{DB}}
for all
f\in\mathbb{H}_{DB}^{s,p}\cap\mathsf{R}(D)
.
We can also adapt the so-called regularity shift from
[
3
, Proposition 5.6]
to deduce that the map
D:\mathbb{H}^{s+1,p}_{BD}\cap\mathsf{D}(D)\to\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{DB}\cap\mathsf{R}(D)
is bijective and satisfies
\left\lVert Df\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{DB}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s+1,p}_{BD}}
for all
f\in\mathbb{H}^{s+1,p}_{BD}\cap\mathsf{D}(D)
.
This is because the proofs of
[
11
, Lemma 8.34]
and
[
3
, Proposition 5.6]
extend to the operators
BD
and
DB
considered here once the following local coercivity inequality is established.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
u\in\mathsf{D}(D)
. Then, for all
t>0
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
it holds that

\int_{B(x,t)}\left|Du\right|^{2}\lesssim\int_{B(x,2t)}\left|BDu\right|^{2}+t^{-2}\int_{B(x,2t)}\left|u\right|^{2}.

Proof.

We follow the argument of the proof of
[
17
, Lemma 5.14]
. Let
u\in\mathsf{D}(D)
, and let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a ball of radius
r>0
. Let
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
0\leq\eta\leq 1
,
\eta=1
on
B
and
\textup{supp}\left(\eta\right)\subset 2B
with
\left\lVert\nabla_{x}\eta\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim r^{-1}
. The proof of
[
51
, Proposition 3.7]
shows that
\eta u\in\mathsf{D}(D)
, with

D(\eta u)=\eta Du+\begin{bmatrix}\left(\nabla_{x}\eta\right)\cdot u_{\parallel}\\
-\left(\nabla_{x}\eta\right)u_{\perp}\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

This allows to conclude as in the proof of
[
17
, Lemma 5.14]
.
∎

Since
\mathsf{D}(D)=\mathsf{D}(BD)
,
\mathsf{R}(D)=\mathsf{R}(DB)
and
\mathsf{R}([BD])=\mathsf{R}(BD)
,
[
11
, Lemma 8.7]
shows that each space appearing in the first column of Figure
1
is the intersection of an operator-adapted Hardy space
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{T}
with one if its dense subsets. Density holds for both the
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{T}
norm and the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
norm.
Following the argument of
[
11
, Section 8.6]
, we also have the decomposition
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{BD}=\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{H}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{M}
with the estimate

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{BD}}\eqsim\left\lVert u_{\perp}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{H}}+\left\lVert(u_{\parallel},u_{\mu})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{M}}
.
Similarly, we have the decomposition
\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{DB}=\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{\widetilde{H}}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{s,p}_{\widetilde{M}}
. Splitting each space in the first column according to the corresponding decomposition, we obtain the complete Figure
1
.

3.
Hardy spaces adapted to Schrödinger operators

The Fefferman–Stein Hardy spaces
\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(see
[
59
, Chapter III]
) are well-known to be adapted to the Laplace operator
-\Delta
. In this section, we consider Hardy spaces adapted to the Schrödinger operator
H_{0}:=-\Delta+V
and introduced by Dziubaǹski and Zienkiewicz in
[
30
]
. We begin by considering
n\geq 1
and non-negative
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The operator
H_{0}:=-\Delta+V
is associated to the sesquilinear form
h_{0}
defined by setting
A_{\parallel\parallel}\equiv 1
and
a\equiv 1
in the form
h
defined by (
2.15
).
The form
h_{0}
is symmetric, densely defined, accretive and closed. The properties of the self-adjoint operator
H_{0}
below are well-known, but a proof is included for completeness. Note that
-\Delta
, defined by setting
V\equiv 0
, generates the classical heat semigroup
\left\{e^{t\Delta}\right\}_{t\geq 0}
given by

e^{t\Delta}f=p_{t}\ast f
, where
p_{t}(x):=(4\pi t)^{-n/2}\exp{\left(-\frac{\left|x\right|^{2}}{4t}\right)}
, for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t>0
.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
n\geq 1
and
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is non-negative, then
-H_{0}
generates a holomorphic semigroup
\left\{e^{-zH_{0}}\right\}_{\operatorname{Re}{{z}}>0}
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and the restriction
\left\{e^{-tH_{0}}\right\}_{t>0}
is real, positive and dominated by the classical heat-semigroup, in the sense that for all
t\geq 0
and all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
it holds that

(3.1)

\left|e^{-tH_{0}}f\right|\leq e^{t\Delta}\left|f\right|

pointwise almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proof.

Since
H_{0}
is self-adjoint and positive, it is sectorial of type
S_{0}^{+}
and thus
-H_{0}
generates a bounded holomorphic semigroup by
[
52
, Theorem 1.54]
.
The semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH_{0}}\right\}_{t>0}
is real and positive by Proposition 2.5 and Theorem 2.7 in
[
52
]
. It is elementary to check, using well-known properties of the Sobolev space
\textup{W}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, that for any real-valued
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it holds that
\left|u\right|\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
h_{0}(\left|u\right|,\left|u\right|)=h_{0}(u,u)
.
The domination property (
3.1
) then follows from Proposition 2.23 and Theorem 2.24 in
[
52
]
.
∎

If
p\in[1,\infty]
, then it follows from (
3.1
) and Young’s convolution inequality that

(3.2)

\displaystyle\left\lVert e^{-tH_{0}}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\left|f\right|\ast p_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\left\lVert p_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p}
and
t>0
. In addition, for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
and
t>0
we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert e^{-tH_{0}}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\lVert\left|f\right|\ast p_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\lVert p_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}.

It follows that the operators
e^{-tH_{0}}
are integral operators (see, e.g.,
[
4
, Theorem 1.3]
). Namely, for each
t>0
there is a bounded measurable function
{k_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}}
such that

(3.3)

(e^{-tH_{0}}f)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}k_{t}(x,y)f(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The self-adjointness of the semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH_{0}}\right\}_{t>0}
implies that
k_{t}(x,y)=\overline{k_{t}(y,x)}
for almost every
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Moreover, testing the domination property (
3.1
) and the positivity of the semigroup with (
3.3
) and
f:={\left\lvert B(\varepsilon,z)\right\rvert}^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{B(\varepsilon,z)}
for arbitrary
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, we obtain by Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem that

(3.4)

\displaystyle 0\leq k_{t}(x,y)\leq p_{t}(x-y)=(4\pi t)^{-n/2}\exp{\left(-\frac{\left|x-y\right|^{2}}{4t}\right)}

for almost every
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.
These Gaussian estimates are strengthened below.

Lemma 3.2
.

If
n\geq 3
,
q>\frac{n}{2}
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}
and
\sigma\in(0,\delta\wedge 1)
, then:

(i)

There exists
c>0
, and for each
N>0
there exists
C_{N}\geq 0
, such that

0\leq k_{t}(x,y)\leq C_{N}t^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-c\frac{\left|x-y\right|^{2}}{t}}\left(1+\frac{t^{1/2}}{\rho(x)}+\frac{t^{1/2}}{\rho(y)}\right)^{-N}

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
t>0
.

(ii)

There exists
c>0
, and for each
N>0
there exists
C_{N}\geq 0
, such that

\left|k_{t}(x,y)-k_{t}(x,z)\right|\leq C_{N}\left(\frac{\left|y-z\right|}{t^{1/2}}\right)^{\sigma}t^{-n/2}e^{-c\frac{\left|x-y\right|^{2}}{t}}\left(1+\frac{t^{1/2}}{\rho(x)}+\frac{t^{1/2}}{\rho(y)}\right)^{-N}

for all
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\left|y-z\right|<t^{1/2}
.

(iii)

There exists
c>0
and
C\geq 0
such that

\left|k_{t}(x,y)-k_{t}(x,z)\right|\leq C\left(\frac{\left|y-z\right|}{t^{1/2}}\right)^{\sigma}t^{-n/2}e^{-c\frac{\left|x-y\right|^{2}}{t}}

for all
t>0
and all
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\left|x-z\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\left|x-y\right|
.

Proof.

The estimate (i) can be found in
[
46
, Theorem 1]
.
The Hölder continuity (ii) can be found in
[
32
, Proposition 4.11]
. The third item is a combination of the previous two.
∎

3.1.
Dziubaǹski–Zienkiewicz Hardy spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}

We now return to consider
n\geq 1
and non-negative
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we define the maximal function

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{V}f:=\sup_{t>0}\left|e^{-tH_{0}}f\right|

as a lattice supremum to ensure measurability of
\mathcal{M}_{V}f
(see, e.g.,
[
50
, Lemma 2.6.1]
for details). For
0<p\leq\infty
, this allows us to define the (quasi)normed space

\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\text{pre}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right):=\left\{f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\mathcal{M}_{V}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right\}

with the (quasi)norm
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\text{H}^{p}_{V}}:=\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
for all
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\text{pre}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. To see that this is indeed a (quasi)norm, observe that if
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}=0
, then
\left|\left(e^{-tH_{0}}f\right)(x)\right|\leq\left(\mathcal{M}_{V}f\right)(x)=0
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
t>0
, so
\left\lVert e^{-tH_{0}}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=0
for all
t>0
, and the limit as
t\to 0
shows that
f=0
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The Dziubaǹski–Zienkiewicz Hardy space
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
can be defined as a completion of
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\text{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We will construct a completion of
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in Section
3.1.2
after establishing the following preliminaries.

3.1.1.
Properties for
p\geq 1

At the level of the pre-completed spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\text{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have the following result.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be nonnegative. Then
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with equivalent
p
-norms for all
p\in(1,\infty]
. Moreover, if
f\in\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}
.

Proof.

Let
1<p\leq\infty
and
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The domination property (
3.1
) implies that

(3.5)

\left(\mathcal{M}_{V}f\right)(x)=\sup_{t>0}\left|\left(e^{-tH_{0}}f\right)(x)\right|\leq\sup_{t>0}\left(\left|f\right|\ast p_{t}\right)(x)\lesssim\mathcal{M}\left(\left|f\right|\right)(x)

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
\mathcal{M}
is the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator (see
[
58
, Chapter III, Theorem 2 (a)]
for the last estimate). Since
p>1
, the maximal theorem implies that
f\in\textup{H}_{V}^{p}
, with
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}=\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

Conversely, if
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\text{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
then for all
t>0
we have
e^{-tH_{0}}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
\left\lVert e^{-tH_{0}}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}
. Moreover, since
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it holds that
e^{-tH_{0}}f\to f
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
t\to 0^{+}
, by strong continuity of the semigroup. In particular, for every
\varphi\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}
we have
\langle f,\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\langle e^{-tH_{0}}f,\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
.
Since in addition we have

\displaystyle\left|\langle e^{-tH_{0}}f,\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|\leq\left\lVert e^{-tH_{0}}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}}

for all
t>0
, we obtain that
\left|\langle f,\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}}
for arbitrary
\varphi\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}
. This implies that
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}
.

Now let
f\in\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\text{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The previous argument gives

\left|\langle f,\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}

for all
\varphi\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
. For each
N\geq 1
, we apply this estimate with
\varphi:=\varphi_{N}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
, where

\varphi_{N}(x):=\begin{cases}\frac{f(x)}{\left|f(x)\right|}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,N)}(x)&\text{ if }f(x)\neq 0,\\
0&\text{ if }f(x)=0.\end{cases}

This yields
\int_{B(0,N)}\left|f(x)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}\left\lVert\varphi_{N}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}
and the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

If
V\equiv 0
, then
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\textup{H}^{p}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
p\in\left(0,\infty\right]
. Indeed, in this case
H_{0}=-\Delta
, and for
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
it holds that

\left(\mathcal{M}_{V}f\right)(x)=\sup_{t>0}\left|\left(f\ast p_{t}\right)(x)\right|
, so the fact that
p_{1}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
yields the claim (see
[
59
, Chapter III]
).

3.1.2.
The space
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}

We now define the Banach space
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
that was mentioned in the introduction.
Lemma
3.3
shows that there is a continuous embedding
\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The completion
of
\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in
\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, in the sense of
[
13
, Definition 2.1]
, will be denoted by
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Its existence follows from
[
13
, Proposition 2.2]
and the fact that every Cauchy sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
in
\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
that converges to
0
in
\mathrm{L}^{1}
satisfies
f_{k}\to 0
in
\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. This follows from the
\mathrm{L}^{1}
-boundedness of the semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH_{0}}\right\}_{t>0}
(see (
3.2
) above). In fact, for every
t>0
and
k\geq 1
, since
\lim_{m\to\infty}f_{m}=0
in
\mathrm{L}^{1}
, this implies that

\displaystyle e^{-tH_{0}}f_{k}=\lim_{m\to\infty}e^{-tH_{0}}(f_{k}-f_{m}),

with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Up to extraction of a subsequence, we get that

\displaystyle\left|(e^{-tH_{0}}f_{k})(x)\right|=\liminf_{m\to\infty}\left|e^{-tH_{0}}(f_{k}-f_{m})(x)\right|\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}\mathcal{M}_{V}(f_{k}-f_{m})(x)

for all
t>0
,
k\geq 1
and almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Taking the supremum over
t>0
, integrating over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and finally using Fatou’s lemma, we deduce that

\left\lVert f_{k}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}\left\lVert f_{k}-f_{m}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}

for all
k\geq 1
. The conclusion follows from the Cauchy property of
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
in
\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For completeness we spell out that
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
consists of all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for which there is a Cauchy sequence

(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
in
\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
converges to
f
in
\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, equipped with the (compatibly defined) norm
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\lVert f_{k}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}
. If
n\geq 3
,
q>\frac{n}{2}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then it also follows from Corollary
3.13
that the classical Hardy space
\textup{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is continuously embedded into
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

3.1.3.
Equivalent norms on
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}

We will use an equivalent characterisation of
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
obtained by Dekel–Kerkyacharian–Kyriazis–Petrushev in
[
25
]
. For
n\geq 1
and non-negative
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we know that
H_{0}
is a non-negative self-adjoint operator on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, mapping real-valued functions to real-valued functions, and the semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH_{0}}\right\}_{t>0}
satisfies the Gaussian bound (
3.4
), so
H_{0}
satisfies the hypotheses (H1) and (H2) of
[
25
, Section 1]
.
Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
be a real-valued and even Schwartz function. For
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we consider the maximal function
\mathcal{M}_{V}(f,\varphi):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,\infty]
defined as

\mathcal{M}_{V}(f,\varphi):=\sup_{t>0}\left|\varphi(tH_{0}^{1/2})f\right|,

where the supremum is understood as a lattice supremum (as at the start of Section
3.1
), and
\varphi(tH_{0}^{1/2})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2})
is defined by the Borel functional calculus for the positive self-adjoint operator
H_{0}^{1/2}
(see Section
2.4.3
). The equivalent characterisation that we shall need is the following result.

Lemma 3.5

(
[
25
, Theorem 3.7]
)
.

Let
p\in(0,1]
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
be real-valued, even, and such that
\varphi(0)\neq 0
. If
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
if and only if
\mathcal{M}_{V}(f,\varphi)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover,
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}(f,\varphi)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
for all
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

3.1.4.
Atomic decompositions

Lemma
3.3
above shows that those Hardy spaces enjoy properties similar to those satisfied by the Fefferman–Stein Hardy spaces. Another very important property that these spaces share with the classical Hardy spaces
\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
p\leq 1
is their atomic decomposition. In this section, unless specified otherwise, we fix
n\geq 3
,
q>\frac{n}{2}
, the potential
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and we let
\delta:=2-\frac{n}{q}>0
.
The definition of the atoms in the spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is based upon the important critical radius function mentioned in subsection
2.2
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1\wedge\delta},1]
. An
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-atom for
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a function
{a:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}}
associated to a ball
B=B(x,r)
such that

(i)

r\leq\rho(x,V),

(ii)

\textup{supp}\left(a\right)\subseteq\overline{B},

(iii)

\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\leq\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{-1/p},

(iv)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0
if
r\leq\frac{1}{4}\rho(x,V)
.

It can be shown that any such atom
a
belongs to
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and satisfies
\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\text{H}^{p}_{V}}\leq C
, where
C
only depends on
n,p,q
and
\llbracket V\rrbracket_{q}
(see
[
30
, Section 4]
).

We note that if
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
(a_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
(\lambda_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{C}
are such that
f=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}a_{k}
with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then

(3.6)

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}^{p}\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}\left|\lambda_{k}\right|^{p}\left\lVert a_{k}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}^{p}.

Indeed, for all
t>0
we can use
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-boundedness of
e^{-tH_{0}}
to obtain
e^{-tH_{0}}f=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}e^{-tH_{0}}a_{k}

with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
. Using the triangle inequality and taking the (lattice) supremum over all
t>0
implies that
(\mathcal{M}_{V}f)(x)\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}\left|\lambda_{k}\right|(\mathcal{M}_{V}a_{k})(x)

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Raising this inequality to the power
p\leq 1
, and integrating with respect to
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we get (
3.6
). In particular, if
\left\{a_{k}\right\}_{k}
is a collection of atoms for
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
, and
(\lambda_{k})_{k\geq 1}\in\ell^{p}(\mathbb{N})
, then
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
with
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert(\lambda_{k})_{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}}
.

The atomic decomposition for the spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
was obtained by Dziubaǹski–Zienkiewicz
[
30
, Theorem 1.11]
. It shows that every function
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{c}
admits such a decomposition.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1\wedge\delta},1]
, and let
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. There exists a sequence
\left\{a_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}
of
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-atoms for
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
, and numbers
(\lambda_{j})_{j\geq 1}\in\ell^{p}(\mathbb{N})
such that
f=\sum_{j\geq 1}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover,
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert\left\{\lambda_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{N})}
.

While the result
[
30
, Theorem 1.11]
doesn’t mention any particular notion of convergence, inspection of the proof reveals that one can obtain
\mathrm{L}^{2}
convergence of the atomic decomposition, because if
h^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
0<p\leq 1
, denote the local Hardy spaces of Goldberg
[
36
]
, then any function
f\in h^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
admits an
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-converging atomic decomposition for
h^{p}
in the sense of
[
36
, Lemma 5]
, as follows from the proof and the corresponding result for
\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Another, more “abstract”, atomic decomposition for the Hardy spaces
\textup{H}_{V}^{p}
was obtained in
[
25
]
for general non-negative self-adjoint operators with certain Gaussian heat kernel bounds. If
n\geq 1
and
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is non-negative, then the heat kernel for the non-negative self-adjoint operator
H_{0}:=-\Delta+V
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies (
3.4
), so the main assumptions (H1)–(H2) in
[
25
, Section 1]
are satisfied. The authors introduced the following type of atoms for such operators.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
p\in(0,1]
and
m:=\lfloor\frac{n}{2p}\rfloor+1
. A function
a:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
is called an abstract
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-atom associated with the operator
H_{0}
if there exists a function
b\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{m})
and a ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of radius
r_{B}>0
such that

(i)

a=H_{0}^{m}b
,

(ii)

\textup{supp}\left(b\right)\subseteq B
(hence
\textup{supp}\left(H_{0}^{k}b\right)\subseteq B
for all
k\in\left\{0,\cdots,m\right\}
),

(iii)

\left\lVert H_{0}^{k}b\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\leq r_{B}^{2(m-k)}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{-1/p}
for all
k\in\left\{0,\cdots,m\right\}
.

A particular case of
[
25
, Theorem 1.4]
is the following atomic decomposition result.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
p\in(0,1]
,
n\geq 1
and
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be non-negative. If
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then there exists a sequence
\left\{a_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}
of abstract
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-atoms associated with
H_{0}
, and numbers
\left\{\lambda_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}\in\ell^{p}(\mathbb{N})
such that
f=\sum_{j\geq 1}\lambda_{j}a_{j}
with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and in the
\textup{H}_{V}^{p}
norm. Moreover,
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert\left\{\lambda_{j}\right\}_{j}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{N})}
.

Remark 3.10
.

Since the abstract
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-atoms associated with
H_{0}
are in
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we can combine Theorems
3.7
and
3.9
to obtain that for any bounded operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2})
satisfying a uniform bound
\left\lVert Ta\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim 1
for all
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-atoms
a
for
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(in the sense of Definition
3.6
), it follows that
\left\lVert Tf\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
for all
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(recall (
3.6
) above).

3.1.5.
Molecular decompositions

Molecules adapted to the spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
have already been introduced in
[
21
, Section 3.2]
. We modify their definition in order to get rid of any size conditions on the cube (or ball, in the context of the aforementioned reference) associated to the molecule. This seems like a reasonable definition in view of the proof of Lemma
6.7
below.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
p\in(0,1]
. A function
m:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
is called a
(p,\varepsilon)
-molecule associated to a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
if there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

(i)

\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(Q))}\leq(2^{j}\ell(Q))^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}2^{-j\varepsilon}
for all
j\geq 1
,

(ii)

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}m(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right|\leq\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\min\left\{1,\left(\ell{(Q)}^{2}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}V\right)^{1/2}\right\}
.

Note that the first condition automatically implies that
m\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, hence the second condition makes sense.
The following result adapts
[
21
, Lemma 3.9]
by incorporating the changes made in the definition of the molecules.

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\in(\frac{n}{2},\infty]
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and let
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}>0
. If
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\frac{\delta}{2}},1]
, then every
(p,\varepsilon)
-molecule belongs to
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim 1
.

Proof.

We follow the argument of the proof of
[
21
, Lemma 3.9]
.
For
j\geq 1
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
a_{j}(x):=\left(m(x)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{C_{j}(Q)}m\right)\mathbbm{1}_{C_{j}(Q)}(x)
.
Hence,
\textup{supp}\left(a_{j}\right)\subseteq C_{j}(Q)
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a_{j}=0
for all
j\geq 1
.
We introduce
\alpha_{k}:=\int_{C_{k}(Q)}m
and
\chi_{k}:=\frac{1}{\left\lvert C_{k}(Q)\right\rvert}\mathbbm{1}_{C_{k}(Q)}
.
Then, for all
j\geq 1
, let
N_{j}:=\sum_{k=j}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}
. We let
b_{j}:=N_{j+1}(\chi_{j+1}-\chi_{j})
for all
j\geq 1
, and obtain that
\textup{supp}\left(b_{j}\right)\subseteq C_{j}(Q)\cup C_{j+1}(Q)
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}b_{j}=0
for all
j\geq 1
.
We can estimate

(3.7)

\displaystyle\left\lVert a_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(Q))}+\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{C_{j}(Q)}m\right|\left\lvert C_{j}(Q)\right\rvert^{1/2}\leq 2\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(Q))}\lesssim(2^{j}\ell(Q))^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}2^{-j\varepsilon}.

Similarly, we get
\left\lVert b_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim 2^{-j\varepsilon}(2^{j}\ell(Q))^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}
. Denoting
a:=\chi_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}m=\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{4Q}}{\left\lvert 4Q\right\rvert}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}m\right)
, we have that
m=a+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(a_{j}+b_{j})
with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Consequently, it follows from (
3.6
) that

\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}(a)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}(a_{j})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}(b_{j})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}.

It remains to bound each term in the right-hand side. Let us start with the terms involving
a_{j}
. Since
p\leq 2
, we can use Hölder’s inequality to get the following local bound for all
j\geq 1
.

(3.8)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}a_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(2^{j+2}Q)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}a_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(2^{j+2}Q)}\left\lvert 2^{j+2}Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert a_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}(2^{j+2}\ell(Q))^{\frac{n}{p}-\frac{n}{2}}\lesssim 2^{-j\varepsilon},

where we have used the boundedness of the maximal operator
\mathcal{M}_{V}
on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(which follows from (
3.5
)) and the estimate (
3.7
). For the global part, we take advantage of the cancellation
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a_{j}=0
and support property
\textup{supp}\left(a_{j}\right)\subseteq C_{j}(Q)
to write for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 2^{j+2}Q
,

\displaystyle(e^{-tH_{0}}a_{j})(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}k_{t}(x,y)a_{j}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{C_{j}(Q)}\left(k_{t}(x,y)-k_{t}(x,x_{Q})\right)a_{j}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,

where
x_{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the centre of the cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Since by assumption
\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}<p\leq 1
, there is some
\tilde{\delta}<\delta\leq 2
such that
\frac{n}{n+\tilde{\delta}/2}<p\leq 1
. The choice
\sigma:=\frac{\tilde{\delta}}{2}
ensures that
\sigma\in(0,\delta\wedge 1)
and that
\frac{n}{n+\sigma}<p
(even if
q=\infty
and thus
\delta=2
).
Since for all
y\in C_{j}(Q)
it holds that
\left|x-y\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\left|x-x_{Q}\right|
, item (iii) of Lemma
3.2
implies that there is
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|(e^{-tH_{0}}a_{j})(x)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{C_{j}(Q)}\left(\frac{\left|y-x_{Q}\right|}{t^{1/2}}\right)^{\sigma}t^{-n/2}e^{-c\frac{\left|x-x_{Q}\right|^{2}}{t}}\left|a_{j}(y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\frac{(2^{j}\ell(Q))^{\sigma}}{\left|x-x_{Q}\right|^{n+\sigma}}2^{-j\varepsilon}(2^{j}\ell(Q))^{n-\frac{n}{p}}.

Using that
n-(n+\sigma)p<0
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}a_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 2^{j+2}Q)}\lesssim(2^{j}\ell(Q))^{\sigma}2^{-j\varepsilon}(2^{j}\ell(Q))^{n-\frac{n}{p}}(2^{j}\ell(Q))^{-(n+\sigma)+\frac{n}{p}}=2^{-j\varepsilon}.

Combining this with the local bound (
3.8
) we obtain that
\left\lVert a_{j}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim 2^{-j\varepsilon}
for all
j\geq 1
. The exact same argument can be applied to the functions
b_{j}
to obtain the bounds
\left\lVert b_{j}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim 2^{-j\varepsilon}
for all
j\geq 1
.

It therefore remains to estimate
\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
. Let us consider a ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
centred at
x_{B}=x_{Q}
, and such that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}B\subseteq 4Q\subseteq B
. We shall now treat two different cases. The first case is when
r_{B}\leq\rho(x_{B})
. In this situation, we can first obtain a local bound

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(8Q)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\left\lvert 8Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}m\right|\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-1}\lesssim 1,

where we have used the cancellation condition on
m
in the last inequality. To obtain the global bound, we then apply item (i) from Lemma
3.2
with
N=\frac{\delta}{2}
to get for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 8Q
,

\displaystyle(e^{-tH_{0}}a)(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{-1/p}\left(\ell(Q)^{2}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}V\right)^{1/2}\int_{4Q}t^{-n/2}e^{-c\frac{\left|x-y\right|^{2}}{t}}\left(\frac{t^{1/2}}{\rho(y)}\right)^{-\frac{\delta}{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-(n+\frac{\delta}{2})/2}e^{-\tilde{c}\frac{\left|x-x_{Q}\right|^{2}}{t}}\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{-1/p}\left(r_{B}^{2}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{1/2}\int_{B}\rho(y)^{\frac{\delta}{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-n-\frac{\delta}{2}}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{-1/p}\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\delta/2}\rho(x_{B})^{\frac{\delta}{2}}\left\lvert B\right\rvert\;\lesssim\;r_{B}^{\frac{\delta}{2}}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-n-\frac{\delta}{2}},

where we have used (
2.5
) and the fact that
\rho(y)\eqsim\rho(x_{B})
for
y\in B
since
r_{B}\leq\rho(x_{B})
. We can now use that
n-p(n+\frac{\delta}{2})<0
to get
\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 8Q)}\lesssim 1
.

Finally we treat the case
r_{B}>\rho(x_{B})
. The reference
[
31
, Proposition 5]
provides a countable collection of points
(x_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

(3.9)

\mathbb{R}^{n}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}B(x_{\alpha},\rho(x_{\alpha})),

with bounded overlap (see Section
2.1.2
for a definition). It is shown in
[
55
, Lemma 1.4]
that there are
C>0
and
k_{0}>0
such that

m(x)\leq C(1+\left|x-y\right|m(y))^{k_{0}}m(y)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This property implies that there is
c\geq 0
such that for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
R>\rho(x)
such that
B(x,R)\cap B(x_{\alpha},\rho(x_{\alpha})))\neq\emptyset
, it holds that
B(x_{\alpha},\rho(x_{\alpha}))\subseteq B(x,cR)
.

We can use the covering (
3.9
) to decompose the function
a=\frac{1}{\left\lvert 4Q\right\rvert}(\int m)\mathbbm{1}_{4Q}
into a finite linear combination of atoms supported in balls of the form
B(x_{\alpha},\rho(x_{\alpha}))
. Let us write
B_{\alpha}:=B(x_{\alpha},\rho(x_{\alpha}))
for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
. Let us also define
B_{1}^{\prime}:=B_{1}
, and
B_{n}^{\prime}:=B_{n}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{n-1}B_{k}\right)
for
n\geq 2
. Then we can decompose
a=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}\\
{B_{\alpha}^{\prime}\cap 4Q\neq\emptyset}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}
,
where
\lambda_{\alpha}:=\left\lvert B_{\alpha}\right\rvert^{1/p}\left\lVert a\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\alpha}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\neq 0
and
a_{\alpha}:=\lambda_{\alpha}^{-1}a\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\alpha}^{\prime}}
. It trivially follows that each
a_{\alpha}
is supported in
B_{\alpha}
, with
\left\lVert a_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\left\lvert B_{\alpha}\right\rvert^{-1/p}
. Moreover, since for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
such that
B_{\alpha}\cap 4Q\neq\emptyset
, it holds that
B_{\alpha}\cap B\neq\emptyset
, we get that
B_{\alpha}\subset cB
, since
r_{B}>\rho(x_{B})
.
Let us denote
A_{Q}:=\left\{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}:B_{\alpha}^{\prime}\cap 4Q\neq\emptyset\right\}
.
The bounded overlap of the balls
\left\{B_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}
implies that
\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in A_{Q}}\left\lvert B_{\alpha}\right\rvert\lesssim\left\lvert\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}\\
{B_{\alpha}\cap 4Q\neq\emptyset}\end{subarray}}B_{\alpha}\right\rvert\lesssim\left\lvert cB\right\rvert\eqsim\left\lvert B\right\rvert
.
As a consequence, the bound
\left|\int m\right|\leq\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{1-1/p}
implies that

(3.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(\sum_{\alpha\in A_{Q}}\left|\lambda_{\alpha}\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}&\lesssim\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}\\
{B_{\alpha}\cap 4Q\neq\emptyset}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert B_{\alpha}\right\rvert\right)^{1/p}\lesssim\left|\int m\right|\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{-1}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1/p}\lesssim 1.\end{split}

Notice that since

a=\sum_{\alpha\in A_{Q}}\lambda_{\alpha}a_{\alpha}
and
p\leq 1
, we have
\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}^{p}\leq\sum_{\alpha\in A_{Q}}\left|\lambda_{\alpha}\right|^{p}\left\lVert a_{\alpha}\right\rVert^{p}_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}
.
The estimate (
3.10
) shows that it suffices to show that
\left\lVert a_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim 1
for all
\alpha\in A_{Q}
. As before, we get the local bound

\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}a_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(2B_{\alpha})}\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}a_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(2B_{\alpha})}\left\lvert 2B_{\alpha}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim 1
.
For the global bound, we note that for all
t>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 2B_{\alpha}
and
y\in B_{\alpha}
it holds that
\left|x-y\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\left|x-x_{\alpha}\right|
and
\rho(y)\eqsim\rho(x_{\alpha})
. Consequently, we can use as before the estimate (i) from Lemma
3.2
with an arbitrary
N\geq 1
to obtain

\displaystyle\left|(e^{-tH_{0}}a_{\alpha})(x)\right|\leq\int_{B_{\alpha}}k_{t}(x,y)\left|a_{\alpha}(y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\left|x-x_{\alpha}\right|^{-n-N}\left\lvert B_{\alpha}\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\rho(x_{\alpha})^{N}.

Since
B_{\alpha}
has radius
\rho(x_{\alpha})
, we obtain that
\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}a_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 2B_{\alpha})}\lesssim 1
, and this finishes the proof.
∎

We remark that Theorem
3.12
implies the following result.

Corollary 3.13
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\in(\frac{n}{2},\infty]
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and let
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}>0
. For all
p\in\left(\frac{n}{n+\delta/2},1\right]
, there is a continuous inclusion
\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subseteq\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for the respective
p
-quasinorms.

Proof.

Let
a\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be an atom for
\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
associated to a cube
Q\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see Section
2.1.6
). For an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
, it is clear that
a
is a
(p,\varepsilon)
-molecule for
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
, associated with
Q
. Consequently, we have a uniform bound of the form
\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim 1
. The conclusion follows from the fact that the atomic decomposition of any function
f\in\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
converges in the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
norm (as well as in the
\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
norm).
∎

3.1.6.
Square function characterisations

We shall need to interpolate between the Hardy spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, for
\frac{n}{n+1}<p<\infty
. This is made possible by the following square function characterisation.

Let us consider the auxiliary function
\psi_{0}(z):=ze^{-z}
. For
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we consider the square function
\mathcal{S}_{0}f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,\infty]
defined as

(\mathcal{S}_{0}f)(x):=(S_{\psi_{0},H_{0}}f)(x)=\left(\iint_{\Gamma(x)}{\left|(\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})f)(y)\right|}^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{n+1}}\right)^{1/2}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As usual, it follows from Fubini’s theorem and that of McIntosh that
\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}
. The following theorem gives a characterisation of the maximal Hardy space
\textup{H}_{V}^{p}
in terms of the square function operator
\mathcal{S}_{0}
.

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
n\geq 1
and let
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be non-negative. If
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},\infty)
and
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
if and only if
\mathcal{S}_{0}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover,
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
for all
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

We first recall that
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}=\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
with equivalent
p
-norms (Lemma
3.3
), and that
H_{0}
is a non-negative self-adjoint operator with a heat-semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH_{0}}\right\}_{t>0}
satisfying the Gaussian estimates (
3.4
).
The result for
p\in(1,\infty)
is stated and proved (in a more general form) in
[
37
, Theorem 1.1]
. Note that the lower bounds can be obtained from the upper bounds by duality (as in the proof of Lemma
6.5
below). The result is also mentioned in
[
57
, Section 1, p. 465]
, based on the earlier work of Auscher
[
6
]
.

Now assume that
\frac{n}{n+1}<p\leq 1
, and denote by
\mathbb{Q}_{0}:=\mathbb{Q}_{\psi_{0},H_{0}}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}((0,\infty),\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t};\mathrm{L}^{2})

and
\mathbb{C}_{0}:=\mathbb{C}_{\psi_{0},H_{0}}:\mathrm{L}^{2}((0,\infty),\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t};\mathrm{L}^{2})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
the (bounded) extension and contraction operators associated with
\psi_{0}
and
H_{0}
, as defined in Section
2.4.8
. Since
\psi^{*}_{0}=\psi_{0}
and
H_{0}
is self-adjoint, we know that
\mathbb{Q}_{0}^{*}=\mathbb{C}_{0}
(see Section
2.4.8
). Moreover, there is a
c>0
such that
f=c\mathbb{C}_{0}\mathbb{Q}_{0}f
for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Let
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}
be such that
\mathcal{S}_{0}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The definition of the square function
\mathcal{S}_{0}f
precisely means that the function
\mathbb{Q}_{0}f
, in addition to being in
\textup{T}^{2}
, belongs to the tent space
\textup{T}^{p}
, with

\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}=\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
.
The atomic decomposition in tent spaces (see Section
2.1.5
) implies that there exists a sequence of
\textup{T}^{p}
atoms
\left\{A_{k}\right\}_{k\geq 1}
and a sequence of scalars
\left\{\lambda_{k}\right\}_{k\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathbb{Q}_{0}f=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}A_{k}

with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
and in
\textup{T}^{2}
. Moreover, we have
\left(\sum_{k\geq 1}\left|\lambda_{k}\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\lesssim\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}
.
By boundedness of
\mathbb{C}_{0}
we get
f=c\mathbb{C}_{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{0}f)=c\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}\mathbb{C}_{0}(A_{k})
with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
. By (
3.6
), it therefore remains to prove that
\mathbb{C}_{0}(A_{k})\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
for all
k\geq 1
, with
\left\lVert\mathbb{C}_{0}(A_{k})\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim 1
uniformly for all
k
. Let
A
be an arbitrary
\textup{T}^{p}
-atom associated to a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
\alpha:=\mathbb{C}_{0}(A)
. We first estimate
\left\lVert\alpha\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
by duality. For arbitrary
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we can use the fact that
\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})
is a bounded self-adjoint operator on
\mathrm{L}^{2}
, the support property of
A
, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and McIntosh’s theorem to get

\displaystyle\left|\langle\alpha,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\ell(Q)}\left|\langle\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})(A(t,\cdot)),g\rangle\right|\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}=\int_{0}^{\ell(Q)}\left|\langle A(t,\cdot),\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})g\rangle\right|\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}}\left|A(t,x)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\lVert\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.

This implies that
\left\lVert\alpha\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}
. By Hölder’s inequality and
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-boundedness of the maximal operator
\mathcal{M}_{V}
, we obtain the local bound
\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}\alpha\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(4Q)}\lesssim 1
. To obtain the global bound, let us note that for fixed
s>0
and arbitrary
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
it holds that

\displaystyle\langle e^{-s^{2}H_{0}}\alpha,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\langle\mathbb{C}_{0}(A),e^{-s^{2}H_{0}}g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\langle\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})(A(t,\cdot))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t},e^{-s^{2}H_{0}}g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.

Now, for fixed
\varepsilon>0
, we can use the fact that the operators
\psi_{0}(s^{2}H_{0})=s^{2}H_{0}e^{-s^{2}H_{0}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
are integral operators for all
s>0
(see
[
31
, Proposition 4]
), with associated kernels
{\mathcal{Q}_{s}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}}
to write

\displaystyle\left|\langle\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})(A(t,\cdot))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t},e^{-s^{2}H_{0}}g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|=\left|\langle\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}e^{-s^{2}H_{0}}\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})(A(t,\cdot))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t},g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\langle\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}\frac{t^{2}}{t^{2}+s^{2}}\psi_{0}((s^{2}+t^{2})H_{0})(A(t,\cdot))\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t},g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\int_{0}^{\ell(Q)}\int_{Q}\frac{t^{2}}{t^{2}+s^{2}}\left|\mathcal{Q}_{(t^{2}+s^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}(x,y)\right|\left|A(t,y)\right|\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)\left|g(x)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

Since
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
can be chosen arbitrarily (and in particular can be any translation of a standard mollifier), this implies that for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\left(e^{-s^{2}H_{0}}\alpha\right)(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\ell(Q)}\int_{Q}\frac{t^{2}}{t^{2}+s^{2}}\left|\mathcal{Q}_{(t^{2}+s^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}(x,y)\right|\left|A(t,y)\right|\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{0}^{\ell(Q)}\int_{Q}\left|\frac{t^{2}}{t^{2}+s^{2}}\mathcal{Q}_{(t^{2}+s^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}(x,y)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{1/2},

where we have used the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the fact that
A
is a
\textup{T}^{p}
-atom.

Now let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4Q
and let
x_{Q}
be the centre of the cube
Q
. Then, for
y\in Q
it holds that
\left|x-y\right|\eqsim\left|x-x_{Q}\right|
. The integral kernel
\mathcal{Q}_{t}(x,y)
satisfies the following Gaussian bounds:

\left|\mathcal{Q}_{t}(x,y)\right|\lesssim t^{-n}e^{-\frac{\left|x-y\right|^{2}}{ct^{2}}}

for almost every
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t>0
(see
[
31
, Proposition 4]
). Consequently, for arbitrary
N>0
we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{t^{2}}{t^{2}+s^{2}}\mathcal{Q}_{(t^{2}+s^{2})^{1/2}}(x,y)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{4}(t^{2}+s^{2})^{-n-2}\exp\left(-\frac{2\left|x-y\right|^{2}}{c(t^{2}+s^{2})}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{4}(t^{2}+s^{2})^{N-n-2}\left|x-x_{Q}\right|^{-2N}\leq t^{2N-2n}\left|x-x_{Q}\right|^{-2N},

provided
N-n-2<0
. We can then estimate

\displaystyle\left|(e^{-s^{2}H_{0}}\alpha)(x)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\left|x-x_{Q}\right|^{-N}\left(\int_{0}^{\ell(Q)}\int_{Q}t^{2N-2n-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left|x-x_{Q}\right|^{-N}\ell(Q)^{N-n}=\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{-\frac{1}{p}}\left|x-x_{Q}\right|^{-N}\ell(Q)^{N},

provided that
N-n>0
. As
s>0
was arbitrary, we have
(\mathcal{M}_{V}\alpha)(x)\lesssim\left|x-x_{Q}\right|^{-N}\ell(Q)^{N-\frac{n}{p}}
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4Q
. If
n<Np
, then
\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4Q}\left|x-x_{Q}\right|^{-Np}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/p}\lesssim\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{p}-N}
. Consequently, we may take
N:=n+1
and obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}\alpha\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}\alpha\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(4Q)}+\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}\alpha\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus{4Q})}\lesssim 1.

This implies that
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
