\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim\left(\sum_{k\geq 1}\left|\lambda_{k}\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\lesssim\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
.

Let us now turn to the converse implication. Let
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We need to show that
\mathcal{S}_{0}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}
. It suffices to prove a uniform bound
\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim 1
for an arbitrary abstract atom
a
for
H_{0}
associated to a ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of radius
r_{B}>0
.
As usual, we use Hölder’s inequality and the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-boundedness of
\mathcal{S}_{0}
to obtain the local estimate
\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(4B)}\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\left\lvert 4B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim 1
.
To estimate the remaining global term
\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4B)}^{p}
, we will obtain a pointwise bound on
(\mathcal{S}_{0}a)(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4B
. We do so by writing
a=H_{0}^{m}b
for some
b\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{m})
with
m=\lfloor\frac{n}{2p}\rfloor+1
and
\textup{supp}\left(b\right)\subseteq B
. It follows that for all
t>0
it holds that

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})a=t^{2}H_{0}^{m+1}e^{-t^{2}H_{0}}b=t^{-2m}(t^{2}H_{0})^{m+1}e^{-t^{2}H_{0}}=:t^{-2m}P_{t}^{m}b,

where
P_{t}^{m}
is an integral operator with kernel
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{m}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the estimate

\left|\mathcal{P}_{t}^{m}(x,y)\right|\lesssim_{\gamma,m}t^{-n}\left(1+\frac{\left|x-y\right|}{t}\right)^{-\gamma}

for all
\gamma>0
and almost every
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see
[
25
, Theorem 2.3]
). Consequently, we can estimate

\displaystyle\left|(\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})a)(y)\right|\lesssim t^{-2m-n}\int_{B}\left(1+\frac{\left|y-z\right|}{t}\right)^{-\gamma}\left|b(z)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z.

We now treat two different cases, depending on the size of
t>0
. If
t>\frac{1}{2}\left|x-x_{B}\right|
, we crudely estimate

\displaystyle\left|(\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})a)(y)\right|\lesssim t^{-2m-n}\int_{B}\left|b\right|\leq\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}r_{B}^{2m}t^{-2m-n}.

If
t\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|x-x_{B}\right|
, then, since
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4B
and
\left|x-y\right|<t
, it holds that
\left|y-z\right|\geq\frac{1}{4}\left|x-x_{B}\right|
for all
z\in B
. Consequently we can estimate

\displaystyle\left|(\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})a)(y)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}r_{B}^{2m}t^{-2m-n}\left(1+\frac{\left|x-x_{B}\right|}{4t}\right)^{-\gamma}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}r_{B}^{2m}t^{\gamma-2m-n}\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-\gamma},

for all
\gamma>0
. We now split the range of integration at
t=\frac{1}{2}\left|x-x_{B}\right|
to obtain

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}_{0}a)(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}r_{B}^{2m}\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-\gamma}\left(\iint_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left|x-y\right|<t\\
0<t\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|x-x_{B}\right|\end{subarray}}t^{2\gamma-4m-2n}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{n+1}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}r_{B}^{2m}\left(\iint_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left|x-y\right|<t\\
t>\frac{1}{2}\left|x-x_{B}\right|\end{subarray}}t^{-4m-2n}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{n+1}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}r_{B}^{2m}\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-\gamma}\left(\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}\left|x-x_{B}\right|}t^{2\gamma-4m-2n-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}r_{B}^{2m}\left(\int_{\frac{1}{2}\left|x-x_{B}\right|}^{\infty}t^{-4m-2n-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}r_{B}^{2m}\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-2m-n},

provided we choose
\gamma>2m+n
to make the integral converge. Since
-p(2m+n)+n<0
by definition of
m
, we can integrate this to the power
p
to obtain
\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4B)}\lesssim 1
.
This shows that
\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim 1
for arbitrary abstract atoms
a
for
H_{0}
.

Now, for
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, there exists an
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-convergent atomic decomposition
f=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}a_{k}
, where
a_{k}
are abstract atoms associated with
H_{0}
and
\left\{\lambda_{k}\right\}_{k\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{C}
satisfies
\left(\sum_{k\geq 1}\left|\lambda_{k}\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}
. For each
n\geq 1
, let
f_{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lambda_{k}a_{k}
. Then, by subadditivity of
\mathcal{S}_{0}
and the fact that
p\leq 1
, we obtain
\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}f_{n}\right\rVert^{p}_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k}\right|^{p}\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}a_{k}\right\rVert^{p}_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\lambda_{k}\right|^{p}
.
This gives a uniform bound
\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}f_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}
for
n\geq 1
. Since
f_{n}\to f
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
, for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{\left|x-y\right|<t}\left|(\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})f)(y)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\left|x-y\right|<t}\left|(\psi_{0}(t^{2}H_{0})f_{n})(y)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

Applying Fatou’s lemma twice we obtain
\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\left\lVert\mathcal{S}_{0}f_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}^{p}
.
∎

Let us remark that the proof of Theorem
3.14
shows that, for
\frac{n}{n+1}<p<\infty
, the bounded operators
\mathbb{Q}_{0}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\textup{T}^{2}
and
\mathbb{C}_{0}:\textup{T}^{2}\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
are such that their restrictions
\mathbb{Q}_{0}|_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}}:\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\textup{T}^{p}\cap\textup{T}^{2}
and
\mathbb{C}_{0}|_{\textup{T}^{p}\cap\textup{T}^{2}}:\textup{T}^{p}\cap\textup{T}^{2}\to\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

are bounded for the respective
p
-quasinorms. To be precise, it holds that

(3.11)

\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}

for all
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and

(3.12)

\left\lVert\mathbb{C}_{0}F\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}

for all
F\in\textup{T}^{p}\cap\textup{T}^{2}
. Indeed, the two-sided estimate (
3.11
) is precisely the statement of Theorem
3.14
, and the estimate (
3.12
) for
\frac{n}{n+1}<p\leq 1
follows immediately from the estimate
\left\lVert\mathbb{C}_{0}(A)\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim 1
for all
\textup{T}^{p}
-atoms
A
, that was established in the proof of Theorem
3.14
.
The estimate (
3.12
) for
1<p<\infty
can be obtained by duality from (
3.11
), using that
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{*}=\mathbb{Q}_{0}
. Indeed, by
[
23
, Theorem 2]
, for
1<p<\infty
,
F\in\textup{T}^{p}\cap\textup{T}^{2}
and
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\langle\mathbb{C}_{0}F,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|=\left|\langle F,\mathbb{Q}_{0}g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}((0,\infty),\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t};\mathrm{L}^{2})}\right|\lesssim\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{0}g\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}}.

3.2.
Hardy–Sobolev spaces
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}

We introduce the class of homogeneous Hardy–Sobolev spaces adapted to the potential
V
that were mentioned in the introduction. We can work in the generality of
n\geq 1
and a non-negative
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

3.2.1.
Definition and properties

For
p\in(0,1]
and
n\geq 1
, we let
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be the space of all functions
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2})
such that
H_{0}^{1/2}f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The space
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is equipped with the quasinorm

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}:=\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}
(note that
H_{0}^{1/2}
is injective).

These spaces may not be complete. We will see in the next section that a completion can sometimes be realised as a subspace of some
\mathrm{L}^{p}
space. For this we require the following proposition.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},1]
, and
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with the estimate

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}.

Proof.

Let us observe that for
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},1]
it holds that
p^{*}\in(1,\infty)
.
We require the following observation.
For
1<r<\infty
, arbitrary
0<\mu<\pi
, and
\psi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}(S^{+}_{\mu})
, it holds that

(3.13)

\left\lVert\psi(H_{0})f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}\lesssim\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(S^{+}_{\mu})}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{r}
. This follows from
[
29
, Theorem 3.4]
. In fact, choose
\varphi\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
such that
\frac{\pi}{2}-\mu<\varphi<\frac{\pi}{2}.
Lemma
3.1
shows that
H_{0}
generates a holomorphic semigroup
\left\{e^{-zH_{0}}\right\}_{\operatorname{Re}{{z}}>0}
, which is uniformly bounded in the sector
S^{+}_{\varphi}
. Moreover, the heat-semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH_{0}}\right\}_{t>0}
has an integral kernel satisfying the Gaussian bounds (
3.4
). Finally,
H_{0}
has a bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}(S^{+}_{\mu})
-functional calculus on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The estimate (
3.13
) then follows from
[
29
, Theorem 3.4]
.

Let now
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathsf{R}(H_{0}^{1/2})
. Then we can use the atomic decomposition of Theorem
3.9
to write
f=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}a_{i}
with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
, where all
a_{i}
are abstract atoms for
H_{0}
, thus
a_{i}\in\mathsf{R}(H_{0}^{1/2})
for all
i\geq 1
. Note that
H_{0}^{-1/2}:\mathsf{R}(H_{0}^{1/2})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded from
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, as follows from a Sobolev embedding in dimension
n\geq 3
.
Since
f\in\mathsf{R}(H_{0}^{1/2})
and
f=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}a_{i}
with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
, this implies that

H_{0}^{-1/2}f=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}H_{0}^{-1/2}a_{i}
with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}}
.
We claim that
\left\lVert H_{0}^{-1/2}a_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\lesssim 1
, uniformly for all
i\geq 1.

Let
a\in\mathsf{R}(H_{0}^{1/2})
be an abstract atom associated to a ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of radius
r_{B}>0
. It follows that there is a function
b\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0})
with support in
B
and such that
a=H_{0}b
, with
\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\leq r_{B}^{2}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{-1/p}
. We can use the Calderón–McIntosh reproducing formula for the injective sectorial operator
H_{0}
and the function
\psi(z)=\sqrt{z}e^{-z}
to get that

H_{0}^{-1/2}a=c\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}e^{-tH_{0}}a\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}=c\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\int_{\varepsilon}^{r_{B}^{2}}e^{-tH_{0}}a\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}+\int_{r_{B}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}e^{-tH_{0}}H_{0}b\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}\right),

with convergence in the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
norm.
To estimate the first term, we use Minkowski’s inequality and uniform
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
-boundedness of the semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH_{0}}\right\}_{t>0}
to get

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{\varepsilon}^{r_{B}^{2}}e^{-tH_{0}}a\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\varepsilon}^{r_{B}^{2}}\left\lVert e^{-tH_{0}}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}\lesssim\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\int_{0}^{r_{B}^{2}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}=r_{B}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}

\displaystyle\leq r_{B}\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1/p^{*}}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1/n}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{-1/p+1/p^{*}}=1,

for all
\varepsilon>0
. Similarly, for the second term, we use that (
3.13
) implies uniform
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
-boundedness of the family
\left\{tH_{0}e^{-tH_{0}}\right\}_{t>0}
to get

\displaystyle\left\lVert\int_{r_{B}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}e^{-tH_{0}}H_{0}b\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{r_{B}^{2}}^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\left\lVert tH_{0}e^{-tH_{0}}b\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{3/2}}\lesssim\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\int_{r_{B}^{2}}^{\infty}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1/p^{*}}r_{B}^{-1}\leq r_{B}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{-1/p}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1/p^{*}}\eqsim 1.

Since the bound doesn’t depend on
\varepsilon>0
, it follows that
H_{0}^{-1/2}a\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
with
{\left\lVert H_{0}^{-1/2}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\lesssim 1}
.
Moreover, as
\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}_{i\geq 1}\in\ell^{p}
and
p\leq 1
, this implies that
\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}H_{0}^{-1/2}a_{i}\right\}_{N\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
. It follows that
H_{0}^{-1/2}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and that
H_{0}^{-1/2}f=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}H_{0}^{-1/2}a_{i}
with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
. Consequently,
\left\lVert H_{0}^{-1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\lesssim\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left|\lambda_{i}\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}
.
Since
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathsf{R}(H_{0}^{1/2})
was arbitrary, this concludes the proof.
∎

3.2.2.
Completion

As in Section
3.1.2
, we can use the continuous embedding
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
from Proposition
3.15
and
[
13
, Proposition 2.2]
(which equally applies to quasinormed spaces) to construct (for
n\geq 3
and
\frac{n}{n+1}<p\leq 1
) the completion
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Its existence follows from the following lemma.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},1]
. Let
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a Cauchy sequence in
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. If
f_{k}\to 0
in
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
, then
f_{k}\to 0
in
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

We know from (
3.13
) with
\psi(z)=\sqrt{z}e^{-z}
that
tH_{0}^{1/2}e^{-t^{2}H_{0}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2})
is actually
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
-bounded for each
t>0
. Consequently, for all
k\geq 1
and
t>0
it holds that

\displaystyle e^{-t^{2}H_{0}}\left(H_{0}^{1/2}f_{k}\right)=\lim_{m\to\infty}e^{-t^{2}H_{0}}\left(H_{0}^{1/2}f_{k}-H_{0}^{1/2}f_{m}\right),

with convergence in the
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
norm.
The conclusion then follows from the argument outlined in Section
3.1.2
.
∎

Again, for completeness, we spell out that
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
consists of all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for which there exists a Cauchy sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
in
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
that converges to
f
in
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and is equipped with the (compatibly defined) quasinorm
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\lVert f_{k}\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}
.

3.2.3.
Atomic decompositions

As for Hardy spaces, the easiest way to deal with Hardy–Sobolev spaces is through the use of a suitable atomic decomposition. We introduce the following definition; compare with
[
11
, Definition 8.30]
. We can work in the generality of non-negative
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
n\geq 1
.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},1]
. An
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-atom for
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
associated to a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a function
m\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2})=\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\textup{supp}\left(m\right)\subseteq Q
and
\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\leq\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}
.

The following result is the required atomic decomposition, to be compared with
[
11
, Proposition 8.31]
.

Theorem 3.18
.

Let
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},1]
and
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. There is a collection
\left\{m_{i}\right\}_{i\geq 1}
of
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-atoms for
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and complex numbers
\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}_{i\geq 1}\in\ell^{p}(\mathbb{N})
such that
\left\lVert\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}_{i\geq 1}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}
and

{H_{0}^{1/2}f=\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}H_{0}^{1/2}m_{i}}
with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
.

Proof.

Let us pick an even function
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\textup{supp}\left(\hat{\eta}\right)\subseteq(-c_{0},c_{0})
, where
c_{0}
is the constant from
[
44
, Lemma 3.5]
. Since
H_{0}
is a positive self-adjoint operator on
\mathrm{L}^{2}
satisfying Davies–Gaffney estimates,
[
44
, Lemma 3.5]
shows that for all
t>0
, the operator
\eta(tH_{0}^{1/2})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2})
has the finite propagation speed property

(3.14)

\textup{supp}\left(\eta(tH_{0}^{1/2})f\right)\subseteq\textup{supp}\left(f\right)+\overline{B(0,t)},

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
\varphi(z):=z\eta(z)
. Using the Borel functional calculus for the positive self-adjoint operator
H_{0}^{1/2}
(see Section
2.4.3
), we can define a bounded extension operator
{\mathbb{Q}^{\eta}:\mathrm{L}^{2}\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,\infty,\mathrm{L}^{2};\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t})\simeq\textup{T}^{2}}
, as in Section
2.4.8
, by
(\mathbb{Q}^{\eta}f)(t)=\varphi(tH_{0}^{1/2})f
for all
t\in(0,\infty)
. Indeed, the boundedness of the operator
\mathbb{Q}^{\eta}
follows from the spectral theorem, because if
E:\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
is the resolution of the identity (or spectral measure) on
(\mathbb{R},\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}))
for the self-adjoint operator
H_{0}^{1/2}
(see
[
53
, Theorem 13.30]
) then for
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\lVert(\mathbb{Q}^{\eta}f)(t)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|tx\eta(tx)\right|^{2}dE_{f,f}(x)\right)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|tx\eta(tx)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)dE_{f,f}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|s\eta(s)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}{s}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}}dE_{f,f}(x)\lesssim E_{f,f}(\mathbb{R})=\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

by changing variables (
s=tx
) and properties of
E
(see, e.g.,
[
53
, Theorem 13.24]
). The operator
\mathbb{Q}^{\eta}
then has a bounded adjoint
\mathbb{C}_{\eta}:=\left({\mathbb{Q}^{\eta}}\right)^{*}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,\infty,\mathrm{L}^{2};\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}
.
We use the Borel functional calculus, which is compatible with the holomorphic functional calculus for self-adjoint operators, as
\varphi
may not have a holomorphic extension to a sector with enough decay at infinity.

As in Section
2.4.8
, for all
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}
and
F\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(0,\infty,\mathrm{L}^{2};\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t})=\textup{T}^{2}
it holds that

(3.15)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbb{C}_{\eta}F,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\langle\int_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\varphi(tH_{0}^{1/2})F(t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t},g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.

Let us consider the auxiliary holomorphic function
\psi(z)=z^{2}e^{-z^{2}}
.
Since
H_{0}
is an injective self-adjoint operator, we can apply the Calderón–McIntosh reproducing formula from
[
13
, Proposition 4.2]
to obtain a
c>0
such that for all
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}
,

(3.16)

g=c\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}\varphi(tH_{0}^{1/2})\psi(tH_{0}^{1/2})g\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t},

with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
Note however that, since
\varphi
is odd, we have
c\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(t)\psi(t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}=1
, but
c\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(-t)\psi(-t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}=-1
. Inspection of the proof of
[
13
, Proposition 4.2]
reveals that the result still holds since
H_{0}
is non-negative.
As in the proof of Theorem
3.14
, we let
(\mathbb{Q}_{0}g)(t):=\psi(tH_{0}^{1/2})g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}
for all
t>0
. It follows from (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) that
c\mathbb{C}_{\eta}\mathbb{Q}_{0}=\textup{Id}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
.

We now have all the ingredients to start the proof. If
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
H_{0}^{1/2}f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
, then we have

H_{0}^{1/2}f=c\mathbb{C}_{\eta}\left(\mathbb{Q}_{0}\left(H_{0}^{1/2}f\right)\right)
.
Theorem
3.14
shows that
\mathbb{Q}_{0}\left(H_{0}^{1/2}f\right)\in\textup{T}^{p}\cap\textup{T}^{2}
, with
\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{0}\left(H_{0}^{1/2}f\right)\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}
. By the atomic decomposition in tent spaces, there is a decomposition
\mathbb{Q}_{0}\left(H_{0}^{1/2}f\right)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}A_{k}

with convergence in the
\textup{T}^{2}
norm, where the
A_{k}
are
\textup{T}^{p}
-atoms and
\left\lVert\left\{\lambda_{k}\right\}_{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}
. It follows from boundedness of
\mathbb{C}_{\eta}
that
H_{0}^{1/2}f=c\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda_{k}\mathbb{C}_{\eta}\left(A_{k}\right)

with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
. Let
A\in\textup{T}^{2}
be a
\textup{T}^{p}
-atom associated with a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We consider a function
m\in\mathrm{L}^{2}
defined as the following absolutely convergent Bochner integral in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
:

\displaystyle m:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\eta(tH_{0}^{1/2})A(t,\cdot)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\ell(Q)}\eta(tH_{0}^{1/2})A(t,\cdot)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.

We claim that
m\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2})
and that
H_{0}^{1/2}m=\mathbb{C}_{\eta}\left(A\right)
. Indeed, if
g\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2})
is arbitrary, and
\varepsilon>0
is fixed, then

\displaystyle\langle\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}\varphi(tH_{0}^{1/2})A(t,\cdot)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t},g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}H_{0}^{1/2}\eta(tH_{0}^{1/2})A(t,\cdot)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}\eta(tH_{0}^{1/2})A(t,\cdot)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t,H_{0}^{1/2}g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}},

where we have used Hille’s theorem (see
[
42
]
or
[
39
, Theorem A.37]
), followed by the self-adjointness of
H_{0}^{1/2}
in the last equality. Letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we obtain
\langle\mathbb{C}_{\eta}\left(A\right),g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\langle m,H_{0}^{1/2}g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
.
Since
H_{0}^{1/2}
is self-adjoint, this implies that
m\in\mathsf{D}(\left(H_{0}^{1/2}\right)^{*})=\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2})
, with
H_{0}^{1/2}m=\mathbb{C}_{\eta}(A)
. It follows that
\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\left\lVert\mathbb{C}_{\eta}(A)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{2}}\lesssim\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}.
Finally, the support property
\textup{supp}\left(m\right)\subseteq 2Q
follows from (
3.14
) because it implies that

\displaystyle\textup{supp}\left(\eta(tH_{0}^{1/2})A(t,\cdot)\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\textup{supp}\left(A(t,\cdot)\right)+B(0,t)\subseteq Q+B(0,\ell(Q))\subseteq 2Q

for all
t\in(0,\ell(Q))
. Applying this construction to
A:=A_{k}
for all
k\geq 1
and hiding all the multiplicative constants in the coefficients
\left\{\lambda_{k}\right\}_{k\geq 1}
concludes the proof.
∎

3.3.
Interpolation, duality and iteration of bounds for families of operators

In analogy with
[
11
, Definition 4.1]
, we introduce the following definition. Again, here we can work with the assumption of a non-negative
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
n\geq 1
.

Definition 3.19
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
and let
W_{1},W_{2}
be finite dimensional Hilbert spaces. Let
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
be a family of bounded operators mapping
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};W_{1})
into
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};W_{2})
. Let
a_{i}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{L}\left(W_{i}\right))
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, such that
a_{i}(x)
is invertible for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
a_{i}^{-1}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{L}\left(W_{i}\right))
.
Let
0<p\leq q\leq\infty
. The family
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
is said to be
a_{1}\textup{H}^{p}_{V}-a_{2}\textup{H}^{q}_{V}
-bounded if

(3.17)

\displaystyle\left\lVert a_{2}^{-1}T(z)\left(a_{1}f\right)\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{q}_{V}}\lesssim\left|z\right|^{\frac{n}{q}-\frac{n}{p}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}

for all
f\in\textup{H}_{V,\textup{pre}}^{p}
and all
z\in\Omega
.

We speak of
a\textup{H}_{V}^{p}
-boundedness when
a_{1}=a_{2}=a
and
p=q
.
Observe that when
p
and
q
are both larger than
1
, this definition coincides with the notion of
a_{1}\textup{H}^{p}-a_{2}\textup{H}^{q}
-boundedness of
[
11
, Definition 4.1]
, which is simply
\mathrm{L}^{p}-\mathrm{L}^{q}
-boundedness, by Lemma
3.3
. In (
3.17
) and in the rest of this paper, for any integer
N\geq 1
and
p\in(0,\infty)
, we let
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{N}):=(\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))^{N}
, with the associated (quasi)norm
\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}:=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left\lVert F_{k}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
for
F=(F_{1},\ldots,F_{N})\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

In the context of Definition
3.19
, the notion of
a_{1}\textup{H}^{p}_{V}-a_{2}\textup{H}^{q}_{V}
-boundedness interpolates in the expected way. In fact, we have the following lemma. Recall the notation
[p_{0},p_{1}]_{\theta}
introduced in Section
2.1.1
.

Lemma 3.20
.

If
\frac{n}{n+1}<p_{j}\leq q_{j}<\infty
,
j\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, and the family
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
is
a_{1}\textup{H}^{p_{j}}_{V}-a_{2}\textup{H}^{q_{j}}_{V}
-bounded for
j\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, then
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
is
a_{1}\textup{H}^{p_{\theta}}_{V}-a_{2}\textup{H}^{q_{\theta}}_{V}
-bounded for all
\theta\in[0,1]
, where
p_{\theta}=[p_{0},p_{1}]_{\theta}
and
q_{\theta}=[q_{0},q_{1}]_{\theta}
.

Proof.

We use the fact that the notion of
\textup{T}^{p}-\textup{T}^{q}
-boundedness interpolates. We also use the operators
\mathbb{C}_{0}:\textup{T}^{2}\to\mathrm{L}^{2}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{0}:\mathrm{L}^{2}\to\textup{T}^{2}
constructed above. In particular, we use their boundedness properties
\mathbb{Q}_{0}|_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}}\in\mathcal{L}(\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}},\textup{T}^{p})
and
\mathbb{C}_{0}|_{\textup{T}^{p}\cap\textup{T}^{2}}\in\mathcal{L}(\textup{T}^{p},\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}})
for all
\frac{n}{n+1}<p<\infty
and the identity
c\mathbb{C}_{0}\mathbb{Q}_{0}=\textup{Id}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
for some
c>0
. To be more precise, the family
\left\{\mathbb{Q}_{0}a_{2}^{-1}T(z)a_{1}\mathbb{C}_{0}\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
is both
\textup{T}^{p_{0}}-\textup{T}^{q_{0}}
-bounded and
\textup{T}^{p_{1}}-\textup{T}^{q_{1}}
-bounded. By complex interpolation in tent spaces (see
[
23
, Proposition 6]
), it follows that the family is
\textup{T}^{p_{\theta}}-\textup{T}^{q_{\theta}}
-bounded. Consequently, for
f\in\textup{H}^{p_{\theta}}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we have
\mathbb{Q}_{0}f\in\textup{T}^{p_{\theta}}\cap\textup{T}^{2}
and we can estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert a_{2}^{-1}T(z)a_{1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{q_{\theta}}}

\displaystyle\eqsim\left\lVert\mathbb{C}_{0}\mathbb{Q}_{0}a_{2}^{-1}T(z)a_{1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{q_{\theta}}}\lesssim\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{0}a_{2}^{-1}T(z)a_{1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{q_{\theta}}}

\displaystyle\eqsim\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{0}a_{2}^{-1}T(z)a_{1}\mathbb{C}_{0}\mathbb{Q}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{q_{\theta}}}\lesssim\left|z\right|^{\frac{n}{q_{\theta}}-\frac{n}{p_{\theta}}}\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p_{\theta}}}\lesssim\left|z\right|^{\frac{n}{q_{\theta}}-\frac{n}{p_{\theta}}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p_{\theta}}}.\qed

We have the following substitute for
[
11
, Lemma 4.4]
for families of operators on the Hardy spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
be a family of operators as in Definition
3.19
, with
a_{1}=a_{2}=a
and
W_{1}=W_{2}
. Let
p,\varrho\in(0,2)
such that
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
is
a\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-bounded and
a\textup{H}^{\varrho}_{V}-\mathrm{L}^{2}
-bounded. Then, for each
q\in(p,2)
, there exists an integer
\beta\geq 1
(depending on
p
,
q
and
\varrho
) such that
\left\{T(z)^{\beta}\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
is
a\textup{H}^{q}_{V}-\mathrm{L}^{2}
-bounded.

Proof.

We follow
[
11
, Lemma 4.4]
verbatim, using that the notion of
a\textup{H}^{q}_{V}
-boundedness interpolates in the expected way (see Lemma
3.20
).
∎

In the same vein, the notion of
a_{1}\textup{H}^{p}_{V}-a_{2}\textup{H}^{q}_{V}
-boundedness dualises in the expected way. The duals of the Hardy spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
can be identified with a class of spaces that we introduce next.

3.3.1.
Campanato spaces
\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}
and duality

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\frac{n}{2}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For
\alpha\in[0,1]
, the Campanato space
\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as the set of all functions
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for which there exists
C\geq 0
such that

(3.18)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|f-f_{B}\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq C\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{\alpha}{n}}

for all balls
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and

(3.19)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|f\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq C\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{\alpha}{n}}

for all balls
B=B(x_{B},r_{B})
such that
r_{B}\geq\rho(x_{B})
. For such a function
f
, we let

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}}:=\inf\left\{C\geq 0:(\ref{Campanato space condition 1})\textup{ and }(\ref{Campanato space condition 2})\textup{ hold}\right\}.

It is clear that
\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a linear subspace of
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and that
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}}
is a norm on
\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
when
V\not\equiv 0
(so there is at least one ball
B(x_{B},r_{B})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
r_{B}\geq\rho(x_{B})
).

We shall need the following alternative characterisation of the spaces
\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
\alpha\in(0,1]
.
We let
\Lambda^{\alpha}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be the space of
\alpha
-Hölder continuous functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
for which
\left|f(x)\right|\lesssim\rho(x)^{\alpha}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. It is equipped with the norm

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\Lambda^{\alpha}_{V}}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|f(x)-f(y)\right|}{\left|x-y\right|^{\alpha}}+\left\lVert\rho^{-\alpha}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}.

When
\alpha\in(0,1]
, it follows from
[
18
, Proposition 4]
that
\Lambda^{\alpha}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with equivalent norms. Accordingly, we define
\Lambda^{0}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\textup{BMO}_{V}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the corresponding norm.

The duality property
\left(\textup{H}^{p}_{V}\right)^{*}=\textup{BMO}_{V}^{n\left(\frac{1}{p}-1\right)}
is well known, and follows from the atomic decomposition of the Hardy spaces
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
from Section
3.1.4
, as is shown in
[
62
, Theorem 2.1]
. As we work with subspaces of
\mathrm{L}^{2}
, we state an
\mathrm{L}^{2}
version, which is proved similarly.

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q>\frac{n}{2}
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\delta\wedge 1},1]
and
\alpha:=n\left(\frac{1}{p}-1\right)
. If
f\in\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
and
g\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\left|\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
.
Conversely, if
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and there exists
C\geq 0
such that
\left|\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|\leq C\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
for all
g\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
f\in\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}
with
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{BMO}_{V}^{\alpha}}\leq C
.

We next introduce the following useful notion. Let
0<p<\infty
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
. In the context of Definition
3.19
, we shall refer to a family
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
being
a_{1}\textup{H}^{p}_{V}-a_{2}\Lambda^{\alpha}_{V}
-bounded if

\displaystyle\left\lVert a_{2}^{-1}T(z)\left(a_{1}f\right)\right\rVert_{\Lambda^{\alpha}_{V}}\lesssim\left|z\right|^{-\alpha-\frac{n}{p}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}

for all
f\in\textup{H}_{V,\textup{pre}}^{p}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and all
z\in\Omega
. We can now state the duality result we had in mind. This is the analogue of
[
11
, Lemma 4.3]
in our context.

Lemma 3.23
.

For
p,r\in[1,\infty]
, a family
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
is
\mathrm{L}^{p}-\mathrm{L}^{r}
-bounded if and only if the dual family
\left\{T(z)^{*}\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
is
\mathrm{L}^{r^{\prime}}-\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}
-bounded.
Moreover, if
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\frac{n}{2}
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}
, then for
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\delta\wedge 1},1]
,
r\in[1,\infty]
and a
a_{1}\textup{H}^{p}_{V}-\mathrm{L}^{r}
-bounded family
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
, the dual family
\left\{T(z)^{*}\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
is
\mathrm{L}^{r^{\prime}}-{a_{1}^{*}}^{-1}\Lambda_{V}^{n\left(\frac{1}{p}-1\right)}
-bounded.

Proof.

The first part is elementary. The second part follows from Proposition
3.22
.
∎

4.
Critical numbers
p_{\pm}(H)
,
q_{\pm}(H)
and their properties

Let us recall that the bounded accretive function
b
is defined as
b(x):=A_{\perp\perp}(x)^{-1}
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We will work with
n\geq 1
and the general assumption that
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is non-negative. We shall consider the sets
\mathcal{J}(H)
and
\mathcal{N}(H)
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(H)

\displaystyle:=\left\{p\in\left(\frac{n}{n+1},\infty\right)\;:\;\left\{\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}\textup{ is }b\textup{H}^{p}_{V}\textup{-bounded}\right\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(H)

\displaystyle:=\left\{p\in\left(\frac{n}{n+1},\infty\right)\;:\;\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}\textup{ is }b\textup{H}^{p}_{V}-\textup{H}^{p}_{V}\textup{-bounded}\right\}.

These sets are intervals containing
p=2
with non-empty interior (see Section
2.5.4
, Lemma
3.20
and Corollary
4.5
). The endpoints of
\mathcal{J}(H)
will be denoted by
p_{\pm}(H)
, and those of
\mathcal{N}(H)
will be denoted by
q_{\pm}(H)
, analogous to
[
11
, Definition 6.1]
. These four numbers will be called the critical numbers.

4.1.
Resolvents bounds

In this subsection, we establish some important properties satisfied by the critical numbers.
We start by mentioning the following lemma for later reference. Its proof is identical to that of
[
11
, Lemma 7.2]
.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
p\in\mathcal{J}(H)\cap(1,\infty)
and
k\geq 1
, then the space
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathsf{D}(H^{k})\cap\mathsf{R}(H^{k})
is dense in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

In contrast with
[
11
, Theorem 6.2]
, it is not clear wether
p_{-}(H)=q_{-}(H)
when
V\not\equiv 0
. Nevertheless, Proposition
4.4
will be enough for our purposes. The following two lemmas will be needed for its proof. The first lemma is adapted from
[
11
, Lemma 6.3]
and follows here by observing that the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-boundedness of the family
\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
implies the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-boundedness of
\left\{t\nabla_{x}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

If
n\geq 3
, then
\left(2_{*},2^{*}\right)\subset\mathcal{J}(H)
. Moreover,
\left\{\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounded and
\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}-\mathrm{L}^{2}
-bounded for every
p\in[2,2^{*}]
.

The second lemma is adapted from
[
11
, Lemma 6.5]
, and is proved in exactly the same way.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\frac{n}{n+1}<p\leq r<\infty
such that
r>1
and
\frac{n}{p}-\frac{n}{r}<1
. If there is an integer
\beta\geq 1
such that the family
\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-\beta-1}\right\}_{t>0}
is
b\textup{H}^{p}_{V}-\mathrm{L}^{r}
-bounded, then
\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-\alpha}\right\}_{t>0}
is also
b\textup{H}^{p}_{V}-\mathrm{L}^{r}
-bounded for all integers
1\leq\alpha\leq\beta
.

We can now state and prove the main result of this section. We follow the argument used in the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 6.2]
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
n\geq 3
. The critical numbers satisfy

\displaystyle p_{-}(H)\vee 1=q_{-}(H)\vee 1\hskip 28.45274pt\textup{ and }\hskip 28.45274ptp_{+}(H)\geq q_{+}(H)^{*}.

Proof.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{N}(H)\cap(1,\infty)
. We treat two cases:

Case 1.

Suppose that
\rho<n
. This implies that the family
\left\{t\nabla_{x}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{\rho}
-bounded. As a consequence, a Sobolev embedding shows that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\rho^{*}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{x}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\rho}}\lesssim t^{-1}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\rho}}=t^{\frac{n}{\rho^{*}}-\frac{n}{\rho}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\rho}}\

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{\rho}
and all
t>0
. This means that the family
\left\{\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{\rho}-\mathrm{L}^{\rho^{*}}
-bounded. Since it also satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates of exponential order,
[
11
, Lemma 4.14]
shows that it satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{[\rho,2]_{\theta}}-\mathrm{L}^{[\rho^{*},2]_{\theta}}
off-diagonal estimates of exponential order, for all
\theta\in(0,1)
. Then,
[
11
, Lemma 4.7]
implies that
\left\{\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{[\rho,2]_{\theta}}
-bounded and
\mathrm{L}^{[\rho^{*},2]_{\theta}}
-bounded for all
\theta\in(0,1)
. This means that
[\rho,2]_{\theta},[\rho^{*},2]_{\theta}\in\mathcal{J}(H)
for all
\theta\in(0,1)
, which implies that
p_{-}(H)\leq\rho\leq\rho^{*}\leq p_{+}(H).

Case 2.

Suppose that
\rho\geq n
. Then, since
\mathcal{N}(H)
is an interval containing
2
, it follows that
[2,n]\subset\mathcal{N}(H)
. We may therefore apply Case 1 to any
\tilde{\rho}\in\mathcal{N}(H)\cap[2,n)
in place of
\rho
, to obtain that
\tilde{\rho}^{*}\leq p_{+}(H)
for all
\tilde{\rho}\in[2,n)
. Since
\tilde{\rho}^{*}\to\infty
as
\tilde{\rho}\to n
, we have
p_{+}(H)=\infty=\rho^{*}
, since
\rho\geq n
.
Moreover,
\rho\geq n>2\geq p_{-}(H)
, hence
p_{-}(H)\leq\rho\leq\rho^{*}\leq p_{+}(H)
.

Altogether, Case 1 and 2 show that

(4.1)

p_{-}(H)\leq\rho\leq\rho^{*}\leq p_{+}(H)

for every
\rho\in\mathcal{N}(H)\cap(1,\infty)
.
The last inequality in (
4.1
) shows that
q_{+}(H)^{*}\leq p_{+}(H).

In addition, the first inequality in (
4.1
) shows that
p_{-}(H)\leq q_{-}(H)\vee 1
.

Now let
p\in\mathcal{J}(H)\cap(1,2)
. Lemma
4.2
shows that
\left\{\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{\rho}-\mathrm{L}^{2}
-bounded for some
\rho\in(1,2)
(we may take
\rho=2_{*}
since
n\geq 3
). We can therefore apply Proposition
3.21
to find, for each
q\in(p,2)
, an integer
\beta\geq 1
such that
\left\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-\beta}\right\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{q}-\mathrm{L}^{2}
-bounded. By composition with the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-bounded family
\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
, we obtain
\mathrm{L}^{q}-\mathrm{L}^{2}
-boundedness of the family
\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-\beta-1}\right\}_{t>0}
. The later family also satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates of exponential order (see
[
11
, Lemma 4.6]
). Therefore,
[
11
, Lemma 4.14]
shows that it has
\mathrm{L}^{[q,2]_{\theta}}-\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates of exponential order for every
\theta\in(0,1)
. Consequently,
[
11
, Lemma 4.7]
shows that
\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-\beta-1}\right\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{[q,2]_{\theta}}
-bounded for each
\theta\in(0,1)
. We may finally apply Lemma
4.3
to deduce that
[q,2]_{\theta}\in\mathcal{N}(H)
for all
\theta\in(0,1)
. Since
q\in\left(p,2\right)
was arbitrary, this implies that
(p,2]\subset\mathcal{N}(H)
, and therefore
q_{-}(H)\leq p
. The later inequality also trivially holds for all
p\in[2,\infty)
, thus
q_{-}(H)\leq p
for all
p\in\mathcal{J}(H)\cap(1,\infty)
, hence
q_{-}(H)\leq p_{-}(H)\vee 1
. Altogether, this shows that
q_{-}(H)\vee 1=p_{-}(H)\vee 1
.

∎

Following the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 6.7]
, we can use the previous result along with
[
51
, Lemma 5.5]
in order to improve on the conclusions of Lemma
4.2
. This yields the following result, which provides a worst-case estimate for the critical numbers.

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q>1
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
q_{+}(H)\geq 2+\varepsilon
and
\left[2_{*}-\varepsilon,2^{*}+\varepsilon\right]\subseteq\mathcal{J}(H)
.

The critical numbers can be effectively estimated in some simple cases that we list in the following remark.

Remark 4.6
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\frac{n}{2}
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and let
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}
. It follows from
[
21
, Theorem 1.5]
and the estimate (
3.2
) that
p_{-}(H_{0})\leq\frac{n}{n+1\wedge\delta}
and that
p_{+}(H_{0})=\infty
. In addition, it follows from Lemma
5.3
below and Theorems 0.5 and 5.10 in
[
55
]
that
q_{+}(H_{0})\geq 2q
.

More generally, if
n\geq 1
,
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is non-negative,
A_{\perp\perp}=a=1
and
A_{\parallel\parallel}
is real, symmetric, bounded and strongly elliptic, then
H
generates a heat semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH}\right\}_{t>0}
satisfying Gaussian bounds. Indeed, the argument of the proof of Lemma
3.1
shows that
\left\{e^{-tH}\right\}_{t>0}
is dominated by
\left\{e^{-tL}\right\}_{t>0}
, where
L:=-\textup{div}_{x}(A_{\parallel\parallel}\nabla_{x})
. The claim then follows from
[
24
, Corollary 3.2.8]
. It follows that
p_{-}(H)\leq 1
and
p_{+}(H)=\infty
.

4.2.
The set
\mathcal{I}(V)
and reverse Riesz transform bounds on
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}

In order to identify the adapted Hardy spaces
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
for
p\leq 1
, more specifically for Proposition
6.8
below, we shall need an additional set of exponents,
\mathcal{I}(V)
, defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(V):=\left\{p\in\left(\frac{n}{n+1},1\right]:\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\gtrsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}\textup{ for all }f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right\}.

We will see in Lemma
4.7
that an exponent
p
belongs to
\mathcal{I}(V)
provided a reverse Riesz transform bound holds on the Hardy space
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(recall that
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}:=\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
).

We now let
n\geq 1
and
V
satisfy either (i), (ii) or (iii) of Proposition
2.9
for
p=2
, so in particular
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is dense in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Recall that
H_{0}=-\Delta+V
is the self-adjoint operator on
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
defined at the start of Section
3
. By Kato’s second representation theorem (see, e.g.,
[
52
, Theorem 8.1]
), we have
\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2})=\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
for all
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
By density, the operator
H_{0}^{1/2}
extends to an isomorphism
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-8.0mu1/2}:\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

(4.2)

\left\lVert\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-8.0mu1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

for all
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Indeed, (
4.2
) shows that
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-8.0mu1/2}
is injective and has closed range. By functional calculus, we have
\mathsf{R}(H_{0})\subseteq\mathsf{R}(H_{0}^{1/2})\subseteq\mathsf{R}(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-8.0mu1/2})\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since
H_{0}
is self-adjoint, we have
\overline{\mathsf{R}(H_{0})}=\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. It follows that
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-8.0mu1/2}
is surjective, whence an isomorphism.
The inverse of this isomorphism shall be denoted
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

The Riesz transform
\mathcal{R}_{0}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})
is the bounded operator
\mathcal{R}_{0}:=\!\!\nabla_{\mu}(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-8.0mu1/2})^{-1}
. Let us note that
\mathcal{R}_{0}=\nabla_{\mu}H_{0}^{-1/2}
on
\mathsf{R}(H_{0}^{1/2})
. It follows from (
4.2
) that
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is an isometry, i.e. it satisfies
\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{0}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}
for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
By the polarisation identity, it follows that the adjoint of
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is a bounded operator
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{R}_{0}=\textup{Id}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
.
We have the following elementary observation.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
p\in\left(\frac{n}{n+1},1\right]
. The exponent
p
belongs to
\mathcal{I}(V)
if and only if
\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
for all
g\in(\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}})^{n+1}\cap\mathsf{R}(\nabla_{\mu})
.

Proof.

Let
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},1]
be such that
\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
for all
g\in(\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}})^{n+1}\cap\mathsf{R}(\nabla_{\mu})
. Then, if
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is such that
\nabla_{\mu}f\in(\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}})^{n+1}
, we note that
\mathcal{R}_{0}(H_{0}^{1/2}f)=\nabla_{\mu}f
, and therefore

\displaystyle\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}=\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{R}_{0}(H_{0}^{1/2}f)\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{0}(H_{0}^{1/2}f)\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}=\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}.

Conversely, if
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)
, then for
g\in(\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}})^{n+1}\cap\mathsf{R}(\nabla_{\mu})
we write
g=\nabla_{\mu}f
for
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}=\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}(\nabla_{\mu}f)\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}=\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}\mathcal{R}_{0}(H_{0}^{1/2}f)\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}=\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}=\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}.\hskip 28.45274pt\qed

The following remarks indicate the importance of the set
\mathcal{I}(V)
.

Remark 4.8
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q>\frac{n}{2}
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}
. It follows from
[
21
, Theorems 1.6 and 1.7]
and Corollary
3.13
that the Riesz transform
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-bounded for all
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\frac{\delta}{2}},1]
. So, if
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)\cap(\frac{n}{n+\frac{\delta}{2}},1]
, there is an equivalence
\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}

for all
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Remark 4.9
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be non-negative. If
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\nabla_{\mu}f\in\left(\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}\right)^{n+1}
for some
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)
, then the atomic decomposition of Theorem
3.18
can be applied to obtain some coefficients
\left\{\lambda_{k}\right\}_{k}\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
\nabla_{\mu}f=\sum_{k}\lambda_{k}\nabla_{\mu}m_{k}
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
, where the
\left\{m_{k}\right\}_{k}
are
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-atoms for
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in the sense of Definition
3.17
, and
\left\lVert\left\{\lambda_{k}\right\}_{k}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
.

The main result of this section is the following boundedness criterion for
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
. The class
S^{\alpha}
is a “smoothness” class for potentials
V\in\textup{RH}^{\frac{n}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
which is introduced at the start of Section
4.2.2
below, and shown to contain a non trivial class of potentials
V
in Section
4.2.3
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q>n
,
\alpha\in(0,1]
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap S^{\alpha}
. The adjoint
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}
is
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-bounded for all
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\alpha\wedge\sigma},1]
, where
\sigma=1-\frac{n}{q}>0
. It follows that
\left(\frac{n}{n+\alpha\wedge\sigma},1\right]\subseteq\mathcal{I}(V)
.

This theorem will be proved in two independent steps, namely Propositions
4.11
and
4.12
below, combined with Lemma
4.7
to obtain the conclusion. The Riesz transform
\mathcal{R}_{0}
can be decomposed into its components as
\mathcal{R}_{0}f:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{R}_{\parallel}f\\
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}f\end{bmatrix}
where the operators
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
are both bounded. We can also further decompose the operator
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel}
as
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel}=\left(\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}\right)_{k=1}^{n}
.
This implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}^{*}\begin{bmatrix}f\\
g\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{\parallel}^{*}f+\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}g=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}^{*}f_{k}+\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}g

for all
f=\left(f_{k}\right)_{k=1}^{n}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},1]
, the boundedness of the adjoint
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel}^{*}
on the Hardy space
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
is investigated in Section
4.2.1
, while that of
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}
is considered in Section
4.2.2
.

4.2.1.
Boundedness of
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel}^{*}
on
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}

In this section, we let
n\geq 3
,
q>n
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. It follows from
[
55
, Theorem 0.8]
that the operators
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}
, and therefore
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}^{*}
, are Calderón–Zygmund operators. This means that there are locally integrable kernels
K_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}((\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})\setminus\left\{(x,y):x=y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\right\})
such that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}f)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{k}(x,y)f(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\hskip 28.45274pt\textup{ for a.e. }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\textup{supp}\left(f\right)

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The same representation also holds for the adjoints
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}^{*}
with the associated kernel
K_{k}^{*}(x,y):=K_{k}(y,x)
. The kernels satisfy the usual Calderón–Zygmund estimates, meaning that there are
C\geq 0
and
\sigma>0
such that

(4.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\left|K_{k}(x,y)\right|\lesssim{\left|x-y\right|^{n}},\\
\left|K_{k}(x+h,y)-K_{k}(x,y)\right|\lesssim\frac{\left|h\right|^{\sigma}}{\left|x-y\right|^{n+\sigma}},\\
\left|K_{k}(x,y+h)-K_{k}(x,y)\right|\lesssim\frac{\left|h\right|^{\sigma}}{\left|x-y\right|^{n+\sigma}},\end{array}\right.

for all
x,y,h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
x\neq y
and
\left|h\right|<\frac{\left|x-y\right|}{2}
, where
\sigma=1-\frac{n}{q}>0
.
To prove
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-boundedness of
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}^{*}
, we shall apply the boundedness criterion
[
21
, Theorem 1.2]
. This criterion is concerned with generalised
H_{0}
-adapted Calderón–Zygmund operators, defined in
[
21
, Definition 1.1]
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q>n
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and let
\sigma=1-\frac{n}{q}>0
. Then
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}^{*}
,
k\in\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
, are
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-bounded for
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\sigma},1]
.

Proof.

Let
k\in\left\{1,\cdots,n\right\}
be fixed. We claim that
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}^{*}\in GCZO_{\rho}(\sigma,2)
in the sense of
[
21
, Definition 1.1]
. We first check that condition (i) of
[
21
, Definition 1.1]
is satisfied. We have that for each
N>0
, there exists
C_{N}>0
such that

(4.4)

\displaystyle\left|K_{k}(x,y)\right|\leq C_{N}(1+\left|x-y\right|m(x))^{-N}\left|x-y\right|^{-n}

for every
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
x\neq y
(see the proof of
[
55
, Theorem 0.8]
).

Using (
4.4
), we get that for
N>0
,
x_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
y\in B(x_{B},\rho(x_{B}))
and
R>2\rho(x_{B})
, we have
\left|K_{k}^{*}(x,y)\right|^{2}\lesssim\left|x-y\right|^{-2n}\left(1+\frac{\left|x-y\right|}{\rho(y)}\right)^{-2N}
.
Note that for
\left|x-x_{B}\right|>R
and
y\in B(x_{B},\rho(x_{B}))
we have
\left|x_{B}-y\right|<\frac{\left|x-x_{B}\right|}{2}
and therefore
\left|x-y\right|\geq\frac{\left|x-x_{B}\right|}{2}>R/2
. Since also
\rho(y)\eqsim\rho(x_{B})
we get
\left(1+\frac{\left|x-y\right|}{\rho(y)}\right)^{-2N}\lesssim\left(1+\frac{R}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{-2N}
.
We therefore obtain

\displaystyle\left(\int_{R<\left|x-x_{B}\right|<2R}\left|K_{k}^{*}(x,y)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{R}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{-N}\left(\int_{R<\left|x-x_{B}\right|<2R}\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-2n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\rho(x_{B})}{R}\right)^{N}R^{-\frac{n}{2}},

which shows that condition (i) of
[
21
, Definition 1.1]
is satisfied. Next, we check that
K_{k}^{*}
satisfies condition (ii) in
[
21
, Definition 1.1]
. If
B=B(x_{B},r_{B})
,
y\in B
and
j\geq 1
is an integer, then we note that for
\left|x-x_{B}\right|>2^{j}r_{B}\geq 2r_{B}
we have
\frac{\left|x-x_{B}\right|}{2}>r_{B}>\left|x_{B}-y\right|
. We can therefore use (
4.3
) to get

\displaystyle\left(\int_{2^{j}r_{B}<\left|x-x_{B}\right|<2^{j+1}r_{B}}\left|K^{*}_{k}(x,y)-K^{*}_{k}(x,x_{B})\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{2^{j}r_{B}<\left|x-x_{B}\right|<2^{j+1}r_{B}}\left(\frac{\left|y-x_{B}\right|^{\sigma}}{\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{n+\sigma}}\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\frac{r_{B}^{\sigma}}{(2^{j}r_{B})^{n+\sigma}}(2^{j}r_{B})^{n/2}\lesssim 2^{-j\sigma}\left\lvert 2^{j}B\right\rvert^{-1/2},

as needed. These estimates show that
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}^{*}\in GCZO_{\rho}(\sigma,2)
. The proof of
[
47
, Proposition 4.12]
shows that
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}1
satisfies the criterion of
[
21
, Theorem 1.2]
(the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}1
is made precise therein).
We conclude that for every
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-atom
a\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
it holds that
\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}^{*}a\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim 1
.
It follows from the atomic decompositions of Theorems
3.7
and
3.9
that
\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\parallel,k}^{*}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
for all
f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
∎

4.2.2.
Boundedness of
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}
on
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}

We now turn to the
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-boundedness of the adjoint
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}
for
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},1]
. We let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\frac{n}{2}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We introduce a “smoothness” class for these potentials
V
. For a parameter
\alpha\in(0,1]
, we say that
V\in S^{\alpha}
if

(4.5)

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|V^{1/2}(x)-(V^{1/2})_{B}\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\lesssim r_{B}^{\alpha}m(x_{B},V)^{1+\alpha}

for all balls
B=B(x_{B},r_{B})
such that
r_{B}\leq\rho(x_{B},V)
.
We will exhibit some potentials that lie in
S^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in(0,1]
in Section
4.2.3
below.

We obtain the following result, which is similar to
[
21
, Theorem 1.8]
for
s=\frac{1}{2}
, although the range of indices
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\alpha\wedge\sigma},1]
obtained here is somewhat different. This is because we avoid their approach based on
[
21
, Lemma 5.5]
, which we could not get to work.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q>n
,
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap S^{\alpha}
and let
\sigma:=1-\frac{n}{q}
. Then
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}
is
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-bounded for all
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\alpha\wedge\sigma},1]
.

Proof.

Since the interval
(\frac{n}{n+\alpha\wedge\sigma},1]
is open at the left endpoint, we note that the case
q=\infty
follows from the case
q<\infty
. We can therefore assume that
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}<2
.
Let
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\alpha\wedge\sigma},1]
. Since
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\delta/2},1]
, we can choose
\eta_{0},\gamma\in(0,1)
sufficiently close to
1
so that
p>\frac{n}{n+\frac{\delta}{2}+(\eta_{0}\gamma-1)}
. Let
a
be an
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-atom for
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
associated to a ball
B=B(x_{B},r_{B})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
r_{B}\leq\rho(x_{B})
. We have
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}a=\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}(V^{1/2}a)
. It follows from
[
47
, Section 4.7]
that
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}
is an integral operator with a symmetric kernel
K\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that for all
0<\eta<\delta\wedge 1=1
and
N>0
there is a
C_{N}\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\left|K(x,y)\right|\leq\frac{C_{N}}{\left|x-y\right|^{n-1}}\left(1+\frac{\left|x-y\right|}{\rho(x)}+\frac{\left|x-y\right|}{\rho(y)}\right)^{-N}\\
\left|K(x,y)-K(x,z)\right|\leq C\frac{\left|y-z\right|^{\eta}}{\left|x-z\right|^{n-1+\eta}}\end{array}\right.

for all
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
x\neq y
and
\left|x-y\right|>2\left|y-z\right|
. (Note it seems that
0<\delta<2-\frac{n}{q}
should be
0<\delta<(2-\frac{n}{q})\wedge 1
at the top of page 588 in
[
47
]
.) These estimates show that
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}
is a
1
-Schrödinger–Calderón–Zygmund operator in the sense of
[
47
, Definition 3.1]
. In particular, this allows us to define
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}(1)\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the property that for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it holds that
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and

(4.8)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}f=\langle f,\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.

We claim that there is
\beta>0
such that
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}V^{1/2}a
is a
(p,2,\beta,\alpha\wedge\sigma)
-molecule for
H_{0}
associated to the ball
B
in the sense of
[
21
, Section 3.2]
(we shall only require
r_{B}\leq\rho(x_{B})
instead of
r_{B}\leq\frac{1}{2}\rho(x_{B})
). Note that this is slightly different from the molecules introduced in Definition
3.11
, because it requires a size condition on the ball associated with the molecule. To this aim, we split

(4.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}V^{1/2}a&=\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}(V^{1/2}-(V^{1/2})_{B})a+(V^{1/2})_{B}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a\\
&=\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}(\eta_{V}a)+(V^{1/2})_{B}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a=:\textup{I}+\textup{II},\end{split}

where we defined
\eta_{V}:=V^{1/2}-(V^{1/2})_{B}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We note that the assumption
V\in S^{\alpha}
implies that
\left\lVert\eta_{V}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B)}\leq r_{B}^{\alpha}m(x_{B})^{1+\alpha}\left\lvert B\right\rvert
.
Since
r_{B}\leq\rho(x_{B})
, it follows from Hölder’s inequality and (
2.5
) that
\left(V^{1/2}\right)_{B}\leq\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{1/2}\lesssim r_{B}^{-1}\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\delta/2}
.
We shall prove that both terms
I
and
II
in (
4.9
) are
(p,2,\beta,\alpha\wedge\sigma)
-molecules for
H_{0}
associated to the ball
B
.

We first check the molecular decay (condition (b) in
[
21
, Definition 3.7]
), and start with term
\textup{II}.
We need to treat two cases depending on the cancellation property of the atom
a
. We first assume that
\frac{\rho(x_{B})}{4}\leq r_{B}\leq\rho(x_{B})
. Let
j\geq 2
, and consider
x\in C_{j}(B)
. Then, for
y\in B
, we have
\rho(y)\eqsim\rho(x_{B})
and
\left|x-y\right|>\left|x-x_{B}\right|(1-2^{-j})>\frac{\left|x-x_{B}\right|}{2}
. It follows from (
4.2.2
) that for all
N>0
it holds that

\displaystyle\left|(V^{1/2})_{B}(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a)(x)\right|\lesssim(V^{1/2})_{B}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-(n-1)}\left(1+\frac{\left|x-x_{B}\right|}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{-N}

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4B
. Consequently, for
j\geq 2
it holds that

\displaystyle\left\lVert(V^{1/2})_{B}(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(B))}

\displaystyle\lesssim(V^{1/2})_{B}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\rho(x_{B})^{N}\left(\int_{2^{j}r_{B}\leq\left|z\right|<2^{j+1}r_{B}}\left|z\right|^{-2(n-1)-2N}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z\right)^{1/2}

(4.10)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\frac{\delta}{2}-N}2^{-j(n-\frac{n}{p}+N-1)}\left\lvert 2^{j}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}.

Taking
N=2
, we get the required molecular decay since
n+1-\frac{n}{p}>0
and
r_{B}\eqsim\rho(x_{B})
. Next, if
r_{B}\leq\frac{\rho(x_{B})}{4}
, we have the cancellation
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0
. This allows to write

\displaystyle(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a)(x)=\int_{B}K(x,y)a(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{B}(K(x,y)-K(x,x_{B}))a(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4B
. If
x\in C_{j}(B)=2^{j+1}B\setminus 2^{j}B
for
j\geq 2
and
y\in B
, then
\left|x-y\right|>2\left|x_{B}-y\right|
. Since
\eta_{0}\in(0,1)
, the kernel bounds (
4.2.2
) give

\displaystyle\left|(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a)(x)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{B}\frac{\left|y-x_{B}\right|^{\eta_{0}}}{\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{n-1+\eta_{0}}}\left|a(y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\lesssim r_{B}^{\eta_{0}}\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-(n-1+\eta_{0})}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4B
. Integrating this over the annular region
C_{j}(B)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert(V^{1/2})_{B}(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(B))}

\displaystyle\lesssim(V^{1/2})_{B}r_{B}^{\eta_{0}}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{2^{j}r_{B}\leq\left|x-x_{B}\right|\leq 2^{j+1}r_{B}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-2(n-1+\eta_{0})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\!\!1/2}

(4.11)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\frac{\delta}{2}}2^{-j(n-\frac{n}{p}+\eta_{0}-1)}\left\lvert 2^{j}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}.

Let us now consider the inequality (
4.10
) with
N=\frac{\delta}{2(1-\gamma)}
, and raise it to the power
1-\gamma
, and raise (
4.11
) to the power
\gamma
. Multiplying the results, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert(V^{1/2})_{B}(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(B))}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-j(n-\frac{n}{p}+\frac{\delta}{2}+\gamma\eta_{0}-1)}\left\lvert 2^{j}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}.

Since
n-\frac{n}{p}+\frac{\delta}{2}+\gamma\eta_{0}-1>0
by our choice of
\gamma
and
\eta_{0}
, this shows that term
II
satisfies the molecular decay required for a
(p,2,\beta,\alpha\wedge\sigma)
-molecule, for some
\beta>0
.

For term
I
, we simply get that for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4B
and all
N>0
it holds that, independently of any cancellation property of
a
,

\displaystyle\left|\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}(\eta_{V}a)(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B}\left|K(x,y)\right|\left|\eta_{V}(y)\right|\left|a(y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-1/p}r_{B}^{\alpha}m(x_{B})^{1+\alpha}\left|x-x_{B}\right|^{-(n-1)}\left(1+\frac{\left|x-x_{B}\right|}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{-N}.

Consequently, we can estimate as above, for all
j\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}(\eta_{V}a)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(B))}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-1/p}r_{B}^{\alpha}m(x_{B})^{1+\alpha}\rho(x_{B})^{N}(2^{j}r_{B})^{-\frac{n}{2}+1-N}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\alpha+1-N}2^{j(1-N-n+\frac{n}{p})}(2^{j}r_{B})^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}\lesssim 2^{-j(n+\alpha-\frac{n}{p})}\left\lvert 2^{j}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}},

by choosing
N=1+\alpha.
Since
n+\alpha-\frac{n}{p}>0
by assumption, this shows that term
I
satisfies the required molecular decay for a
(p,2,\beta,\alpha\wedge\sigma)
-molecule, for some
\beta>0
. This means that we get the required molecular decay for
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}a
on
C_{j}(B)
for
j\geq 2
. For
j=1
, we can simply use
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-boundedness of
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}
to get
\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(4B)}\lesssim\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}
.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}a
satisfies the global cancellation estimate for a
(p,2,\beta,\alpha\wedge\sigma)
-molecule (condition (c) in
[
21
, Definition 3.7]
). First, using the support property of
a
, an application of (
4.8
) yields

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}(\eta_{V}a))(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\langle\eta_{V}a,\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|\leq\left\lVert\eta_{V}a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B)}\left\lVert\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}r_{B}^{\alpha}m(x_{B})^{1+\alpha}\rho(x_{B})=\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\alpha}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}.

Here, we have used the fact that
\left|(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1)(x)\right|\lesssim\rho(x_{B})
for almost every
x\in B
. This can be seen as follows. For
R:=\rho(x_{B})
,
N>1
and a.e.
x\in B
, we have

\displaystyle\left|(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1)(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B(x_{B},2R)}\left|K(x,y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus B(x_{B},2R)}\left|K(x,y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\left|x-y\right|<R}\left|x-y\right|^{-(n-1)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\left|x-y\right|\geq 3R}\left|x-y\right|^{-(n-1)}\left(1+\frac{\left|x-y\right|}{\rho(x)}\right)^{-N}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim R+\rho(x)^{N}\int_{\left|x-y\right|\geq R}\left|x-y\right|^{-(n-1)-N}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\lesssim R+\rho(x)^{N}R^{-N+1}\lesssim\rho(x_{B}),

since
\rho(x)\eqsim\rho(x_{B})
by (
2.4
). For the remaining term, we use (
4.8
) again to get

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\langle a,\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1-(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1)_{B}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|+\left|\langle a,(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1)_{B}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert a\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(B)}\left\lVert\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1-(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1)_{B}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(B)}+\left|(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1)_{B}\right|\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a\right|.

A slight modification of the arguments used in the proof of
[
47
, Proposition 4.14]
(and, by extension, of the arguments of the proof of
[
47
, Proposition 4.6]
) show that for
0<\eta<1\wedge\delta=1
it holds that

\frac{1}{\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1+\frac{1}{n}}}\int_{B}\left|\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}(1)-(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1)_{B}\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\eta-1}.

We point out that there seems to be an inaccuracy in the proof of this estimate in
[
47
]
, which is due to a typo in the statement of
[
47
, Lemma 4.4]
. Indeed, “every
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
” should be “every
0<\delta<1\wedge\delta_{0}
”. This prevents the
t
-integral in the penultimate display of the proof of
[
47
, Proposition 4.14]
to converge. The issue can be fixed by splitting the integral with respect to
t
in (4.29) of
[
47
]
into three parts:
0<t\leq 4r_{B}^{2}
,
4r_{B}^{2}<t\leq 4\rho(x_{B})^{2}
, and
t>4\rho(x_{B})^{2}
, similarly to what is done in the proof of
[
47
, Proposition 4.6]
. One must then use the full decay available in
t
from
[
47
, Lemma 4.4]
, to treat the part
t>4\rho(x_{B})^{2}
. This can be achieved by picking
N=1
.

Moreover,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a\neq 0
only if
\frac{\rho(x_{B})}{4}\leq r_{B}\leq\rho(x_{B})
, in which case
\left|(\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}1)_{B}\right|\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}a\right|\lesssim\rho(x_{B})\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}
.
Consequently, if
r_{B}<\frac{\rho(x_{B})}{4}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(V^{1/2})_{B}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim(V^{1/2})_{B}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{-1/p}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1+\frac{1}{n}}\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\eta-1}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\eta+\frac{\delta}{2}-1}.

On the other hand, if
\frac{\rho(x_{B})}{4}\leq r_{B}\leq\rho(x_{B})
, then we also have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(V^{1/2})_{B}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}a\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\eta+\frac{\delta}{2}-1}+r_{B}^{-1}\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\delta/2}\rho(x_{B})\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\eta+\frac{\delta}{2}-1}.

We make the choice
\eta:=\sigma+1-\frac{\delta}{2}=\frac{\delta}{2}\in(0,1)
to obtain the cancellation bound

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}a\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left[\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\alpha}+\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\sigma}\right]\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{r_{B}}{\rho(x_{B})}\right)^{\alpha\wedge\sigma}.

This shows that
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}a
satisfies the global cancellation estimate for a
(p,2,\beta,\alpha\wedge\sigma)
-molecule. It follows from (the proof of)
[
21
, Lemma 3.9]
that
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}a\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
with
\left\lVert\mathcal{R}_{\mu}^{*}a\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim 1
. This concludes the proof by Remark
3.10
.
∎

4.2.3.
The
S^{\alpha}
class

In this section we describe a collection of potentials
V
that lie in the intersection
S^{\alpha}\cap\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
\alpha\in(0,1]
when
q>n\geq 1
. We recall that the class
S^{\alpha}
was defined in (
4.5
).

Let
P:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
be a polynomial, and let
a\in(0,2]
. Note that
V:=\left|P\right|^{a}\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by Proposition
2.3
. We claim that
V\in S^{a/2}
, so
\left(\frac{n}{n+a/2},1\right]\subseteq\mathcal{I}(V)
by Theorem
4.10
.
To see this, we first note that (
2.6
) implies that
\left|\nabla P(x)\right|\lesssim m(x,V)^{\frac{a+2}{a}}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Also, note that
\left|t^{p}-s^{p}\right|\leq\left|t-s\right|^{p}
for all
t,s\geq 0
and
p\in(0,1]
. Let now
B=B(x_{B},r_{B})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be such that
r_{B}\leq\rho(x_{B},V)
. Then, for all
x,y\in B
, the mean-value theorem and (
2.4
) imply that there is a point
\xi\in B
such that

\displaystyle\left|V(x)^{1/2}-V(y)^{1/2}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\left|P(x)\right|^{a/2}-\left|P(y)\right|^{a/2}\right|\leq\left|\left|P(x)\right|-\left|P(y)\right|\right|^{a/2}\leq\left|P(x)-P(y)\right|^{a/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left|x-y\right|^{a/2}\left|\nabla P(\xi)\right|^{a/2}\lesssim r_{B}^{a/2}m(\xi,V)^{1+a/2}\eqsim r_{B}^{a/2}m(x_{B},V)^{1+a/2},

which proves the claim.

5.
Riesz transform bounds and Theorem
1.1

In this section, we consider
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-boundedness of the Riesz transforms associated with the Schrödinger operator
H
. This is an important step towards the identification of the Hardy spaces
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
in the next section. The starting point for the definition of the Riesz transform is the Kato square root result obtained by Morris–Turner
[
51
]
. They have shown that if
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\left\{\frac{n}{2},2\right\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})=\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the estimate

(5.1)

\left\lVert H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\eqsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}}

for all
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
By density of
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(see Proposition
2.9
), the operator
H^{1/2}
has a bounded extension
\dot{H}^{1/2}:\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
which still satisfies (
5.1
). Since
\overline{\mathsf{R}(H)}=\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by (
2.20
), the argument outlined at the start of Section
4.2
shows that this extension is an isomorphism.
The inverse of this isomorphism shall be denoted
\dot{H}^{-1/2}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Consequently, the
Riesz transform

R_{H}:=\nabla_{\mu}\dot{H}^{-1/2}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\right)
is a bounded mapping, satisfying
\left\lVert R_{H}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let us also note that
R_{H}=\nabla_{\mu}H^{-1/2}
on
\mathsf{R}(H^{1/2})
.

In the rest of this section, we shall obtain boundedness of the operator
R_{H}
on
\mathrm{L}^{p}
for a range of exponents
p\neq 2
by adapting the method that was used in
[
11
, Chapter 7]
.

5.1.
A conservation property for resolvents of Schrödinger operators

This section describes the analogue of the conservation property
[
11
, Corollary 5.4]
that holds in the presence of the singular potential
V
. The main result in this direction is Lemma
5.2
below. We shall first need the following elementary lemma. It is recorded at the start of the proof of
[
11
, Corollary 5.4]
, but since we will be using it several times, we include some details for the interested reader.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, let
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
be finite dimensional Hilbert spaces, and let
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
be a family of linear operators mapping
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};W_{1})
into
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};W_{2})
that satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates of order
\gamma>\frac{n}{2}
. Then for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n};W_{1})
, it holds that
T(z)f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};W_{2})
, locally uniformly in
z\in\Omega
.

Proof.

Let
f
be supported in a ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of radius
r>0
. Using Hölder’s inequality and the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates satisfied by
\left\{T(z)\right\}_{z\in\Omega}
, we obtain for every
j\geq 2
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert T(z)f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(C_{j}(B))}\leq\left\lvert C_{j}(B)\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert T(z)f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(B))}\lesssim\left|z\right|^{\gamma}r^{\frac{n}{2}-\gamma}2^{(\frac{n}{2}-\gamma)j}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.

We get
\left\lVert T(z)f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(C_{1}(B))}\leq\left\lvert 4B\right\rvert^{1/2}\left\lVert T(z)f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(4B)}\lesssim r^{n/2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
by Hölder’s inequality and the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-bounds for
T(z)
.
Finally, since
\gamma>\frac{n}{2}
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert T(z)f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lVert T(z)f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(C_{j}(B)))}\lesssim r^{n/2}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}+\left(\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}2^{(\frac{n}{2}-\gamma)j}\right)r^{\frac{n}{2}-\gamma}\left|z\right|^{\gamma}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}<\infty.\qed

We can now state and prove the main result of this section. This is to be compared with
[
11
, Corollary 5.4]
. We note that it holds for general
n\geq 1
and non-negative
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then for every integer
k\geq 1
and every
t>0
, it holds that both
H(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)
and
V(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)
belong to
\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and

(5.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}A_{\perp\perp}H(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}aV(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f).

Proof.

For every
t>0
and every integer
k\geq 1
we have

(5.3)

t^{2}H(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}=(1+t^{2}H)^{-(k-1)}-(1+t^{2}H)^{-k},

with the convention that
(1+t^{2}H)^{0}:=1
. By composition (when
k\geq 2
