), we know that the family
\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order, for any
k\geq 1
. It follows from Lemma
5.1
that
(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
t>0
. Since also
f\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we conclude from (
5.3
) that
t^{2}H(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
k\geq 1
and
t>0
.

To prove that
V(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we pick some ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of radius
r>0
such that
\textup{supp}\left(f\right)\subseteq B
, and we write

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|t^{2}V(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\int_{C_{j}(B)}\left|tV^{\frac{1}{2}}\right|\left|tV^{\frac{1}{2}}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\right|

(5.4)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lVert tV^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(B))}\left\lVert t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(B))}.

By the doubling property of
V
, we get
\left\lVert V^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(B))}\lesssim\kappa^{\frac{j}{2}}\left(\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
for each
j\geq 1
, where
\kappa
denotes the doubling constant of Lemma
2.1
.
By composition (when
k\geq 2
) we know that the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order. Therefore, for
j\geq 2
and all
\gamma>0
we can write

\displaystyle\left\lVert t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(B))}\lesssim\left(1+\frac{\textup{dist}(B,C_{j}(B))}{t}\right)^{-\gamma}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim\left(\frac{t}{r}\right)^{\gamma}2^{-j\gamma}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}},

while for
j=1
we have the uniform
\mathrm{L}^{2}
estimate
\left\lVert t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{1}(B))}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
.
Now it is only a matter of choosing
\gamma
large enough so that
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j\gamma}\kappa^{\frac{j}{2}}<\infty
to prove that the sum in (
5.4
) is indeed finite.
Let us finally prove the identity (
5.2
). Let
\{\chi_{r}\}_{r>0}
be a family of smooth, compactly supported and
\left[0,1\right]
-valued functions such that
\chi_{r}(x)=1
for all
\left|x\right|\leq r
,
\textup{supp}\left(\chi_{r}\right)\subseteq\textup{B}(0,2r)
and such that
\left\lVert\nabla_{x}\chi_{r}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\lesssim r^{-1}
.
Then, by dominated convergence we can write

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}A_{\perp\perp}H(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)=\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\chi_{r}A_{\perp\perp}H(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f).

Let
r>1
be fixed. Since
(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\in\mathsf{D}(H)
and
\chi_{r}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we can use the form definition of
H
to write

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\chi_{r}A_{\perp\perp}H(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)

\displaystyle=\langle A_{\parallel\parallel}\nabla_{x}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f),\nabla_{x}\chi_{r}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}+\langle aV(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f),\chi_{r}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.

Now, using the off-diagonal estimates for the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\}_{t>0}
, the boundedness of
A_{\parallel\parallel}
, and the fact that
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
has compact support, we deduce from Lemma
5.1
that
A_{\parallel\parallel}\nabla_{x}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Since
\lim_{r\to\infty}\nabla_{x}\chi_{r}(x)=0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and since
\left\lVert\nabla_{x}\chi_{r}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\lesssim r^{-1}\leq 1
, we can deduce by dominated convergence that

\lim_{r\to\infty}\langle A_{\parallel\parallel}\nabla_{x}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f),\nabla_{x}\chi_{r}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=0.

Similarly, since
aV(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f)\in\mathrm{L}^{1},
we obtain by dominated convergence that

\lim_{r\to\infty}\langle aV(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f),\chi_{r}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}aV(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}(f).\qed

5.2.
Riesz transforms bounds on
\mathrm{L}^{p}
– Proof of Theorem
1.1

Let us now turn to the boundedness properties of
R_{H}
. In this section, we let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\left\{\frac{n}{2},2\right\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We first note that the arguments used in Sections 7.3 and 7.4 of
[
11
]
can be used verbatim (upon replacing
L
with
H
and
\nabla_{x}
with
\nabla_{\mu}
) to obtain the following result.

Lemma 5.3
.

If
R_{H}
is
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounded for some
p\in(1,\infty)
, then
p\in[p_{-}(H),q_{+}(H)]
.

We shall now prove Theorem
1.1
. Let
\alpha\geq 1
be an integer. We obtain a singular integral representation of
R_{H}
in terms of powers of resolvents by following the approach that leads to equation (7.3) in
[
11
]
. In fact, by applying the Calderón–McIntosh reproducing formula to the injective sectorial operator
H
with the auxiliary function
\psi_{\alpha}(z)=z^{3\alpha+1/2}(1+z)^{-9\alpha}
, we obtain that the truncated Riesz transforms

(5.5)

\displaystyle R_{H}^{\varepsilon}:=c_{\alpha}^{-1}\int_{\varepsilon}^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-3\alpha}\left(t^{2}H\right)^{3\alpha}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-6\alpha}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2}),

where
\varepsilon>0
and
c_{\alpha}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\psi_{\alpha}(t^{2})\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}>0
, give the representation
R_{H}f=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}R_{H}^{\varepsilon}f
, with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\right)
for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}
.

Before starting the proof of Theorem
1.1
we need two lemmas. The first is the analogue of
[
11
, Lemma 7.4]
in our setting. We note that it holds for general non-negative
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{J}(H)\cap(1,\infty)
and let
r
be between
p
and
2
(i.e. either
r\in(p,2)
or
r\in(2,p)
). There exists an integer
\beta\geq 1
(depending on
p
,
r
and
n
) with the following properties:

(i)

If
p\leq 2
, then
\left\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-\beta}\right\}_{t>0}
and
\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-\beta-1}\right\}_{t>0}
satisfy
\mathrm{L}^{r}-\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order.

(ii)

If
p\geq 2
, then
\left\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-\beta}\right\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{r}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order.

Proof.

This is proved as in
[
11
, Lemma 7.4]
, using Proposition
3.21
instead of
[
11
, Lemma 4.4]
and Lemma
4.2
instead of
[
11
, Lemma 6.3]
.
∎

The following lemma will be crucial in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
p_{0}\in(2,q_{+}(H))
. There exists an integer
\omega\geq 2
such that the following properties hold for all integers
k\geq 1
and
m\geq\omega
:

(i)

\left\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-(m-1)}\right\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order;

(ii)

\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}\right\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
and
\mathrm{L}^{r}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order for all
r\in({p_{0}}_{*},p_{0}]
;

(iii)

\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m}\right\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
4.4
that
p_{0}\in(2,p_{+}(H))
, hence Lemma
5.4
shows that there exists an integer
\omega\geq 2
such that the family
\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-(\omega-1)}\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order.
Moreover, interpolation of the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates for
\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-1}\}_{t>0}
(recall the start of Section
2.5.4
) with its
\mathrm{L}^{s}
-boundedness for some
p_{0}<s<p_{+}(H)
implies that
\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-1}\}_{t>0}
has
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order (see
[
11
, Lemma 4.14]
). By composition (see
[
11
, Lemma 4.6]
), we obtain (i).

Similarly, interpolation of the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
off-diagonal estimates for
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-1}\}_{t>0}
with its
\mathrm{L}^{s}
-boundedness for some
p_{0}<s<q_{+}(H)
implies that
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-1}\}_{t>0}
has
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order.
By composition with those satisfied by
\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-1}\}_{t>0}
, we obtain the first part of (ii).

Combining (by composition) (i) with the
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order for the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-1}\}_{t>0}
yields (iii).

Finally, we prove the second part of (ii). Let
m\geq\omega
be arbitrary. It follows from (iii) and the first part of (ii) that both
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m}\}_{t>0}
and
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m-1}\}_{t>0}
are
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-bounded and
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-bounded. We now treat two different cases:

(1)

First, if
p_{0}\geq 2^{*}
, then we have
2\leq p_{0_{*}}<p_{0}
. By interpolation (see
[
11
, Lemma 4.13]
) of the
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-boundedness of the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m-1}\}_{t>0}
with its
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-boundedness, we deduce that this family is
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0_{*}}}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-bounded.

(2)

If, on the contrary,
2<p_{0}<2^{*}
, then we have
2_{*}<p_{0_{*}}<2.
By Lemma
4.2
, we deduce that
\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-1}\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0_{*}}}-\mathrm{L}^{2}
-bounded. Then, by composition with the
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-boundedness of the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m}\}_{t>0}
, we obtain that the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m-1}\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0_{*}}}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-bounded.

In conclusion, the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m-1}\}_{t>0}
is always
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0_{*}}}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-bounded. By interpolation with the
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-boundedness of this family, we deduce that
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m-1}\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{r}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-bounded for all
r\in(p_{0_{*}},p_{0}]
.
Now, note that for each
r\in(p_{0_{*}},p_{0}]
, we have
\frac{n}{n+1}<r\leq p_{0}<\infty
and
\frac{n}{r}-\frac{n}{p_{0}}<1
, hence Lemma
4.3
shows that the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}\}_{t>0}
is
\mathrm{L}^{r}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-bounded for all integers
1\leq k\leq m+1
.
Finally, interpolating (see
[
11
, Lemma 4.14]
) with the
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order satisfied by
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}\}_{t>0}
, we obtain the second part of (ii), and the proof is complete.
∎

We now have all the ingredients needed for the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The Riesz transform
R_{H}
is
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounded for all
p\in\left(p_{-}(H)\vee 1,2\right]
. This actually follows merely from the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-boundedness of
R_{H}
, and does not require any additional assumption on
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
other than non-negativity. This can be seen by following
[
11
, Section 7.1]
verbatim, replacing
L
by
H
and
\nabla_{x}
by
\nabla_{\mu}
appropriately. One should also use Lemma
5.4
instead of
[
11
, Lemma 7.4]
.

We now show that if
p\in\left(2,q_{+}(H)\right)
and
p\leq 2q
, then
R_{H}
is
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounded. We proceed by adapting the proof in
[
11
, Section 7.2]
. There are several steps:

Step 1.

We start with some preliminaries. Let us first note that for integers
\alpha\geq 1
we have

(5.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}t\nabla_{\mu}\!\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{\!-3\alpha}\!\!\left(t^{2}H\right)^{\!3\alpha}\!\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{\!-6\alpha}\!\!=t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{\!-1}\!\!\left(1-\!\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{\!-1}\right)^{\!\!3\alpha}\!\!\!\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{\!-6\alpha+1}.\end{split}

Proposition
4.4
implies that
2<p<p^{*}<q_{+}(H)^{*}\leq p_{+}(H)
,
hence
p\in\mathcal{J}(H)
, and therefore both
\left\{\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
and
\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-1}\right\}_{t>0}
are
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounded. The identity (
5.6
) above implies (by composition) that the family

(5.7)

\left\{t\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-3\alpha}\left(t^{2}H\right)^{3\alpha}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-6\alpha}\right\}_{t>0}

is
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounded. As a consequence,
R_{H}^{\varepsilon}
is
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounded with a bound depending on
\varepsilon
. We will apply the Blunck–Kunstmann boundedness criterion stated in
[
11
, Proposition 7.6]
with
T=R_{H}^{\varepsilon}
for a parameter
\varepsilon>0
, and a family
\left\{A_{r}\right\}_{r>0}\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2})
, in order to obtain an
\mathrm{L}^{p}
bound which is independent of
\varepsilon
. The parameter
\alpha\geq 1
will have to be chosen large enough in the course of the proof.

Using Lemma
2.1
, we may pick
\beta>q
such that
V\in\textup{RH}^{\beta}
. Then, we can find some
p_{0}\in(p,q_{+}(H))
such that
p_{0}\leq 2\beta
. Since
\beta>\frac{n}{2}
, this automatically implies that
p_{0}<\beta^{*}
(with the understanding that
\beta^{*}=\infty
if
\beta\geq n
). Lemma
5.5
.(i) shows that there exists an integer
\omega\geq 2
such that the family
\{(1+t^{2}H)^{-(\omega-1)}\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order. Let us assume from now on that
6\alpha\geq\omega
.
Then, the identity (
5.6
) and the definition of
\omega
imply (by composition and the choice of
\alpha
) that the family (
5.7
) is
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
-bounded. As a consequence,
R_{H}^{\varepsilon}
maps
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Consider the function
\varphi(z):=\left(1-\left(1+z\right)^{-\omega}\right)^{3\alpha}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\left\{-1\right\}
. A binomial expansion shows that the approximating family
\left\{A_{r}\right\}_{r>0}:=\left\{1-\varphi\left(r^{2}H\right)\right\}_{r>0}\subset\mathcal{L}\left(\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)
satisfies

(5.8)

A_{r}=-\sum_{k=1}^{3\alpha}\binom{3\alpha}{k}(-1)^{k}\left(1+r^{2}H\right)^{-\omega k}.

Step 2.

Let us now check that both conditions in the Blunck–Kunstmann-type criterion
[
11
, Proposition 7.6]
are satisfied by the linear operator
R_{H}^{\varepsilon}
and the family
\left\{A_{r}\right\}_{r>0}
. Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a ball of radius
r>0
,
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
y\in B
. Let
\mathcal{M}
denote the uncentred Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator for balls. These conditions ask for a constant
C>0
, independent of
\varepsilon
,
f
,
y
and
B
, such that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|R_{H}^{\varepsilon}\left(1-A_{r}\right)f\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq C\left(\mathcal{M}\left(\left|f\right|^{2}\right)\right)^{1/2}(y),

and

(5.10)

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|R_{H}^{\varepsilon}A_{r}f\right|^{p_{0}}\right)^{1/p_{0}}\leq C\left(\mathcal{M}\left(\left|R_{H}^{\varepsilon}f\right|^{2}\right)\right)^{1/2}(y).

The proof of (
5.9
) is obtained by following Step 1 of
[
11
, Section 7.2]
, replacing
\nabla_{x}
by
\nabla_{\mu}
and
L
by
H
.
The proof of (
5.10
) is based on the following claim: There are positive constants
\left\{c_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}
such that for all
g\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
it holds that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}A_{r}g\right|^{p_{0}}\right)^{1/p_{0}}\lesssim\sum_{j\geq 1}c_{j}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{2^{j+1}B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}g\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2},

where
\sum_{j\geq 1}c_{j}<\infty
and the implicit constant does not depend on the ball
B
. Assuming this for now (it will be proved in Step 3), for any
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we can apply it with

g:=c_{\alpha}^{-1}\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}t\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-3\alpha}\left(t^{2}H\right)^{3\alpha}\left(1+t^{2}H\right)^{-6\alpha}f\;\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}

to obtain (
5.10
). Indeed, by
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-boundedness of
A_{r}
and the definition of
R_{H}^{\varepsilon}
, we obtain that
\nabla_{\mu}A_{r}g=R_{H}^{\varepsilon}A_{r}f
, and that
\nabla_{\mu}g=R_{H}^{\varepsilon}f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, because
R_{H}^{\varepsilon}
maps
\mathrm{L}^{2}
to
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
, as observed in Step 1. Hence
g\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p_{0}}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
, and the claim yields (
5.10
). Altogether, this shows that both conditions in
[
11
, Proposition 7.6]
are satisfied, hence
\left\lVert R_{H}^{\varepsilon}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p}
, where the implicit constant doesn’t depend on
\varepsilon
. Since
R_{H}f=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}R_{H}^{\varepsilon}f
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, this concludes the proof.

Step 3.

We prove the claim (
5.11
). First, the expansion (
5.8
) shows that it suffices to prove the claim when
A_{r}
is replaced by
\left(1+r^{2}H\right)^{-\omega k}
for integers
1\leq k\leq 3\alpha
.
It follows from Lemma
5.5
.(ii) that the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order for all
k\geq 1
.
By
[
11
, Proposition 5.1]
, for any integer
m\geq 1
, we can define
t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m}(1)
as an element of
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. To be clear, here we mean
1:=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
.
We claim that the following “cancellation” bound,

(5.12)

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-m}(1)\right|^{p_{0}}\right)^{1/p_{0}}\lesssim\min\left\{r^{-1},\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\},

holds for all integers
m\geq\omega
, where the implicit constant does not depend on
B
. Note that this estimate is trivial when
V\equiv 0
.
Let us assume (
5.12
) for now (it will be proved in Step 4) to conclude the proof. To this end, let
g\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p_{0}}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
and
1\leq k\leq 3\alpha
. We write

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+r^{2}H\right)^{-\omega k}g\right|^{p_{0}}\right)^{1/p_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+r^{2}H\right)^{-\omega k}\left(g-(g)_{B}\right)\right|^{p_{0}}\right)^{1/p_{0}}

\displaystyle+\left|(g)_{B}\right|\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+r^{2}H\right)^{-\omega k}\left(1\right)\right|^{p_{0}}\right)^{1/p_{0}}=:\textup{I}+\textup{II}.

For term
I
, we proceed as in
[
11
, Section 7.2]
, using the
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates for
\left\{r\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+r^{2}H\right)^{-\omega k}\right\}_{r>0}
(see Lemma
5.5
.(iii)) and Poincaré’s inequality to obtain

(5.13)

\displaystyle\textup{I}=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+r^{2}H\right)^{-\omega k}\left(g-(g)_{B}\right)\right|^{p_{0}}\right)^{1/p_{0}}\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j\gamma}2^{\frac{3nj}{2}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{2^{j+1}B}\left|\nabla_{x}g\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

for any
\gamma>0
.

For term
II
, we first fix a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
B\subseteq Q\subseteq\sqrt{n}B
. We then use the cancellation bound (
5.12
), Holder’s inequality and the Fefferman–Phong inequality (Lemma
2.8
with
p=2
) to obtain

(5.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|(g)_{B}\right|\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+r^{2}H\right)^{-\omega k}\left(1\right)\right|^{p_{0}}\right)^{1/p_{0}}&\lesssim\left|(g)_{B}\right|\min\left\{r^{-1},\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{1/2}\right\}\\
&\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left|g\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\min\left\{\ell(Q)^{-1},\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}V\right)^{1/2}\right\}\\
&\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left|\nabla_{\mu}g\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{2^{j_{0}+1}B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}g\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2},\end{split}

where the integer
j_{0}\geq 1
is chosen so that
\sqrt{n}B\subseteq 2^{j_{0}+1}B
.

We can now combine (
5.13
) and (
5.14
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}\left(1+r^{2}H\right)^{-\omega k}g\right|^{p_{0}}\right)^{1/p_{0}}\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j\gamma}2^{\frac{3nj}{2}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{2^{j+1}B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}g\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

We choose
\gamma>\frac{3n}{2}
to prove (
5.11
).

Step 4.

It remains to prove (
5.12
). We first prove, for all integers
m\geq\omega
, that

(5.15)

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}\left|\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-m}(1)\right|^{p_{0}}\right)^{1/{p_{0}}}\lesssim r^{-1}.

By definition (recall
[
11
, Proposition 5.1]
), we have

t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m}(1):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{C_{j}(B)}\right),

with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Therefore, using the
\mathrm{L}^{2}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates of (arbitrarily large) order
\gamma>\frac{n}{2}
for the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-m}\}_{t>0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-m}(1)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(B)}

\displaystyle=r^{-1}\left\lVert r\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-m}(1)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(B)}

\displaystyle\leq r^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lVert r\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-m}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{C_{j}(B)}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(B)}

\displaystyle\lesssim r^{-1+\frac{n}{p_{0}}-\frac{n}{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j\gamma}\left\lvert 2^{j+1}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim r^{-1+\frac{n}{p_{0}}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{j(\frac{n}{2}-\gamma)}\lesssim r^{-1}\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p_{0}}},

and dividing by
\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p_{0}}}
gives (
5.15
).

To prove the estimate (
5.12
), we observe that (
5.15
) reduces matters to proving that

(5.16)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-m}(1)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(B)}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p_{0}}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

under the assumption that
r^{-1}\geq\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
We will estimate
\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-m}(1)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(B)}
by duality. Let us first observe that if
k\geq 1
is an integer, then it follows from the definition that
r\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-k}(1):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}r\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-k}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{C_{j}(B(0,1))}\right)
, with convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Therefore, for any
\varphi=(\varphi_{\parallel},\varphi_{\mu})\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{n+1})
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\langle\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-k}(1),\varphi\right\rangle_{\textup{L}^{2}}&=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-k}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{C_{j}(B(0,1))}\right),\varphi\right\rangle_{\textup{L}^{2}}\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\langle\mathbbm{1}_{C_{j}(B(0,1))},(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}\!\left(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi\right)\right\rangle_{\textup{L}^{2}}\!\!=\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\overline{(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}\!\left(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi\right)},\end{split}

by duality and dominated convergence, using similarity and Lemma
5.1
applied to
H^{\sharp}
to deduce that
(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}\left(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi\right)=A_{\perp\perp}^{*}(1+r^{2}H^{\sharp})^{-k}\left(b^{*}\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi\right)\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
since
b^{*}\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
By telescopic summation we have

\displaystyle(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-m}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left((1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}-(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-(k-1)}\right)=1-\sum_{k=1}^{m}r^{2}H^{*}(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}.

As a consequence, we obtain that

(5.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|\left\langle\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-m}(1),\varphi\right\rangle_{\textup{L}^{2}}\right|&=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-m}\left(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi\right)\right|\\
&\leq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi\right|+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}r^{2}H^{*}(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi)\right|\\
&=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi\right|+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}r^{2}A_{\perp\perp}^{*}H^{\sharp}(1+r^{2}H^{\sharp})^{-k}(b^{*}\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi)\right|\\
&=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{1/2}\varphi_{\mu}\right|+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}r^{2}a^{*}V(1+r^{2}H^{\sharp})^{-k}(b^{*}\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi)\right|\\
&=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{1/2}\varphi_{\mu}\right|+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}r^{2}a^{*}b^{*}V(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi)\right|,\end{split}

where we have used similarity between
H^{\sharp}
and
H^{*}
, Lemma
5.2
applied to
H^{\sharp}
, and the fact that
\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi=-\textup{div}\varphi_{\parallel}+V^{1/2}\varphi_{\mu}
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\textup{div}{\varphi_{\parallel}}=0
.

We first estimate the first term in the last line of (
5.17
). Recall that
V\in\textup{RH}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some
\beta>\frac{n}{2}
. Since
\frac{p_{0}}{2}\leq\beta
, Hölder’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(B)}

\displaystyle=\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1/p_{0}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V^{\frac{p_{0}}{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{0}}}\leq\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1/p_{0}}\left(\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V^{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1/p_{0}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Hence, another application of Hölder’s inequality gives

(5.18)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{1/2}\varphi_{\mu}\right|\leq\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(B)}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1/p_{0}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}.

We now estimate the second term in the last line of (
5.17
). We claim that there is an exponent
q_{0}\in(1,2\beta]
such that

(5.19)

\frac{1}{p_{0}}\leq\frac{1}{q_{0}}+\frac{1}{2\beta}<\frac{1}{{p_{0}}_{*}}=\frac{1}{p_{0}}+\frac{1}{n}.

Indeed, it suffices to pick
\frac{1}{q_{0}}\in[\frac{1}{p_{0}}-\frac{1}{2\beta},\frac{1}{p_{0}}-\frac{1}{2\beta}+\frac{1}{n})\cap[\frac{1}{2\beta},1)
. This intersection is non empty since
\frac{1}{\beta}<\frac{1}{p_{0}}+\frac{1}{n}
(as
p_{0}<\beta^{*}
) and
\frac{1}{p_{0}}-\frac{1}{2\beta}<1
(as
p_{0}>2)
.
Let us now fix an integer
1\leq k\leq m
. We can apply Hölder’s inequality with the exponent
q_{0}>1
to get

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}r^{2}a^{*}b^{*}V(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\int_{C_{j}(B)}\left|rV^{1/2}\right|\left|rV^{1/2}(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi)\right|

(5.20)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lVert rV^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q_{0}}(C_{j}(B))}\!\left\lVert rV^{1/2}(1\!+\!r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(C_{j}(B))}.

First, since
\frac{q_{0}}{2}\leq\beta
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{\beta}
, we can use Hölder’s inequality to estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert rV^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q_{0}}(C_{j}(B))}

\displaystyle\leq r\left[\left(\int_{2^{j+1}B}V^{\frac{q_{0}}{2}}\right)^{\frac{2}{q_{0}}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}=r\left\lvert 2^{j+1}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\left[\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{2^{j+1}B}V^{\frac{q_{0}}{2}}\right)^{\frac{2}{q_{0}}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

(5.21)

\displaystyle\leq r\left\lvert 2^{j+1}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}}\left[\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{2^{j+1}B}V^{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim r\left\lvert 2^{j+1}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{q_{0}}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{2^{j+1}B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Let us now estimate the second factor in (
5.20
).
Let
s_{\beta}
be defined by
\frac{1}{s_{\beta}}:=\frac{1}{q_{0}}+\frac{1}{2\beta}
, and note that (
5.19
) shows that
s_{\beta}\in({p_{0}}_{*},p_{0}]
.
Let the conjugate exponent
s^{\prime}_{\beta}
be defined by
\frac{1}{s^{\prime}_{\beta}}:=1-\frac{1}{s_{\beta}}
. Since
\frac{1}{q_{0}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2\beta}+\frac{1}{s_{\beta}^{\prime}}
, Hölder’s inequality and the fact that
V\in\textup{RH}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
yield

\displaystyle\left\lVert V^{1/2}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(C_{j}(B))}\leq\left[\left(\int_{C_{j}(B)}V^{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s^{\prime}_{\beta}}(C_{j}(B))}\lesssim\!\left\lvert 2^{j+1}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2\beta}-\frac{1}{2}}\!\!\left(\int_{2^{j+1}B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s^{\prime}_{\beta}}(C_{j}(B))}\!.

Applying this to
g:=\left(r\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-k}\right)^{*}\varphi
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert rV^{1/2}(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(C_{j}(B))}=\left\lVert V^{1/2}\left(r\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-k}\right)^{*}\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q^{\prime}_{0}}(C_{j}(B))}

(5.22)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert 2^{j+1}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2\beta}-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{2^{j+1}B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\left(r\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-k}\right)^{*}\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s^{\prime}_{\beta}}(C_{j}(B))}.

Since
s_{\beta}\in({p_{0}}_{*},p_{0}]
, Lemma
5.5
.(ii) implies that the family
\{t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{s_{\beta}}-\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}
off-diagonal estimates, whence the dual family
\left\{\left(t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-k}\right)^{*}\right\}_{t>0}
satisfies
\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}-\mathrm{L}^{s^{\prime}_{\beta}}
off-diagonal estimates of arbitrarily large order.
Since
\frac{1}{s^{\prime}_{\beta}}-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}_{0}}=\frac{1}{p_{0}}-\frac{1}{s_{\beta}}
, this means that we have the following estimate for all
\gamma>0
and
j\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(r\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-k}\right)^{*}\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s^{\prime}_{\beta}}(C_{j}(B))}

\displaystyle\lesssim r^{\frac{n}{s^{\prime}_{\beta}}-\frac{n}{p^{\prime}_{0}}}\left(1+\frac{\textup{dist}(B,C_{j}(B))}{r}\right)^{-\gamma}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\lesssim r^{\frac{n}{p_{0}}-\frac{n}{s_{\beta}}}2^{-j\gamma}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}.

We can now combine the estimates (
5.20
), (
5.21
), (
5.22
) and (
5.23
) and use the doubling property of
V
(with doubling constant
\kappa
from Lemma
2.1
.(ii)) to get, for any
k\in\left\{1,\ldots,m\right\}
,

(5.24)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}r^{2}a^{*}b^{*}V(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi)\right|&\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lVert rV^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q_{0}}(C_{j}(B))}\left\lVert rV^{1/2}(1+r^{2}H^{*})^{-k}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\varphi)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q_{0}^{\prime}}(C_{j}(B))}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lvert 2^{j+1}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2\beta}+\frac{1}{q_{0}}-1}r^{1+\frac{n}{p_{0}}-\frac{n}{s_{\beta}}}\left(\int_{2^{j+1}B}V\right)2^{-j\gamma}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lvert 2^{j+1}B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{s_{\beta}}-1}r^{1+\frac{n}{p_{0}}-\frac{n}{s_{\beta}}}\kappa^{j+1}\left(\int_{B}V\right)2^{-j\gamma}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}\\
&\lesssim r^{1+\frac{n}{p_{0}}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j\gamma}2^{jn\left(\frac{1}{s_{\beta}}-1\right)}\kappa^{j+1}\right)\\
&\lesssim r^{1+\frac{n}{p_{0}}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)},\end{split}

provided we choose
\gamma>n\left(1-\frac{1}{s_{\beta}}\right)
+
\log_{2}(\kappa)
.

Combining (
5.18
) and (
5.24
) in (
5.17
), with the assumption
r^{-1}\geq\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left|\left\langle\nabla_{\mu}(1+r^{2}H)^{-m}(1),\varphi\right\rangle_{\textup{L}^{2}}\right|&\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{1/p_{0}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}r^{1+\frac{n}{p_{0}}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}\\
&\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p_{0}}}\left[\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+r\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right]\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}\\
&\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p_{0}}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B}V\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}_{0}}(B)}\end{split}

for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B)
, hence (
5.16
) holds. This proves the cancellation estimate (
5.12
), so the entire proof is complete.∎

Remark 5.6
.

We could have studied the
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-boundedness of the components of
R_{H}
separately by introducing the operators
R_{H}^{\parallel}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
R_{H}^{\mu}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
R_{H}:=\begin{bmatrix}R_{H}^{\parallel}\\
R_{H}^{\mu}\end{bmatrix}
, with
R_{H}^{\parallel}=\nabla_{x}H^{-1/2}
and
R_{H}^{\mu}=V^{1/2}H^{-1/2}
on
\mathsf{R}(H^{1/2})
. Inspection of the proof above reveals that if
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\left\{\frac{n}{2},2\right\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then for a given
p\in\left(2,q_{+}(H)\right)
, the operator
R_{H}^{\parallel}
is
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounded if
p<q^{*}
, whereas
R^{\mu}_{H}
is
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-bounded provided
p\leq 2q
. As was previously noted, since
q\geq\frac{n}{2}
,
p\leq 2q
automatically implies that
p<q^{*}
. Let us also remark that this is consistent with
[
55
, Theorems 0.5 and 5.10]
.

6.
Identification of the Hardy spaces
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}

This section is concerned with the identification of the operator-adapted spaces
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
with “concrete” function spaces. In fact, we shall prove the following theorem. We recall here that
b
is the bounded accretive function defined as
b(x)=A_{\perp\perp}(x)^{-1}
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and that the (quasi)norm on
b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{b\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}:=\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
for all
f\in b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q>\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and let
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}
. The following identifications hold with equivalent
p
-(quasi)norms:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}=b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}},&\textup{ if }p\in(p_{-}(H)\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}\;,p_{+}(H));\\
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}=\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2},&\textup{ if }p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\;,q_{+}(H))\cap(1,2q];\\
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}=\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}},&\textup{ if }p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}\,,1]\cap\mathcal{I}(V).\end{array}

We shall mostly follow the strategy used in
[
11
, Chapter 9]
. The theorem is obtained by combining Corollary
6.11
and Proposition
6.12
. We note that if
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},\infty)
is such that
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}=b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
with equivalent
p
-(quasi)norms, then
p\in\mathcal{J}(H)
. This follows from the sectorial version of
[
11
, Proposition 8.10]
as in Part 1 of the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 9.7]
. This shows that the identification
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}=b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
in Theorem
6.1
is the best possible when
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

6.1.
A Calderón–Zygmund–Sobolev decomposition adapted to singular potentials

The main ingredient for the identification of the
H
-adapted Hardy spaces (more specifically the proof of Corollary
6.4
) is a Calderón–Zygmund–Sobolev decomposition adapted to singular potentials. This was obtained by Auscher–Ben Ali
[
9
, Lemma 7.1]
. We recall their construction because we need certain additional properties. In particular, their result only treated smooth compactly supported functions
f
, and properties (v), (viii) and (ix) stated below were not stated. We also point out that there is an inaccuracy in the construction in
[
9
, Lemma 7.1]
, that dates back to
[
5
, Theorem 6]
, where this Calderón–Zygmund decomposition for Sobolev functions was first introduced, but a correction has been provided in
[
7
]
, which we follow.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
n\geq 1
,
q>1
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
p\in[1,2]
. Let
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\alpha>0
. There exists a sequence of cubes
\{Q_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
, a sequence of functions
\{b_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and a function
g\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the following properties:

(i)

f=g+\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
;

(ii)

b_{k}
is supported in
Q_{k}
for each
k\geq 1
;

(iii)

The cubes
\{Q_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
have bounded overlap (see Section
2.1.2
) and satisfy

\sum_{k\geq 1}\nolimits\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert\lesssim\left\lvert\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\nolimits Q_{k}\right\rvert\lesssim\alpha^{-p}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p};

(iv)

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{k}}\left|\nabla_{x}b_{k}\right|^{p}+\left|V^{1/2}b_{k}\right|^{p}+\ell(Q_{k})^{-p}\left|b_{k}\right|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\alpha^{p}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert
for all
k\geq 1
;

(v)

The series
\sum_{k\geq 1}\nabla_{\mu}b_{k}
converges unconditionally in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with

\left\lVert\sum_{k\geq 1}\nolimits\nabla_{\mu}b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}};

(vi)

\left\lVert\nabla_{x}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\lesssim\alpha;

(vii)

\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}^{r}\lesssim\alpha^{r-p}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}
for all
r\in\left[p,2\right]
;

(viii)

If
p<n
and
q\in\left[1,p^{*}\right]
, then
\left\lVert b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q}}^{q}\lesssim\alpha^{q}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert^{1+\frac{q}{n}}
for all
k\geq 1
. If
p\geq n
, then this holds for all
q\in[1,\infty)
;

(ix)

If
r\in\left[p,2\right]
and
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(in addition to
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
) then
b_{k}\in\mathcal{V}^{1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
k\geq 1
, and the series
\sum_{k\geq 1}\nabla_{\mu}b_{k}
converges unconditionally in
\mathrm{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}
denote the uncentred Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator for cubes. Consider the open subset
\Omega:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\mathcal{M}\left(\left|\nabla_{x}f\right|^{p}+\left|V^{1/2}f\right|^{p}\right)(x)>\alpha^{p}\right\}
. If
\Omega
is empty, then we can take
g:=f
and
b_{k}:=0
for all
k\geq 1
. Then, properties (vi) and (vii) follows by Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem, and all remaining properties are trivial.

We henceforth assume that
\Omega\neq\emptyset
. Let
F:=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega
, and let
\{W_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
be a Whitney decomposition of
\Omega
, as constructed in
[
58
, Chapter VI, Theorem 1]
. This means that the
W_{k}
are essentially disjoint (dyadic) cubes, such that
\Omega=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}W_{k}
and

(6.1)

\textup{diam}(W_{k})\leq\textup{dist}(W_{k},F)\leq 4\textup{diam}(W_{k}).

We can then define the enlarged cubes
Q_{k}:=(1+\varepsilon)W_{k}
where
\varepsilon>0
is small enough to ensure that
\Omega=\bigcup_{k\geq 1}Q_{k}
, with bounded overlap. We can also find some
c>1
such that the further enlarged cubes
\widetilde{Q}_{k}:=cQ_{k}
satisfy
\widetilde{Q}_{k}\cap F\neq\emptyset
for all
k\geq 1.

The bounded overlap of the cubes
\left\{Q_{k}\right\}_{k\geq 1}
and the weak-type
(1,1)
bound for the maximal operator yield property (iii).

As in
[
58
, p.170]
, we can construct a partition of unity of
\Omega
subordinate to the covering
\Omega=\cup_{k\geq 1}Q_{k}
. For each
k\geq 1
, there is
\chi_{k}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\left[0,1\right])
with support in
Q_{k}
and such that
\sum_{k\geq 1}\chi_{k}=\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}
, the sum being locally finite on
\Omega
. Moreover,
\left\lVert\chi_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}+\ell_{k}\left\lVert\nabla_{x}\chi_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
for all
k\geq 1
, where
\ell_{k}:=\ell(Q_{k})
.
Moreover, the construction in
[
58
, p.170]
shows that
\chi_{k}\gtrsim 1
on
W_{k}
, uniformly for all
k\geq 1
.

Since
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
p\leq 2
, we know that
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover, since
p/2\in(0,1]
, the third item of Lemma
2.1
shows that
V^{p/2}\in\textup{RH}^{\frac{2}{p}}
if
p\neq 2
, while
V^{p/2}\in\textup{RH}^{q}
if
p=2
. The local Fefferman–Phong inequality (Proposition
2.8
) gives

(6.2)

\int_{Q_{k}}\left|\nabla_{x}f\right|^{p}+\left|V^{1/2}f\right|^{p}\gtrsim\min\left\{\textup{av}_{Q_{k}}V^{p/2},\ell_{k}^{-p}\right\}\int_{Q_{k}}\left|f\right|^{p}

uniformly for all
k\geq 1
. We say that a cube
Q_{k}
is of type 1 if
\textup{av}_{Q_{k}}V^{p/2}\geq\ell_{k}^{-p}
, and of type 2 otherwise.
The functions
b_{k}
are then defined as

b_{k}:=\begin{cases}f\chi_{k},&\text{ if }Q_{k}\text{ is type 1;}\\
(f-(f)_{Q_{k}})\chi_{k},&\text{ if }Q_{k}\text{ is type 2}.\end{cases}

Property (ii) follows immediately from this definition.
Let us now prove property (iv). The proof of
[
9
, Lemma 7.1]
readily shows that

\int_{Q_{k}}\left(\left|\nabla_{x}b_{k}\right|^{p}+\ell_{k}^{-p}\left|b_{k}\right|^{p}\right)\lesssim\alpha^{p}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert

for all
k\geq 1
. In addition, if
Q_{k}
is type 2, the Fefferman–Phong inequality (
6.2
) shows that

\displaystyle\left\lVert V^{1/2}b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q_{k}}V^{p/2}\left|f-(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|^{p}\lesssim\int_{Q_{k}}V^{p/2}\left|f\right|^{p}+\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|^{p}\int_{Q_{k}}V^{p/2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q_{k}}\left|V^{1/2}f\right|^{p}+\left(\int_{Q_{k}}\left|f\right|^{p}\right)\textup{av}_{Q_{k}}(V^{p/2})\lesssim\int_{Q_{k}}\left|\nabla_{x}f\right|^{p}+\left|V^{1/2}f\right|^{p}\lesssim\alpha^{p}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert,

by enlarging the cube
Q_{k}
to
\widetilde{Q}_{k}
and using the definition of
F
. On the other hand, if
Q_{k}
is type 1, we have
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|V^{1/2}b_{k}\right|^{p}\leq\int_{Q_{k}}V^{p/2}\left|f\right|^{p}\lesssim\alpha^{p}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert
. This completes the proof of property (iv).

The proof of
[
9
, Lemma 7.1]
shows that the series
\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}
converges in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We can therefore define
g:=f-\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}
as a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Note that the bounded overlap
\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{k}}(x)\leq N
implies that for any finite subset
K\subset\mathbb{N}
it holds that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{k\in K}\nolimits b_{k}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}^{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\sum_{k\in K}\nolimits\nabla_{\mu}b_{k}\right|^{p}\leq N^{p}\sum_{k\in K}\nolimits\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\nabla_{\mu}b_{k}\right|^{p}\lesssim\alpha^{p}\sum_{k\in K}\nolimits\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert,

using property (iv) to obtain the last inequality. Since
{\sum_{k\geq 1}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert<\infty}
by property (iii), this proves property (v) (see, e.g.,
[
41
, Theorem 3.10]
).

Since
\nabla_{x}g=\nabla_{x}f-\sum_{k\geq 1}\nabla_{x}b_{k}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the distributional gradient
\nabla_{x}g
coincides with an
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{n})
function, and

(6.3)

\left\lVert\nabla_{x}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

In particular, both distributions
g
and
\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}
coincide with locally integrable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see , e.g.,
[
26
, Théorème 2.1]
). Consequently, the representation
f=g+\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}
holds almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, as well as in the
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
norm, which proves property (i). In addition, the proof of
[
9
, Lemma 7.1]
shows that
\left\lVert\nabla_{x}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\lesssim\alpha
. This proves property (vi).

Property (vii) in the case
r=2
is already contained in the statement of
[
9
, Lemma 7.1]
. The case
r=p
follows immediately from property (v), so the result follows by Hölder’s inequality. We will however rewrite the argument for general
r\in[p,2]
, as some steps will be needed for other properties. We first claim that for all cubes
Q
it holds that

(6.4)

\textup{av}_{Q}\left(V^{r/2}\right)\lesssim\textup{av}_{Q}\left(V^{p/2}\right)^{r/p}.

If
r=p
, this is trivial, so we may assume that
p<r\leq 2
. Since
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}
and
0<\frac{r}{2}\leq 1
, the third item of Lemma
2.1
implies that
V^{r/2}\in\cup_{q>1}\textup{RH}^{q}
. Then, since
\frac{p}{r}<1
, the same result implies that
V^{p/2}=\left(V^{r/2}\right)^{p/r}\in\textup{RH}^{\frac{r}{p}}
, so
\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}V^{r/2}\right)^{p/r}\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}\left(V^{r/2}\right)^{p/r}
for each cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and (
6.4
) holds.

Clearly,
g=f
on
F
. Moreover, the sum
\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}
is locally finite on
\Omega
, and the proof of
[
9
, Lemma 7.1]
shows that
g=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1\\
\textup{ type 2}\end{subarray}}(f)_{Q_{k}}\chi_{k}
almost everywhere on
\Omega
.
Hence, using the bounded overlap of the supports of the
\chi_{k}
and (
6.4
), we obtain

(6.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}\left(V^{1/2}\left|g\right|\right)^{r}&=\int_{\Omega}V^{r/2}|{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1\\
\textup{ type 2}\end{subarray}}(f)_{Q_{k}}\chi_{k}}|^{r}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1\\
\textup{ type 2}\end{subarray}}\int_{\Omega}V^{r/2}\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\chi_{k}\right|^{r}\\
&\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1\\
\textup{ type 2}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert\textup{av}_{Q_{k}}(V^{r/2})\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|^{r}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1\\
\textup{ type 2}\end{subarray}}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert\left(\textup{av}_{Q_{k}}(V^{p/2})\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|^{p}\right)^{r/p}.\end{split}

Now, for all cubes
Q_{k}
of type 2 we have, by the Fefferman–Phong inequality (
6.2
),

(6.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\textup{av}_{Q_{k}}(V^{p/2})\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|^{p}&\leq\textup{av}_{Q_{k}}(V^{p/2})\textup{av}_{Q_{k}}\left(\left|f\right|^{p}\right)\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q_{k}}\left(\left|\nabla_{x}f\right|^{p}+\left|V^{1/2}f\right|^{p}\right)\lesssim\alpha^{p},\end{split}

by enlarging the cube
Q_{k}
to
\widetilde{Q}_{k}
and using the definition of
F=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega
in the last inequality. Combining (
6.5
) and (
6.6
), and using property (iii), we get
\int_{\Omega}\left(V^{1/2}\left|g\right|\right)^{r}\lesssim\alpha^{r-p}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}
.
Moreover, since
r\geq p
and
\left|V^{1/2}f\right|\leq\alpha
almost everywhere in
F
(by Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem), we have

\displaystyle\int_{F}\left(V^{1/2}\left|g\right|\right)^{r}\leq\alpha^{r-p}\int_{F}\left(V^{1/2}\left|f\right|\right)^{p}\leq\alpha^{r-p}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}.

This proves that
\left\lVert V^{1/2}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{r}\lesssim\alpha^{r-p}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}
. We also combine (vi) and (
6.3
) to obtain
\left\lVert\nabla_{x}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}^{r}\lesssim\alpha^{r-p}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}
for all
r\geq p
. This proves property (vii).

We now prove property (viii). Let
q\in\left[1,p^{*}\right]
if
1\leq p<n
, or
q\in[1,\infty)
if
p\geq n
. Therefore, if
Q_{k}
is any type 2 cube, the Sobolev–Poincaré inequality (see, e.g.,
[
35
, Lemmas 7.12 and 7.16]
) yields

\left\lVert b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q}}\leq\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert^{1/q}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q_{k}}\left|f-(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|^{q}\right)^{1/q}\lesssim\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert^{1/q}\ell_{k}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{2Q_{k}}\left|\nabla_{x}f\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\lesssim\alpha\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{n}}.

Similarly, if
Q_{k}
is type 1 we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q}}\leq\left\lVert(f-(f)_{Q_{k}})\chi_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q}}+\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|\left\lVert\chi_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q}}\lesssim\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert^{1/q}\ell_{k}\alpha+\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert^{1/q}\lesssim\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert^{1/q}\ell_{k}\alpha,

where we have used the fact that
\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|\lesssim l_{k}\alpha
for all cubes
Q_{k}
of type 1. In fact,

(6.7)

\displaystyle\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q_{k}}\left|f\right|^{p}\lesssim l_{k}^{p}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q_{k}}\left(\left|\nabla_{x}f\right|^{p}+\left|V^{1/2}f\right|^{p}\right)\lesssim l_{k}^{p}\alpha^{p},

by the Fefferman–Phong inequality (
6.2
). This proves property (viii).

Finally, we prove property (ix). Let
r\in\left[p,2\right]
and
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We claim that
b_{k}\in\mathcal{V}^{1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
k\geq 1
. First,
b_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is clear from its definition. Now, if
Q_{k}
is type 2, we simply have

(6.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\nabla_{x}b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}&\lesssim\left\lVert\chi_{k}\nabla_{x}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}+\left\lVert\left(\nabla_{x}\chi_{k}\right)(f-(f)_{Q_{k}})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}\\
&\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{x}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}+\ell_{k}^{-1}\left\lVert f-(f)_{Q_{k}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{x}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}\end{split}

by Poincaré’s inequality. Moreover, we can crudely estimate, using (
6.4
) and (
6.6
),

\displaystyle\left\lVert V^{1/2}b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}^{r}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{r/2}\left|f-(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|^{r}\left|\chi_{k}\right|^{r}\lesssim\int_{Q_{k}}V^{r/2}\left|f\right|^{r}+\int_{Q_{k}}\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|^{r}V^{r/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert V^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}^{r}+\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert\left(\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|^{p}\textup{av}_{Q_{k}}(V^{p/2})\right)^{r/p}\lesssim\left\lVert V^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}^{r}+\alpha^{r}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert.

If
Q_{k}
is type 1, then, as
p\leq r
, the Fefferman–Phong inequality (
6.2
) yields

\displaystyle\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|\leq\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q_{k}}\left|f\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\lesssim\ell_{k}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q_{k}}\left|\nabla_{\mu}f\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\lesssim\ell_{k}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q_{k}}\left|\nabla_{\mu}f\right|^{r}\right)^{1/r}.

Consequently, using Poincaré’s inequality as in (
6.8
), we get

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla_{x}b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\nabla_{x}\left(\chi_{k}\left(f-(f)_{Q_{k}}\right)\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}+\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|\left\lVert\nabla_{x}\chi_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{x}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}+\left|(f)_{Q_{k}}\right|\ell_{k}^{-1}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert^{1/r}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}.

In addition, we have
\left\lVert V^{1/2}b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}^{r}\leq\left\lVert V^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}^{r}
.
This proves that
b_{k}\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}^{r}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}^{r}+\alpha^{r}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert
for all
k\geq 1
.

Finally, we use the estimate above to prove the unconditional convergence of the series
\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For all finite subsets
K\subset\mathbb{N}
we can write, by bounded overlap of the supports of the
b_{k}
’s,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{k\in K}\nolimits\nabla_{\mu}b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}^{r}\!=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\!\left|\sum_{k\in K}\nolimits\nabla_{\mu}b_{k}\right|^{r}\lesssim\sum_{k\in K}\nolimits\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\!\left|\nabla_{\mu}b_{k}\right|^{r}\lesssim\sum_{k\in K}\nolimits\!\left(\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}^{r}\!+\alpha^{r}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert\right).

By bounded overlap of
\left\{Q_{k}\right\}_{k\geq 1}
and property (iii), we have

\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}(Q_{k})}^{r}+\alpha^{r}\left\lvert Q_{k}\right\rvert\right)\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert^{r}_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}+\alpha^{r-p}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}<\infty.

This proves property (ix), and concludes the proof.
∎

6.2.
Embeddings
\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}\subseteq\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
and
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{H}^{1,p}
for
p\in(1,2]

In the rest of Section
6
, we assume that
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\left\{\frac{n}{2},2\right\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
As a first step towards the proof of Theorem
6.1
, we will use Lemma
6.2
to prove the continuous inclusions
\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}\subseteq\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
and
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{H}^{1,p}
(for the respective
p
-(quasi)norms) for a subinterval of exponents
p\in(1,2]
in Corollary
6.4
below.

For any integer
\alpha\geq 1
, we define
\psi_{\alpha}(z):=z^{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}(1+z)^{-3\alpha}
for all
z\in S^{+}_{\pi}
, and consider the square function

(6.9)

\displaystyle\left(S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}f\right)(x):=\left(\iint_{\left|x-y\right|<t}\left|\psi_{\alpha}(t^{2}H)f(y)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}{t^{n+1}}\right)^{1/2}

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We note that, since
\psi_{\alpha}\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}
, it follows from McIntosh’s theorem that
S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\left\lVert S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

We shall need the following extrapolation lemma, adapted from
[
11
, Lemma 9.10]
. Recall that
\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})=\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Lemma 6.3
.

If
s\in\left(p_{-}(H)\vee 1,2\right]
,
p\in(s_{*}\vee 1,s)
and there exists an
\alpha_{0}\geq 1
such that

(6.10)

\left\lVert S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}\left(H^{1/2}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s}}

for all
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and all
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
, where the implicit constant is allowed to depend on
\alpha
, then there exists
\alpha_{1}\geq 1
, depending on
p
,
s
,
p_{-}(H)
,
n
and
\alpha_{0}
, such that

(6.11)

\displaystyle\left\lVert S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}\left(H^{1/2}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and all
\alpha\geq\alpha_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
p\in(s_{*}\vee 1,s)
. The proof of (
6.11
) relies on the following weak-type estimate:

(6.12)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}\left(H^{1/2}f\right)(x)>3\lambda\}\right\rvert\lesssim\lambda^{-p_{0}}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p_{0}}

for all
p_{0}\in(s_{*}\vee 1,s)
,
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and all
\lambda>0
.
Indeed, let us assume that (
6.12
) holds for some fixed
\alpha\geq\alpha_{0}
. We shall deduce that the extrapolated estimate (
6.11
) holds for the same
\alpha
. As a first step we pick some
p_{0}\in(s_{*}\vee 1,s)
such that
p_{0}<p
. We proceed as in the start of the proof of Lemma 9.10 in
[
11
]
, but our argument is more involved as we don’t have a suitable analogue of the space
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
used therein. We rely on the space
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
instead.

The fractional power
H_{0}^{-1/2}=\left(-\Delta+V\right)^{-1/2}
extends to a bounded invertible operator
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}:\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, as described in Section
4.2
.
By
[
47
, Section 4.7]
, for all
1<p<n
, it holds that

(6.13)

\left\lVert\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Since
n\geq 3
, the estimate (
6.13
) with
p=2_{*}>1
implies that
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}=\mathcal{V}^{1,2}=\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})
for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2_{*}}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
. This allows us to define a positive, sub-additive operator
T:\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2_{*}}\to\mathrm{L}^{2}
by

\displaystyle Tf:=S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}\left(H^{1/2}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}f\right)

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2_{*}}
. The fact that
T
maps into
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
follows from the remark following (
6.9
).
By
[
55
, Thm. 0.5, Thm. 5.10]
(or Theorem
1.1
), the Riesz transform
\nabla_{\mu}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}
is
\mathrm{L}^{r}
-bounded for all
r\in(1,2]
. Let us denote
\textup{X}:=\bigcap_{1<r\leq 2}\mathrm{L}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and note that if
f\in\textup{X}
, then
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,r}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
for all
r\in(1,2]
. Since
s\in(1,2]
, we can use assumption (
6.10
) to get

\displaystyle\left\lVert Tf\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s}}=\left\lVert S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}\left(H^{1/2}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s}}

for all
f\in\textup{X}
.
Similarly, since
1<p_{0}\leq 2
, the weak-type estimate (
6.12
) implies that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:(Tf)(x)>3\lambda\right\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-p_{0}}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}}^{p_{0}}\lesssim\lambda^{-p_{0}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}}^{p_{0}}

for all
f\in\textup{X}
. By sub-additivity of
T
and the Marcinkiewicz interpolation theorem, we deduce that for all
p_{0}<r\leq s
and
f\in\textup{X}
, it holds that

(6.14)

\displaystyle\left\lVert Tf\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}.

Now, by
[
9
, Theorem 1.2]
, we know that for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\subseteq\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2})
it holds that
H_{0}^{1/2}f\in\textup{X}
, with
\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}
for all
r\in(1,2]
. Moreover, since
\dot{H_{0}}^{\mkern-9.0mu-1/2}=H_{0}^{-1/2}
on
\mathsf{R}(H_{0}^{1/2})
, for all
f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and all
r\in(p_{0},s]
we obtain by (
6.14
) that

(6.15)

\displaystyle\left\lVert S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}(H^{1/2}f)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}=\left\lVert T(H_{0}^{1/2}f)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}\lesssim\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}.

Finally, for
r\in(p_{0},s]
and
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we use Corollary
2.10
to find a sequence
\left(f_{k}\right)_{k\geq 1}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
f_{k}\to f
in both
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,r}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. Let us define
g_{k}:=H^{1/2}f_{k}
and
g:=H^{1/2}f
. Since
g_{k}\to g
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
, hence
\psi_{\alpha}(t^{2}H)g_{k}\to\psi_{\alpha}(t^{2}H)g
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
for all
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B(x,t)}\left|\psi_{\alpha}\left(t^{2}H\right)g\right|^{2}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{B(x,t)}\left|\psi_{\alpha}\left(t^{2}H\right)g_{k}\right|^{2}

for all
t>0
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By Fatou’s lemma, this implies that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\left|x-y\right|<t}\left|\psi_{\alpha}\left(t^{2}H\right)g(y)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{n+1}}\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{B(x,t)}\left|\psi_{\alpha}\left(t^{2}H\right)g_{k}(y)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{n+1}}.

Raising this inequality to the power
r/2
and using (
6.15
) and Fatou’s lemma once more yields

\displaystyle\left\lVert S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}\left(g\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}^{r}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\left\lVert S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}\left(g_{k}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}^{r}\lesssim\liminf_{k\to\infty}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}^{r}=\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}^{r}.

Since
p_{0}<p\leq s
, applying this with
r=p
concludes this part of the proof.

It remains to prove the weak-type estimate (
6.12
).
Let
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Note that this implies that
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Fix
\lambda>0
, and consider the Calderón–Zygmund decomposition of
f
at height
\lambda
, from Lemma
6.2
. We obtain a sequence of cubes
\{Q_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
, functions
g\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
\{b_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p_{0}}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
, such that
f=g+\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}
with convergence in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We let
\ell_{k}:=\ell(Q_{k})
for all
k\geq 1
.

As in the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 9.10]
, we consider the bounded functions
\varphi_{\alpha}(z)=z^{\alpha}(1+z)^{-\alpha}
for integers
\alpha\geq 1
. We can decompose
f
further as
f=g+\tilde{g}+b
where

\tilde{g}:=\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(1-\varphi_{\alpha}(\ell_{k}^{2}H)\right)b_{k}\hskip 28.45274pt\textup{ and }\hskip 28.45274ptb:=\sum_{k\geq 1}\varphi_{\alpha}(\ell_{k}^{2}H)b_{k}.

This decomposition can be justified by proving that the series
\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(1-\varphi(\ell_{k}^{2}H)\right)b_{k}
converges unconditionally in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with the estimate

\left\lVert\sum_{k\geq 1}\nolimits\nabla_{\mu}\left(1-\varphi_{\alpha}(\ell_{k}^{2}H)\right)b_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s}}^{s}\lesssim\lambda^{s-p}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}.

This is obtained by following the argument of step 4 in the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 9.10]
, replacing
L
by
H
and
\nabla_{x}
by
\nabla_{\mu}
. In addition, Proposition
4.4
should be used instead of
[
11
, Theorem 6.2]
, and property (viii) of the Calderón–Zygmund–Sobolev decomposition from Lemma
6.2
should be used instead of property (i’) stated in the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 9.10]
.
This justifies defining
\tilde{g}:=\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(1-\varphi_{\alpha}(\ell_{k}^{2}H)\right)b_{k}
as an element of
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfying
\left\lVert\widetilde{g}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}}^{s}\lesssim\lambda^{s-p}\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{p}_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}
.

Since
p_{0}\in\left(s_{*}\vee 1,s\right)
, we have
s\in\left(p_{0},2\right]
and
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Property (ix) of the Calderón–Zygmund decomposition implies that
b_{k}\in\mathcal{V}^{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
k\geq 1
, and that the series
\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}
converges unconditionally in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}
. Since for any finite subset
K\subset\mathbb{N}
we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in K}\varphi_{\alpha}(\ell_{k}^{2}H)b_{k}=\sum_{k\in K}b_{k}-\sum_{k\in K}\left(1-\varphi_{\alpha}(\ell_{k}^{2}H)\right)b_{k},

this proves the convergence of the series
b:=\sum_{k\geq 1}\varphi_{\alpha}(\ell_{k}^{2}H)b_{k}
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}
. This justifies the decomposition
f=g+\tilde{g}+b
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}
.
Now, since it is assumed that
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the previous argument can also be carried out for
s=2
, and yields convergence of all the series above in the
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}
norm. Therefore,
g,\tilde{g}
and
b
are in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Let us now remark that, by density of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in the intersection
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}\cap\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}
(Proposition
2.9
), the extension of the square root
\dot{H}^{1/2}:\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
still verifies the estimate (
6.10
) for all
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By linearity of
\dot{H}^{1/2}
and subadditivity of the square function operator
S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}(H^{1/2}f)(x)>3\lambda\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{x:S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}(\dot{H}^{1/2}g)(x)>\lambda\right\}\cup\left\{x:S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}(\dot{H}^{1/2}\tilde{g})(x)>\lambda\right\}

\displaystyle\cup\left\{x:S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}(\dot{H}^{1/2}b)(x)>\lambda\right\}=:A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}.

It therefore remains to bound the measures of
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
. First, by Markov’s inequality, the assumption (
6.10
) and property (vii) of the Calderón–Zygmund decomposition, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert A_{1}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{-s}\left\lVert S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}(\dot{H}^{1/2}g)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s}}^{s}\lesssim\lambda^{-s}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{s}}^{s}\lesssim\lambda^{-p_{0}}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}}^{p_{0}}.

Similarly, we also obtain
\left\lvert A_{2}\right\rvert\lesssim\lambda^{-p_{0}}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p_{0}}
. To prove the remaining estimate for
\left\lvert A_{3}\right\rvert
, as explained in step 6 of the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 9.10]
, it suffices to prove the estimate

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}|S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}(\dot{H}^{1/2}b_{F})(x)>\lambda\right\}\right\rvert\lesssim\lambda^{-p}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}

uniformly for all finite subsets
F\subset\mathbb{N}
, where
b_{F}:=\sum_{k\in F}\varphi_{\alpha}\left(\ell_{k}^{2}H\right)b_{k}
. This estimate is obtained by following the argument in Steps
7
and
8
of the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 9.10]
, by replacing
L
by
H
and
\nabla
by
\nabla_{\mu}
appropriately, and by using Lemma
5.4
instead of
[
11
, Lemma 7.4]
. ∎

We obtain the following important consequence.

Corollary 6.4
.

For
p\in\left(p_{-}(H)\vee 1,2\right]
, there is a continuous inclusion
\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}\subseteq\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
for the respective
p
-(quasi)norms. For
p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee 1,2]
, there is a continuous inclusion
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{H}^{1,p}
for the respective
p
-(quasi)norms.

Proof.

It follows from McIntosh’s theorem and the Kato square root estimate (
5.1
) that the estimate (
6.10
) holds for
s=2
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
. As explained in
[
11
, Remark 9.11]
, we can iterate Lemma
6.3
to obtain that

(6.16)

\left\lVert S_{\psi_{\alpha},H}\left(H^{1/2}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all
p\in\left(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee 1,2\right]
, all sufficiently large
\alpha
(possibly depending on
p
) and all
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The first continuous inclusion then follows from the argument of the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 9.12]
, upon replacing
L
by
H
and
\nabla_{x}
by
\nabla_{\mu}
. In particular, we rely on the
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-boundedness of
R_{H}
established in Theorem
1.1
. We also need to use Lemma
4.1
instead of
[
11
, Lemma 7.2]
. The second inclusion is proved as in
[
11
, Proposition 9.13]
, using that
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
is dense in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
by Corollary
2.10
.
∎

6.3.
Embeddings
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\subseteq\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
for
p\in(1,2]

The main result of this section is Proposition
6.6
below. We shall need the following lemma, which follows from the machinery used to treat general bisectorial operators
T
satisfying the standard assumptions of
[
11
, Section 8.1]
. Here we use the operators
B
and
D
from Section
2.5
, and recall that
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\left\{\frac{n}{2},2\right\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
T\in\left\{BD,DB\right\}
. The embeddings below hold for the respective
p
-(quasi)norms:

(i)

For all
p\in[2,\infty)
, there is a continuous inclusion
\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})\subseteq\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}
.

(ii)

For all
p\in(1,2]
, there is a continuous inclusion
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
.

Proof.

The first item is a bisectorial version of Part 2 in the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 9.7]
. In fact, if
\alpha>\frac{n}{2}
,
p\in[2,\infty)
and
{\varphi(z)=z^{\alpha}(1+iz)^{-2\alpha}}
for all
z\in S_{\pi/2}
, then it shows that

(6.17)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi,T}f\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p}
. Therefore, if
f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
, we obtain that
f\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}
with
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}}\eqsim\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi,T}f\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
.

To prove (ii), we cannot rely on interpolation as in the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 9.14]
, and we shall therefore rely on (i) and duality.
Let
p\in(1,2]
and
f\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
.
Pick a function
\psi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}
which is admissible in the definition of
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}
. If
