\varphi\in\Psi^{\infty}_{\infty}
is chosen so that
\varphi\psi
satisfies the Calderón–McIntosh reproducing formula, then for all
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}\langle(\varphi\psi)(tT)f,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}\langle\psi(tT)f,\varphi^{*}(tT^{*})g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}.

In the formula above, we do not restrict the operator
T
to the closure of its range. We can still make sense of
(\varphi\psi)(tT)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2}))
since
\varphi\psi\in\textup{H}^{\infty}_{0}
.
Consequently, we can use (
2.1
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left|\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\langle\psi(tT)f,\varphi^{*}(tT^{*})g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\leq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}}\left|(\mathbb{Q}_{\psi,T}f)(t,y)\right|\left|(\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi^{*},T^{*}}g)(t,y)\right|\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}{t}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{\psi,T}f\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi^{*},T^{*}}g\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}}\left\lVert\mathbb{Q}_{\varphi^{*},T^{*}}g\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p^{\prime}}},

by definition of the adapted Hardy space
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}
. Here it is important that we haven’t restricted the operator
T
to the closure of its range. Now, since
p^{\prime}\geq 2
and
T^{*}\in\left\{DB^{*},B^{*}D\right\}
has the same properties as
T
, (
6.17
) implies that
\left|\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}}
. Since
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}\cap\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}
was arbitrary, this implies that
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}
with the estimate
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T}}
.
∎

The previous lemma has the following consequence.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
p\in(1,2]
. The following are continuous inclusions for the respective
p
-(quasi)norms:

\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\subseteq\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Proof.

The first inclusion follows from
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{BD}=\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}\oplus\mathbb{H}_{M}^{p}
(see Section
2.5.4
) and Lemma
6.5
.(ii).
To prove the second inclusion, we first consider
f\in\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})
.
Then, Figure
1
shows that
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\nabla_{\mu}f\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}
, with
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}}\eqsim\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{BD}}\eqsim\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\nabla_{\mu}f\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}}
.
Hence, Lemma
6.5
.(ii) implies that
\nabla_{\mu}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}
, with

(6.18)

\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\nabla_{\mu}f\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}}.

Now, if
f\in\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
is arbitrary, then we can find (see
[
11
, Lemma 8.7]
) a sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
in
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})
such that
f_{k}\to f
in both
\mathrm{L}^{2}
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
as
k\to\infty
. Applying the estimate (
6.18
) to
f_{k}-f_{j}
,
k,j\geq 1
, shows that the sequence
(\nabla_{\mu}f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Therefore, we can apply
[
51
, Lemma 2.1]
to deduce that
f
is weakly differentiable, with
\nabla_{\mu}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and that
\nabla_{\mu}f_{k}\to\nabla_{\mu}f
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
as
k\to\infty
. As a consequence,
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
, and we can pass to the limit
k\to\infty
in the estimate (
6.18
) applied to
f_{k}
to conclude the proof.
∎

6.4.
Identification of
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
for
p\leq 2

In this section (see Corollary
6.11
below), we obtain identifications
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}=b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(up to equivalent
p
-quasinorms) for exponents
p
in certain subintervals of
(\frac{n}{n+1},2]
.

The following lemma is a substitute for
[
11
, Corollary 8.29]
that will be needed in the proof of Proposition
6.8
. The definition of
\left(\mathbb{H}^{p}_{T},\varepsilon,1\right)
-molecule is recalled in the proof.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}
and
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\frac{\delta}{2}},1]
. There exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that all
\left(\mathbb{H}^{p}_{D},\varepsilon_{0},1\right)
-molecules
m
belong to
\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
, with
\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim 1
.

Proof.

By definition, if
m
is a
\left(\mathbb{H}^{p}_{D},\varepsilon_{0},1\right)
-molecule, there is a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a function
b\in\mathsf{D}(D)
such that
m=Db
and

\displaystyle\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(Q))}+\ell(Q)^{-1}\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(Q))}\lesssim(2^{j}\ell{(Q)})^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}2^{-j\varepsilon_{0}}

for all
j\geq 1
. This implies that
m\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
. In fact,

(6.19)

\displaystyle\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left\lvert C_{j}(Q)\right\rvert^{1/2}\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(Q))}\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{\frac{nj}{2}}\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{2}}(2^{j}\ell{(Q)})^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}2^{-j\varepsilon_{0}}\lesssim\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}.

Writing
b=\begin{bmatrix}b_{\perp}\\
(b_{\parallel},b_{\mu})\end{bmatrix}
and
m=\begin{bmatrix}-\nabla_{\mu}^{*}(b_{\parallel},b_{\mu})\\
-\nabla_{\mu}b_{\perp}\end{bmatrix},
where
b_{\perp}\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
(b_{\parallel},b_{\mu})\in\mathsf{D}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*})
, gives

(6.20)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}m\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla_{\mu}^{*}(b_{\parallel},b_{\mu})\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla_{x}b_{\perp}\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{1/2}b_{\perp}\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{1/2}b_{\mu}\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{1/2}b_{\perp}\right|.

To see the second equality, observe that since
b_{\perp}\in\dot{\textup{W}}^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
n\geq 3
, we have
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla_{x}b_{\perp}=0
(this general property of
\dot{\textup{W}}^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
functions for
n\geq 2
follows from
[
40
, Theorem 4]
with
m=1
). In our case, this can also be seen directly. Indeed, let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be such that
\textup{supp}\left(\varphi\right)\subseteq 2Q
and
\varphi=1
on
Q
. Then, define
\varphi_{j}(x)=\varphi\left(\frac{x}{2^{j}}\right)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
j\geq 1
. Since
b_{\perp}\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we are justified in writing

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi_{j}\nabla_{x}b_{\perp}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}b_{\perp}\nabla_{x}\varphi_{j}\right|\lesssim 2^{-j}\int_{2^{j+1}Q\setminus 2^{j}Q}\left|b_{\perp}\right|\leq 2^{-j}\left\lVert b_{\perp}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(Q))}\left\lvert 2^{j+1}Q\right\rvert^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-j}\ell{(Q)}(2^{j}\ell{(Q)})^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}2^{-j\varepsilon_{0}}2^{\frac{jn}{2}}\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{1/2}\lesssim 2^{j(n-\frac{n}{p}-\varepsilon_{0}-1)},

where the implicit constants depend on
Q
. Since
\nabla_{x}b_{\perp}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it follows from dominated convergence that
\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla_{x}b_{\perp}\right|=\lim_{j\to\infty}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi_{j}\nabla_{x}b_{\perp}\right|=0
.
Similarly, as
\nabla_{\mu}^{*}(b_{\parallel},b_{\mu})\in\mathrm{L}^{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla_{\mu}^{*}(b_{\parallel},b_{\mu})

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi_{j}\nabla_{\mu}^{*}(b_{\parallel},b_{\mu})=\lim_{j\to\infty}\langle(b_{\parallel},b_{\mu}),\nabla_{\mu}\varphi_{j}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

(6.21)

\displaystyle=\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}b_{\parallel}\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi_{j}+\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}b_{\mu}V^{1/2}\varphi_{j}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{1/2}b_{\mu},

where we have used the same reasoning as above to show that the penultimate limit is equal to zero. In order to make (
6.21
) rigorous, we need to choose
\varepsilon_{0}>0
suitably large to ensure that
V^{1/2}b_{\mu}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
In fact, if
\hat{b}\in\left\{b_{\mu},b_{\perp}\right\}
and
\kappa
is the doubling constant from Lemma
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}V^{1/2}\left|\hat{b}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq 1}\left\lVert V^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(Q))}\left\lVert b\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(C_{j}(Q))}\leq\sum_{j\geq 1}\left(\int_{2^{j+1}Q}V\right)^{1/2}\ell(Q)(2^{j}\ell(Q))^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}2^{-j\varepsilon_{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(Q)^{1+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}\sum_{j\geq 1}2^{j\left(\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}-\varepsilon_{0}\right)}\kappa^{\frac{j+1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}V\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\ell(Q)^{2}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}V\right)^{1/2},

provided we choose
\varepsilon_{0}>\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{p}+\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}{\kappa}
.
Consequently, it follows from (
6.19
) and (
6.20
) that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}m\right|\lesssim\left\lvert Q\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\min\left\{1,\left(\ell(Q)^{2}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}V\right)^{1/2}\right\}.

It follows that
m
is (a generic multiple of) a
(p,\varepsilon_{0})
-molecule associated to the cube
Q
for
\textup{H}_{V}^{p}
, in the sense of Definition
3.11
. The desired conclusion follows from Theorem
3.12
.
∎

Using Lemma
6.7
, we follow the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 9.14]
to obtain the next result.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}
and
p\in(\frac{n}{n+\frac{\delta}{2}},1].
There is a continuous inclusion
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}\subseteq b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
for the respective
p
-quasinorms.
If in addition
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)
, then there is a continuous inclusion
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}\subseteq\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
for the respective
p
-quasinorms.

Proof.

We claim that there is a continuous inclusion
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}\subseteq\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
for the respective
p
-quasinorms.
Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0
be the constant found in Lemma
6.7
. Let
f\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}
and let us choose some
M>\frac{n}{p}-\frac{n}{2}
. The molecular decomposition
[
11
, Theorem 8.17]
implies that

f=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}m_{i}

with (unconditional) convergence in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
, where
m_{i}
is a
(\mathbb{H}_{DB}^{p},\varepsilon_{0},M)
-molecule for all
i\geq 1
, and such that
\left\lVert(\lambda_{i})_{i\geq 1}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}}
. Writing
m_{i}:=DBb_{i}
for
b_{i}\in\mathsf{D}(DB)
, and using boundedness of
B
we see that
m_{i}
is a multiple of some
(\mathbb{H}^{p}_{D},\varepsilon_{0},1)
-molecule. Lemma
6.7
implies that
m_{i}\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
with
{\left\lVert m_{i}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim 1}
uniformly for all
i\geq 1
. The claim follows from (
3.6
).

Let us now prove the first inclusion. Let
f\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{R}(b\nabla_{\mu}^{*})
. Using Figure
1
, going from the third to the fourth row, we obtain that
\begin{bmatrix}b^{-1}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}
, with
\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}b^{-1}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}}\eqsim\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{BD}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}}
. The previous claim thus implies that
b^{-1}f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}}
.

Let us now consider an arbitrary
f\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
. We know from Figure
1
that there is a sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{R}(b\nabla_{\mu}^{*})
such that
f_{k}\to f
in both
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
and
\mathrm{L}^{2}
.
We claim that
g:=b^{-1}f\in\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Indeed, since
g_{k}\to g
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
as
k\to\infty
, up to a subsequence we get
\mathcal{M}_{V}g(x)\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{M}_{V}g_{k}(x)
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see Section
3.1.2
). Raising this inequality to the power
p
and applying Fatou’s lemma, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}g_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}=\liminf_{k\to\infty}\left\lVert g_{k}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim\liminf_{k\to\infty}\left\lVert f_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}}<\infty.

This means that
g=b^{-1}f\in\mathrm{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}}
, as claimed.

We now prove the second inclusion for
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)
. Let
f\in\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})
. It follows from Figure
1
that
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\nabla_{\mu}f\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{H}_{DB}^{p}\cap\mathsf{R}(D)
, with

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}}\eqsim\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{BD}}\eqsim\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\nabla_{\mu}f\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}}\gtrsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}\gtrsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}},

where we have used the earlier claim and the fact that
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)
(see Section
4.2
).
∎

We now study the converse inclusions. The first result is an analogue of
[
11
, Lemma 9.17]
.

Lemma 6.9
.

For all
p\in(p_{-}(H),1]
, there is a continuous inclusion
b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
for the respective
p
-quasinorms.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\geq 1
be an integer, and consider the auxilliary function
\eta_{\alpha}(z)=z^{\alpha}(1+z)^{-2\alpha}
for all
z\in S^{+}_{\pi}
. Then,
\eta_{\alpha}\in\Psi^{\alpha}_{\alpha}
on all sectors. For
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
consider the square function
S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}f
defined as

S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}f(x)=\left(\iint_{\left|x-y\right|<t}\left|(\eta_{\alpha}(t^{2}H)f)(y)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}{t^{1+n}}\right)^{1/2}.

By Table
1
, we know that
\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}(\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
is an equivalent norm on
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
provided we choose
\alpha>\frac{n}{2p}-\frac{n}{4}
.
If
f\in b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then Theorem
3.9
yields an
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-convergent atomic decomposition
b^{-1}f=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}m_{i}
, where
\left\lVert\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}_{i}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}_{V}^{p}}
and the
m_{i}
are abstract
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-atoms for
H_{0}
(see Definition
3.8
). By the Fatou’s lemma argument used in the proof of Lemma
6.3
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}(f)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}\leq\sum_{i}\left|\lambda_{i}\right|^{p}\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}(bm_{i})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}.

Consequently, it suffices to prove the existence of some
\alpha\geq 1
large enough (depending on
n,p
and
p_{-}(H)
) such that
\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}(bm)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim 1
for all abstract
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-atoms
m
for
H_{0}
. This is obtained by following the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 9.17]
, applying Lemma
4.2
instead of
[
11
, Lemma 6.3]
and Proposition
3.21
instead of
[
11
, Lemma 4.4]
. Let us also note that for
\frac{n}{n+1}<r<p\leq 1
, it holds that
\lfloor\frac{n}{2p}\rfloor+1=\lfloor\frac{n}{2r}\rfloor+1
, and it is therefore clear from Definition
3.8
that if
m
is an abstract
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
-atom associated to a ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then the multiple
\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{r}}m
is an abstract
\textup{H}^{r}_{V}
-atom associated to the same ball.
∎

The following result is proved by adapting
[
11
, Lemma 9.16]
.

Lemma 6.10
.

For
p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+1},1]
there is a continuous inclusion
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
for the respective
p
-quasinorms.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\geq 1
be an integer, and consider the auxilliary function
\eta_{\alpha}(z)=z^{\alpha}(1+z)^{-2\alpha}
. Then,
\eta_{\alpha}\in\Psi^{\alpha}_{\alpha}
on all sectors. For
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
consider the square function
S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}f
defined as

S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}f(x):=\left(\iint_{\left|x-y\right|<t}\left|t^{-1}(\eta_{\alpha}(t^{2}H)f)(y)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y}{t^{1+n}}\right)^{1/2}.

We know that
\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}(\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
is an equivalent norm on
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
provided we choose
\alpha>\frac{n}{2p}-\frac{n}{4}
(see Table
1
). Let
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
. By Theorem
3.18
there is an
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-convergent atomic decomposition
H_{0}^{1/2}f=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}H_{0}^{1/2}m_{i}
,
where the
m_{i}\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2})
are
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-atoms for
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
{\left\lVert\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}_{i}\right\rVert_{\ell^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}}
. By the Kato square root estimate (
5.1
), we have
\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})=\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2})
with
\left\lVert H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\eqsim\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
for all
f\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{1/2})=\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Consequently, we obtain an
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-convergent decomposition
H^{1/2}f=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}H^{1/2}m_{i}
.

Since
H^{-1/2}\eta_{\alpha}(t^{2}H)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2})
for all
t>0
, we obtain
\eta_{\alpha}(t^{2}H)f=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\eta_{\alpha}(t^{2}H)m_{i}
, where the sum converges in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
. As in the proof of Lemma
6.9
, this implies that
\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}(f)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}\leq\sum_{i}\left|\lambda_{i}\right|^{p}\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}(m_{i})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{p}
,
and this shows that it suffices to obtain a uniform estimate of the form
\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim 1
for all
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-atoms
m
for
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, for all sufficiently large
\alpha
.

Let
m
be an
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-atom for
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, associated to a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it holds that

(S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}m)(x)=S_{\varphi_{\alpha},H}(H^{1/2}m)(x)
, where
\varphi_{\alpha}(z):=z^{\alpha-1/2}(1+z)^{-2\alpha}
. Using Hölder’s inequality followed by McIntosh’s theorem, we obtain the local bound

\displaystyle\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(4Q)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert 4Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(4Q)}=\left\lvert 4Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert S_{\varphi_{\alpha},H}(H^{1/2}m)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(4Q)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert 4Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert H^{1/2}m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\eqsim\left\lvert 4Q\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim 1.

To obtain the global bound, we can follow the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 9.16]
, by choosing some
q\in(p_{-}(H),p^{*})\cap(1,2]
and applying Lemma
5.4
instead of
[
11
, Lemma 7.4]
. This yields the global bound
\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4Q)}\lesssim\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{p}-\frac{n}{q}-1}\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q}}
for suitably large
\alpha
.
Since
m
is supported in
Q
and
q\leq 2<2^{*}
, the conclusion follows from Hölder’s inequality and a Sobolev embedding as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lVert S_{\eta_{\alpha},H}^{(1)}(m)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus 4Q)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{p}-\frac{n}{q}-1}\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{q}}\leq\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{p}-\frac{n}{2}}\left\lVert m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}}(Q)}\lesssim\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{p}-\frac{n}{2}}\left\lVert\nabla_{x}m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{p}-\frac{n}{2}}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{p}-\frac{n}{2}}\left\lVert H_{0}^{1/2}m\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim 1.\qed

We can now combine Corollary
6.4
, Propositions
6.6
and
6.8
, and Lemmas
6.9
and
6.10
, to obtain the following consequence.

Corollary 6.11
.

Let
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}
. The following identifications hold:

(i)

If
p\in(p_{-}(H)\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2},2]
, then
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}=b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
with equivalent
p
-(quasi)norms.

(ii)

If
p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2},1]\cap\mathcal{I}(V)
, then
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}=\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
with equivalent
p
-quasinorms.

(iii)

If
p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee 1,2]
, then
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}=\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
with equivalent
p
-norms.

6.5.
Identification of
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
for
p\geq 2

The following lemma provides an identification of the
H
-adapted spaces
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
and
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
in a subinterval of exponents
p\in(2,\infty)
to conclude the proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Proposition 6.12
.

If
p\in(2,p_{+}(H))
, then
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}=\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
with equivalent
p
-norms.
If
p\in(2,q_{+}(H))
and
p\leq 2q
, then
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}=\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
with equivalent
p
-norms.

Proof.

The first identification follows both from the first component of the continuous inclusion of Lemma
6.5
.(i) (with
T=BD
), and by duality and similarity as in Part 8 of the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 9.7]
, by applying the first item of Corollary
6.11
to the operator
H^{\sharp}
(recall Section
2.5.3
).

To prove the second identification, we need to establish two inclusions. First, if
p\in(2,p_{+}(H))
, we claim that
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}\subseteq\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
.
We follow the proof of (9.34) in
[
11
]
. Let us first pick
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
. By duality and similarity, this implies that
p^{\prime}\in(p_{-}(H^{\sharp})\vee 1,2)
. Since
H^{\sharp}
satisfies the same hypotheses as
H
, Theorem
1.1
implies that the Riesz transform
R_{H^{\sharp}}
is
\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}
-bounded. Now, for any
g\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}\cap\mathsf{R}(H^{*})\cap\mathsf{D}(H^{*})
, we can write, using the similarity between
H^{*}
and
H^{\sharp}
(see Section
2.5.3
),

\displaystyle\langle H^{1/2}f,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle f,{H^{*}}^{1/2}g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\langle f,{b^{*}}^{-1}H^{\sharp}\left(b^{*}{H^{*}}^{-1/2}g\right)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{x}f,A_{\parallel\parallel}^{*}\nabla_{x}\left(b^{*}{H^{*}}^{-1/2}g\right)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}+\langle V^{1/2}f,a^{*}V^{1/2}b^{*}{H^{*}}^{-1/2}g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\begin{bmatrix}A_{\parallel\parallel}&0\\
0&a\end{bmatrix}\nabla_{\mu}f,\nabla_{\mu}{H^{\sharp}}^{-1/2}(b^{*}g)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\left\langle\begin{bmatrix}A_{\parallel\parallel}&0\\
0&a\end{bmatrix}\nabla_{\mu}f,R_{H^{\sharp}}(b^{*}g)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}},

where the last equality follows from the characterisation of
R_{H^{\sharp}}
on
\mathsf{R}({H^{\sharp}})=b^{*}\mathsf{R}({H^{*}})
.
Following the argument of Part 9 of the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 9.7]
, using boundedness of
R_{H^{\sharp}}
, Lemma
4.1
, Proposition
6.12
and Figure
1
, we obtain the estimate
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
.
Finally, we can use Corollary
2.10
and the usual Fatou’s lemma argument to extend to the general case
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
.

Next, we claim that if
p\in(2,q_{+}(H))
and
p\leq 2q
, then
\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\subseteq\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
. This is the analogue of (9.33) in Step 9 of the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 9.7]
. It follows from Figure
1
, Proposition
4.4
, Proposition
6.12
, and Theorem
1.1
, following the argument in the aforementioned reference.
Finally, one should conclude using
[
51
, Lemma 2.1]
as in the proof of Proposition
6.6
. Since
p_{+}(H)>q_{+}(H)
by Proposition
4.4
, the two inclusions conclude the proof.
∎

7.
Properties of weak solutions

In this section, we collect some important features of the elliptic equation
\mathscr{H}u=g
on open subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}
(recall (
1.7
)). We work with
n\geq 1
and a general non-negative
V\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The potential
V
is extended to
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}
by
t
-independence by setting
V(t,x):=V(x)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
With that in mind, the space
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
is defined as in (
2.9
), and now we distinguish the components
\nabla_{\mu}u:=\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{t}u\\
\nabla_{x}u\\
V^{1/2}u\end{bmatrix}
for a weakly differentiable function
u:\mathbb{R}^{1+n}\to\mathbb{C}
.

We first note that the
t
-independence of
V
and of the coefficients
A
implies that the ellipticity assumption (
1.5
) can be extended to
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. In fact, for
u\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, we can integrate (
1.5
) with respect to
t
to obtain
\operatorname{Re}{{\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}u,\nabla_{\mu}u\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})}}}\geq\lambda\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})}^{2}
, where
\mathcal{A}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{1+n};\mathcal{L}\left({\mathbb{C}^{n+2}}\right)\right)
is defined as

(7.1)

\mathcal{A}(t,x):=\begin{bmatrix}A(x)&0\\
0&a(x)\end{bmatrix}:=\begin{bmatrix}A_{\perp\perp}(x)&0&0\\
0&A_{\parallel\parallel}(x)&0\\
0&0&a(x)\end{bmatrix}

for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{1+n}
. This ellipticity inequality extends to all
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
by Lemma
2.6
.

Even though only the homogeneous norm appears here, we may not be able to extend the ellipticity to
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, because the
t
-extension
V(t,x):=V(x)
may only belong to
\textup{RH}^{\frac{n}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, which is not enough to apply Proposition
2.9
.

We now record a Caccioppoli-type inequality and reverse Hölder estimates for weak solutions, as defined in (
1.7
).
If
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is open,
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
, and
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=g
in
\Omega
, and
\alpha>1
, then

(7.2)

\displaystyle\iint_{Q}\left|\nabla_{\mu}u\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\ell(Q)^{-2}\iint_{\alpha Q}\left|u\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\left(\iint_{\alpha Q}\left|g\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\iint_{\alpha Q}\left|u\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

for all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{1+n}
such that
\alpha\overline{Q}\subseteq\Omega
, where the implicit constant depends on
\alpha.

This type of Caccioppoli inequality can be proven using the same argument as in the proof of
[
51
, Proposition 5.1]
.
Moreover, if
g\equiv 0
, and
\delta\in(0,\infty)
, then the reverse Hölder estimate

(7.3)

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{Q}\left|u\right|^{2^{*}_{+}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2^{*}_{+}}\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{\alpha Q}\left|u\right|^{\delta}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/\delta}

holds (see
[
51
, Proposition 5.2]
or
[
11
, Lemma 16.7]
), where the implicit constant depends on
\alpha
and
\delta
, and
2^{*}_{+}=\frac{2(n+1)}{(n+1)-2}
is the upper Sobolev exponent in dimension
n+1
.

The following result will be crucial for constructing solutions in Section
9
. The proof shows how the argument outlined in
[
11
, Lemma 16.8]
can be adapted to the case of the Schrödinger equations considered here.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be open, and let
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
. Let
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a sequence of weak solutions of
\mathscr{H}u_{k}=g
in
\Omega.
Let
u\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
such that
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
as
k\to\infty
. Then
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=g
in
\Omega
, and
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

Let
k,j\geq 1
be integers. By linearity,
u_{k}-u_{j}\in\mathcal{V}_{\textup{loc}}^{1,2}(\Omega)
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}(u_{k}-u_{j})=0
in
\Omega
.
Let
Q\subset\Omega
be any cube such that
\overline{Q}\subset\Omega
. Then, there is
\alpha>1
such that
\alpha\overline{Q}\subseteq\Omega
. By Caccioppoli’s inequality (
7.2
) and Hölder’s inequality, we have (where
2^{*}_{+}=\frac{2n+2}{n-1}
)

\displaystyle\ell(Q)\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{Q}\left|\nabla_{\mu}u_{k}-\nabla_{\mu}u_{j}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{\alpha^{1/2}Q}\left|u_{k}-u_{j}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{\alpha^{1/2}Q}\left|u_{k}-u_{j}\right|^{2^{*}_{+}}\right)^{1/2^{*}_{+}}.

Hence, the reverse Hölder estimate (
7.3
) with
\delta=1
implies that

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{Q}\left|u_{k}-u_{j}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{Q}\left|\nabla_{\mu}u_{k}-\nabla_{\mu}u_{j}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left(1+\ell(Q)^{-1}\right)\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{\alpha Q}\left|u_{k}-u_{j}\right|.

By a covering argument, this shows that for any open set
O\subset\Omega
with compact closure
\overline{O}\subseteq\Omega
(henceforth denoted
O\Subset\Omega
), there exists
C>0
, depending on
O
, and an open set
O^{\prime}
such that
O\subset O^{\prime}\Subset\Omega
and

\left\lVert u_{k}-u_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(O)}\leq C\left\lVert u_{k}-u_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}(O^{\prime})}.

This holds for any
j,k\geq 1
, and since
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
converges in
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
, this shows that
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
. Let
v\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
be such that
u_{k}\to v
in
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
as
k\to\infty
. Since
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
, we deduce that
u=v
almost everywhere in
\Omega
, thus
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\Omega)
. Finally, we can take the limit as
k\to\infty
in the weak formulation of
\mathscr{H}u_{k}=g
to conclude that
u
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=g
in
\Omega
.
∎

7.1.
Poisson semigroup solutions

As stated in the introduction, block coefficients allow us to rewrite equation (
1.2
) in the form
\partial_{t}^{2}u=Hu
, which allows us to construct solutions to boundary value problems for
\mathrm{L}^{2}
boundary data by considering the Poisson semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH^{1/2}}\right\}_{t>0}
. We now make this precise.

Since
H
is an injective sectorial operator of type
S_{2\omega(B)}^{+}
, we know that its square root
H^{1/2}
is an injective sectorial operator of type
S_{\omega(B)}^{+}
(see, e.g.,
[
38
, Propositions 3.1.1 and 3.1.2]
) which generates a holomorphic semigroup
\left\{e^{-\lambda H^{1/2}}\right\}_{\lambda\in S_{\frac{\pi}{2}-\omega(B)}^{+}}
,
satisfying the properties stated in
[
38
, Proposition 3.4.1]
. Moreover,
[
38
, Proposition 3.1.4]
shows that for all
\lambda\in S^{+}_{\frac{\pi}{2}-\omega(B)}
, it holds that
e^{-\lambda H^{1/2}}=f_{\lambda}(H^{1/2})=g_{\lambda}(H)
, where
f_{\lambda}(z)=e^{-\lambda z}
and
g_{\lambda}(z)=e^{-\lambda\sqrt{z}}
for all
z\in S^{+}_{\pi}
.

These properties show that for fixed
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the mapping

(7.4)

\mathrm{P}:(0,\infty)\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):t\mapsto e^{-tH^{1/2}}f

is continuous and satisfies
\left\lVert P(t)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
uniformly for all
t>0
. A slight variation of the argument of the proof of
[
4
, Lemma 2.2]
shows that there exists a (measurable)
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
such that
u(t,x)=\mathrm{P}(t)(x)
for almost every
t\in(0,\infty)
and almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This is how the notation
u(t,x):=(e^{-tH^{1/2}}f)(x)
should be understood in Theorem
7.2
below, and in the rest of the paper. In particular,
u(t,\cdot):=\mathrm{P}(t)
for all
t>0
.

Note that in what follows, for a normed space
X
, we let
C_{0}([0,\infty);X)
denote the space of continuous
X
-valued functions on
[0,\infty)
vanishing at
\infty
, equipped with the supremum norm.
To avoid confusion, we also mention that for any measurable function
v:\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
, the notation
v\in C_{0}\left([0,\infty);X\right)
, for a subspace
X
of
\mathbb{C}
-valued measurable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, means that there exists some
F\in C_{0}\left([0,\infty);X\right)
such that
v(t,x)=F(t)(x)
for almost every
t>0
and almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This notation extends to the spaces different from
C_{0}
used below.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
u:\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
be defined as
u(t,x):=(e^{-tH^{1/2}}f)(x)

for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then,
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, and it is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
. Moreover, it is of class
C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{2})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{2})
.

Proof.

The general properties of holomorphic semigroups imply that
u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{2})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{2})
, with
\partial_{t}^{k}u(t,\cdot)=(-1)^{k}H^{k/2}u(t,\cdot)
in the strong sense in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
for all
t>0
.
Moreover, for all
t>0
, we can use (
2.21
) to write

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}u(t,\cdot)\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}e^{-tH^{1/2}}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}=B\begin{bmatrix}e^{-t\widetilde{H}^{1/2}}(b^{-1}f)\\
0\end{bmatrix}=Be^{-t\sqrt{(DB)^{2}}}\begin{bmatrix}b^{-1}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}=Be^{-t[DB]}\begin{bmatrix}b^{-1}f\\
0\end{bmatrix},

where we have defined
[z]:=\sqrt{z^{2}}
for all
z\in S_{\pi/2}
. Consequently, since
\mathsf{R}(e^{-t[DB]})\subseteq\mathsf{D}([DB])=\mathsf{D}(DB)
, we may apply the operator
D
to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
t\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)\end{bmatrix}=-tDBe^{-t[DB]}\begin{bmatrix}b^{-1}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

Since
t\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot)=-tH^{1/2}e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
, we may write for all
t>0
,

(7.5)

t\nabla_{\mu}u(t,\cdot)=\begin{bmatrix}t\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot)\\
t\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\psi(t^{2}H)f\\
\left(\varphi(tDB)g\right)_{\parallel,\mu}\end{bmatrix},

where
\psi(z)=-\sqrt{z}e^{-\sqrt{z}}
,
\varphi(z)=-ze^{-[z]}
, and
g:=\begin{bmatrix}b^{-1}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}
. It follows from (
7.5
) and the (baby) convergence lemma
[
38
, Lemma 5.12]
that the map

(0,\infty)\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2}):t\mapsto\nabla_{\mu}u(t,\cdot)

is continuous, and each of its
n+2
components can therefore be identified with an element of
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, as follows from the argument following (
7.4
) above. It follows that
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
has weak derivatives in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, thus
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
.

We now prove that
u
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
. Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
and observe that the slices
\phi(t,\cdot)
belong to
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
t>0
. Since
\partial_{t}^{2}u(t,\cdot)=Hu(t,\cdot)
in the strong sense in
\mathrm{L}^{2}
for all
t>0
, and
b^{-1}=A_{\perp\perp}
is independent of
t
and bounded, we may use the form definition (
2.15
) of the operator
H
to obtain

(7.6)

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}^{2}\left(A_{\perp\perp}u\right),\phi(t,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle=\langle b^{-1}Hu(t,\cdot),\phi(t,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle A\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot),\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}\phi(t,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})}

for each
t>0
. Since both sides of (
7.6
) are continuous functions of the variable
t
, and since
\phi
has compact support, we may integrate with respect to
t
to obtain

(7.7)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle\partial_{t}^{2}\left(A_{\perp\perp}u\right),\phi(t,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle A\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot),\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}\phi(t,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.

Using Fubini’s theorem, we may write the integral on the left-hand side as

\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle\partial_{t}^{2}\left(A_{\perp\perp}u\right),\phi(t,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}A_{\perp\perp}(x)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{t}^{2}u(t,x)\overline{\phi(t,x)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

We may integrate by parts the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-valued Bochner integral to obtain

\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{t}^{2}u(t,\cdot)\overline{\phi(t,\cdot)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot)\overline{\partial_{t}\phi(t,\cdot)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t
.
Applying Fubini’s theorem again, (
7.7
) becomes

-\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot),\partial_{t}\phi(t,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle A\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot),\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}\phi(t,\cdot)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t,

which means that
u
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
.
∎

8.
Estimates for Poisson semigroup solutions

Henceforth we assume that
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and as always, let
\delta=2-\frac{n}{q}
. In this section, we shall derive the important square function and non-tangential maximal function bounds on Poisson semigroup solutions which will be central in the existence part of the proofs of Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
.

8.1.
Dirichlet data

We first consider estimates for the Dirichlet problem.

8.1.1.
Square function estimates

The following result is adapted from
[
11
, Proposition 17.1]
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
p\in(p_{-}(H)\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}\,,\,p_{+}(H)^{*})
. If
f\in b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
, then
u(t,\cdot)=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
satisfies

(8.1)

\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}.

Proof.

We first treat the case
p_{-}(H)\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}<p\leq 2
. We note that the functions
\psi
and
\varphi
in (
7.5
) are such that
\psi\in\Psi^{\infty}_{1/2}
on any sector, and
\varphi\in\Psi^{\infty}_{1}
on any bisector. Moreover, the functions
\psi
and
\varphi
are admissible for the definition of the adapted spaces
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}
and
\mathbb{H}_{DB}^{p}
, respectively. By Corollary
6.11
, the assumption on
p
implies that
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}=b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
and thus for
f\in b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
we have

\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}}\eqsim\left\lVert S\left(\psi(t^{2}H)f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}=\left\lVert S\left(t\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot)\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\leq\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}},

and if
h=\begin{bmatrix}b^{-1}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}
, then

\displaystyle\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert S\left(\varphi(tDB)h\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert\begin{bmatrix}b^{-1}f\\
0\end{bmatrix}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}}\eqsim\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{\widetilde{H}}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}}\eqsim\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}},

where we have used the decomposition
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{DB}=\mathbb{H}^{p}_{\widetilde{H}}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{p}_{\widetilde{M}}
from Section
2.5.4
, and the similarity between
H
and
\widetilde{H}
from Section
2.5.3
.
Those two estimates show that (
8.1
) holds.

Next, we prove the upper bound in (
8.1
) for
2<p<p_{+}(H)^{*}
. Consider the function
\phi(z):=e^{-\sqrt{z}}-(1+z)^{-2}
and define
v(t,\cdot):=\phi(t^{2}H)f
for all
t>0
, so we have

A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}^{2}v-\mathscr{H}v(t,\cdot)=t^{-2}A_{\perp\perp}\eta(t^{2}H)f,

where
\eta(z):=z(1+z)^{-2}+4z(1+z)^{-3}-24z^{2}(1+z)^{-4}
. Note that
\eta\in\Psi^{1}_{1}
on any sector.
If
g(t,x):=-t^{-2}A_{\perp\perp}(x)\left(\eta(t^{2}H)f\right)(x)
, then
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, and the argument of the proof of Theorem
7.2
implies that
v\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}v=g
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
. Following the argument of the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.1]
, applying Caccioppoli’s inequality (
7.2
) gives

\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert S\left(\phi(t^{2}H)f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}+\left\lVert S\left(\psi(t^{2}H)f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}+\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-2}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

Since
\phi\in\Psi^{2}_{1/2}
and
\eta\in\Psi^{2}_{1}
on any sector, applying
[
11
, Theorem 9.22]
with
\sigma=1/2
gives

\left\lVert S\left(\phi(t^{2}H)f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}+\left\lVert S\left(\psi(t^{2}H)f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}},

for all
2\leq p<\frac{np_{+}(H)}{n-p_{+}(H)}=p_{+}(H)^{*}
, with the understanding that
p_{+}(H)^{*}=\infty
if
p_{+}(H)\geq n
.
The remaining estimate,

\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}(1+t^{2}H)^{-2}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
,
holds for any
p\in[2,\infty)
and is obtained as in the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.1]
, by applying
[
11
, Remark 9.9]
and (
5.1
).

Finally, we prove the lower bound for
2<p<p_{+}(H)^{*}
. We follow the duality argument used in Step 3 of the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.1]
by introducing the adapted Schrödinger operator
\widetilde{H}_{0}:=b^{*}H_{0}
. Let
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
and
g\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
, and consider the infinitely differentiable function

\phi:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{C}:t\mapsto\langle A_{\perp\perp}e^{-tH^{1/2}}f,e^{-t\widetilde{H}_{0}^{1/2}}g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.

The smoothness of
\phi
follows from the properties of holomorphic semigroups on
\mathrm{L}^{2}
.
Moreover, properties of the holomorphic functional calculus, and in particular the bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus of
H
and
\widetilde{H}_{0}
, justify the identity

\displaystyle\langle A_{\perp\perp}f,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\varepsilon}^{1/\varepsilon}t^{2}\phi^{\prime\prime}(t)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}.

Following the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.1]
, replacing
\nabla_{x}
with
\nabla_{\mu}
and
L
with
H
, and using that
\nabla_{\mu}=\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{t}\\
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}\end{bmatrix}
, we get
\left|\langle A_{\perp\perp}f,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|\lesssim\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}e^{-tH^{1/2}}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}e^{-t\widetilde{H}_{0}^{1/2}}g\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}}
.
Now, note that
p_{-}(\widetilde{H}_{0})\vee 1=p_{-}(H_{0})\vee 1=1
. Indeed, the first equality is proved exactly as in
[
11
, Theorem 6.9]
, and the second equality follows from the
\mathrm{L}^{p}
-boundedness of the heat semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH_{0}}\right\}_{t>0}
in (
3.2
) for all
p\in[1,\infty]
. We can therefore apply (
8.1
) to
\widetilde{H}_{0}
and
p^{\prime}\in(1,2]
to get

\left|\langle A_{\perp\perp}f,g\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|\lesssim\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}e^{-tH^{1/2}}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}}.

Since
g\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{\prime}}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
was arbitrary, this concludes the proof.
∎

8.1.2.
Non-tangential maximal functions estimates

We next turn to the non-tangential maximal function bounds. We need the following result from the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-theory, which is an elaboration of
[
51
, Theorem 5.8, Proposition 5.9]
.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
T\in\left\{BD,DB\right\}
. Then, for all
f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(T)}
it holds that

(8.2)

\left\lVert N_{*}\left(e^{-t[T]}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

and

(8.3)

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|e^{-s[T]}f-f(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proof.

Let us remark that the reference
[
51
, Theorem 5.8]
only proves the non-tangential maximal function estimate (
8.2
) for
T=DB
and
f\in\mathcal{H}^{+}_{DB}
, a spectral subspace of
\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}
. Let us first discuss how the proof can be adapted to cover the general case
f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}
.

First, we note that the lower bound in (
8.2
) follows from exactly the same argument as in the start of the proof of
[
51
, Proposition 5.8]
. In fact, let
f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}
and
F(t)=e^{-t[DB]}f
for all
t>0
. Since
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\left\lVert F(t)-f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=0
, it follows from
[
51
, Lemma 5.7]
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{t}^{2t}\left\lVert F(s)\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\left\lVert N_{*}F\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}

as claimed. Let us now focus on the reverse estimate. We recall that the spectral subspaces
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{DB}
,
\mathcal{H}^{-}_{DB}\subset\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}
are defined using the bounded
\textup{H}^{\infty}
-calculus of
DB
as
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}_{DB}:=\left\{\chi^{\pm}(DB)f\;:\;f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}\right\}
,
where
\chi^{\pm}:S_{\pi/2}\to\mathbb{C}
are defined as

\chi^{\pm}(z)=\begin{cases}1,&\textup{ if }\pm\operatorname{Re}{{(}}z)>0;\\
0,&\textup{ if }\pm\operatorname{Re}{{(}}z)<0,\end{cases}

for all
z\in S_{\pi/2}
. As in
[
51
, Section 4.2]
we introduce the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}:=\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}=\overline{\mathsf{R}(D)}
. There is a topological direct sum decomposition
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{+}_{DB}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{-}_{DB}
.

For
f\in\mathcal{H}^{-}_{DB}
, the function
F\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}^{-}_{DB})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathcal{H})
defined as
F(t):=e^{-t[DB]}f
satisfies the first-order equation
\partial_{t}F(t)-DBF(t)=0
, for all
t>0
, in the strong sense in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
(see the proof of
[
51
, Proposition 4.4]
). Let us consider the reflection
G\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}((-\infty,0]);\mathcal{H}^{-}_{DB})\cap C^{\infty}((-\infty,0);\mathcal{H})
defined as
G(t)=F(-t)
for all
t\leq 0
. This new function
G
satisfies the first-order equation
\partial_{t}G(t)+DBG(t)=0
for all
t<0
, in the strong sense. Using
t
-independence of the coefficients
A
,
a
and
V
, and by using the argument of the proof of
[
51
, Proposition 4.3 (2)]
, we can obtain a function
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{-})
, defined on the lower half-space
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{-}
, such that
G=\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mu}u
and
\mathscr{H}u=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{-}.
The notation
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
is introduced at the start of
[
51
, Section 4.1]
.

Let us now consider an arbitrary Whitney cube
W:=(t,2t)\times Q(x,t)
with
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
, and its reflection
-W:=(-2t,-t)\times Q(x,t)
. Let us now choose
p<2
as in the proof of
[
51
, Theorem 5.8]
. We can therefore apply the reverse Hölder inequality
[
51
, Proposition 5.3]
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{-}
to obtain,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W}\left|F\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{-W}\left|G\right|^{2}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{-W}\left|\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mu}u\right|^{2}\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{-W}\left|\nabla_{\mu}u\right|^{2}\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{-2W}\left|\nabla_{\mu}u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{-2W}\left|\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mu}u\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{-2W}\left|G\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}=\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{2W}\left|F\right|^{p}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}.

We can then follow the rest of the proof of
[
51
, Theorem 5.8]
to obtain the estimate
\left\lVert N_{*}F\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}
. The desired conclusion (
8.2
) for a general
f\in\mathcal{H}
then follows from the topological decomposition
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{+}_{DB}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{-}_{DB}
.

The result for
T=BD
(more precisely, its injective part, i.e. its restriction to
\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}
) and
f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}
can be obtained as follows. First, note that the lower bound
\left\lVert N_{*}\left(e^{-t[T]}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\gtrsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
follows from exactly the same argument as in the
DB
case. Next, we can observe that if
f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(BD)}
, then
g:=(B|_{\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}})^{-1}f\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(DB)}
with
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\eqsim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
, and the similarity (
2.22
) between
DB
and
BD
implies that
e^{-t[BD]}f=Be^{-t[DB]}g
for all
t>0
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\left\lVert N_{*}\left(e^{-t[BD]}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert N_{*}\left(Be^{-t[DB]}g\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert N_{*}\left(e^{-t[DB]}g\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

follows from the boundedness of
B
and the result for
DB
.

Similarly, we should note that the reference
[
51
, Proposition 5.9]
only proves the non-tangential convergence (
8.3
) for
T=DB
. However, the similarity argument above allows us to deduce the result for
BD
from that of
DB
. Indeed, we can use boundedness of the multiplication operator
B
and the triangle inequality to obtain, for arbitrary
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|e^{-s[BD]}f-f(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|Be^{-s[DB]}g-(Bg)(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|(e^{-s[DB]}g)(y)-g(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x,t)}\left|g(x)-g(y)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x,t)}\left|(Bg)(y)-(Bg)(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

Since
g
and
Bg=f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
, the conclusion follows from Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem and the result for
DB
.
∎

In order to prove the last part of Proposition
8.4
below, we shall need the following lemma.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t>0
. If
g\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0})
and
\textup{supp}\left(g\right)\subseteq B(x,t)
with
\left\lVert H_{0}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{-n-2}
, then
\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{-n-1}
.

Proof.

Let
L=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:x=y\right\}
and let
\Gamma^{V}:(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})\setminus L\to\mathbb{R}
denote the fundamental solution of the operator
H_{0}=-\Delta+V
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We refer to
[
48
, Section 5]
for its construction and important properties, including that
\Gamma^{V}(\cdot,y)\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\left\{y\right\})
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since
H_{0}g\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it follows from
[
48
, Theorem 5.17]
that

\displaystyle g(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Gamma^{V}(z,y)\left(H_{0}g\right)(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

for almost every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Also, as
V\in\textup{RH}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
[
55
, Theorem 2.7, Remark 4.9]
shows that

\displaystyle\left|\Gamma^{V}(z,y)\right|\lesssim(1+\left|z-y\right|m(z))^{-1}\left|z-y\right|^{-(n-2)},\hskip 28.45274pt\left|\nabla_{z}\Gamma^{V}(z,y)\right|\lesssim\left|z-y\right|^{-(n-1)},

for all
y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
y\neq z
. Since
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the first estimate above can be combined with the inequality
V(z)\lesssim\rho(z)^{-2}
(see (
2.5
)) to obtain
\left|V^{1/2}(z)\Gamma^{V}(z,y)\right|\lesssim\left|z-y\right|^{-(n-1)}

for all
y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
y\neq z
. Altogether, for all
z\in B(x,t)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\nabla_{\mu}g(z)\right|\lesssim\int_{B(x,t)}\left|z-y\right|^{-(n-1)}t^{-n-2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\leq t^{-n-2}\int_{B(z,2t)}\left|z-y\right|^{-(n-1)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\lesssim t^{-n-2}t=t^{-n-1}.

Since
\nabla_{\mu}g
is supported in
B(x,t)
, this finishes the proof.
∎

We have the following proposition, the proof of which is adapted from
[
11
, Proposition 17.3]
.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
p\in(p_{-}(H)\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}\,,\,p_{+}(H)^{*})
. If
f\in b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
, then
u(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
satisfies the estimate
\left\lVert N_{*}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}

and the non-tangential limit

(8.4)

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)-f(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
p>1
, or if
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}
and
\frac{n}{n+1}<p\leq 1
, then the reverse estimate also holds.

Proof.

For
p\leq 2
, these are obtained as in the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.3]
, using Theorem
8.2
(instead of
[
11
, Theorem 17.2]
),
[
11
, Proposition 8.27]
, and Corollary
6.11
.

For
2<p<p_{+}(H)^{*}
, the estimate is proved exactly as in
[
11
, Proposition 17.3]
.
In addition, when
1<p<p_{+}(H)^{*}
, the reverse estimate follows from the fact that the non-tangential limit implies that
\left|f(x)\right|\leq N_{*}(u)(x)
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Finally, let us assume that
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}
. We shall prove that
\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert N_{*}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
for
\frac{n}{n+1}<p\leq 1
and
f\in b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
\chi:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
be smooth, such that
\chi(s)=1
for
s\in[0,1/2]
, and
\chi(s)=0
for
s\geq 2
. For all
t>0
, we consider the function
\chi_{t}\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,\infty))
defined as
\chi_{t}(s):=\chi(\frac{s}{t})
for all
s\geq 0
.
Let us also pick
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
real-valued, even, and such that
\varphi(0)\neq 0
, with Fourier transform
\hat{\varphi}
supported near the origin, so that the integral operators
\varphi(tH_{0}^{1/2})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{L}^{2})
,
t>0
, have integral kernels
K_{t}(x,y)
satisfying
K_{t}(x,y)=0
for
\left|x-y\right|\geq t
, by
[
44
, Lemma 3.5]
.
It follows from
[
45
, Proposition 5.3]
that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t>0
, the function
K_{t}(x,\cdot)
belongs to
\cap_{m\geq 1}\mathsf{D}(H_{0}^{m})\subseteq\mathsf{D}(H_{0})
.

For fixed
t>0
, let us introduce the function
\Phi^{t}:[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
defined as
\Phi^{t}(s,x,y):=K_{t}(x,y)\chi_{t}(s)
. Following the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.3]
we obtain for fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\displaystyle\left|\left[\varphi(tH_{0}^{1/2})(b^{-1}f)\right](x)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}}\left|u(s,y)\right|\left|\partial_{s}\Phi^{t}(s,x,y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}}\left|s\partial_{s}\Phi^{t}(s,x,y)\right|\left|\partial_{s}u(s,y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}}\left|s\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(s,y)\right|\left|\nabla_{\mu,y}^{\parallel}\Phi^{t}(s,x,y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=:\textup{I}+\textup{II}+\textup{III}.

The notation
\nabla_{\mu,y}^{\parallel}
refers to the operator
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}
acting with respect to the variable
y
. To estimate terms
I
and
II
, we can use the support properties of
K_{t}(x,\cdot)
and the derivative
\chi^{\prime}
, as well as the uniform bound
\left|K_{t}(x,y)\right|\lesssim t^{-n}
(see
[
45
, Corollary 3.5]
), to obtain (recall
W(t,x):=(t/2,2t)\times B(x,t)
)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{s}\Phi^{t}(s,x,y)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|t^{-1}\chi^{\prime}\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)K_{t}(x,y)\right|\lesssim t^{-n-1}\mathbbm{1}_{B(x,t)}(y)\mathbbm{1}_{(\frac{t}{2},2t)}(s)\lesssim\frac{1}{\left\lvert W(t,x)\right\rvert}\mathbbm{1}_{W(t,x)}(s,y)

for all
s>0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. To estimate term
III
, we note that the function
F_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
,
F(y):=K_{t}(x,y)
, satisfies
F_{t}\in\mathsf{D}(H_{0})
,
\textup{supp}\left(F_{t}\right)\subseteq B(x,t)
and
\left\lVert H_{0}F_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{-n-2}
by
[
45
, Corollary 3.5]
. It follows from Lemma
8.3
that
\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}F_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{-n-1}
.
Since
\nabla_{\mu}F_{t}
has compact support in
B(x,t)
, this implies the uniform estimate

(8.5)

\displaystyle\left|\nabla_{\mu,y}^{\parallel}\Phi^{t}(s,x,y)\right|\lesssim t^{-n-1}\mathbbm{1}_{B(x,t)}(y)\mathbbm{1}_{(0,2t)}(s)

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
s>0
. By Lemma
3.5
, these estimates allow us to conclude the proof as in
[
11
, Proposition 17.3]
, using Caccioppoli’s inequality (
7.2
) in the final step.
∎

8.1.3.
Uniform estimates and strong continuity

Next, we establish uniform bounds in
t>0
, and strong continuity at
t=0
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
. As in
[
11
, Proposition 17.4]
, the following result follows from the identification
\mathbb{H}_{H}^{p}=b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
from Theorem
6.1
.

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
p\in(p_{-}(H)\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}\,,\,p_{+}(H))
. If
f\in b\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
and
u(t,\cdot)=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
, then
b^{-1}u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}})
with
u(0,\cdot)=f
, and satisfies

\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert b^{-1}u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\quad\textup{and}\quad\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert t^{k}\partial_{t}^{k}\left(b^{-1}u(t,\cdot)\right)\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim k^{k}e^{-k}\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}

for all integers
k\geq 1
.

For exponents
p\in[p_{+}(H),p_{+}(H)^{*})
, we don’t have the identification
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}=\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
, but we can still obtain some control of the solution in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The relevant estimate is obtained by following the method outlined in
[
11
, Lemma 17.5]
. This yields the following result.

Proposition 8.6
.

If
p\in[p_{+}(H),p_{+}(H)^{*})
, then for all
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
, all balls
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
t>0
, it holds that

\left\lVert e^{-tH^{1/2}}f-f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B)}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\left(1+\frac{t}{r_{B}}\right)\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}},

and
\left\lVert e^{-tH^{1/2}}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(B)}\lesssim\left\lvert B\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\left(2+\frac{t}{r_{B}}\right)\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
.

8.2.
Regularity data

We now consider estimates for the Regularity problem.

8.2.1.
Square function estimates

The following result is adapted from
[
11
, Proposition 17.6]
.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}
and
u(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
. The following properties hold:

(i)

If
p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*},q_{+}(H))\cap(1,2q]
and
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}\cap\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}
, then
\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
.

(ii)

If
p\in\left(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+1},1\right]
and
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
, then
\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}
.

(iii)

If
p\in\left(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2},1\right]\cap\mathcal{I}(V)
and
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
, then
\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\gtrsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}
.

Proof.

For the first item, we note that the assumptions on
p
imply that
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}=\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}
and
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}=\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
with equivalent
p
-norms (see Theorem
6.1
).
We can now follow the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.6]
, using Figure
1
and the intertwining relations of Lemma
2.15
to obtain the desired conclusion.
For the remaining items, we use the continuous inclusion
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})
from Lemma
6.10
and the converse inclusion from Proposition
6.8
along with the argument of the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.6]
.
∎

8.2.2.
Non-tangential maximal function estimates

We now turn to the non-tangential maximal function bounds for the Regularity problem.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee 1\,,\,q_{+}(H))
with
p\leq 2q
. If
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}\cap\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}
, then
u(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
satisfies
\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

and

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|\begin{bmatrix}A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u\\
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}-(A_{\perp\perp}H^{1/2}f)(x)\\
\nabla_{\mu}f(x)\end{bmatrix}\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.7]
, using Lemma
2.15
, Theorem
6.1
and (
2.18
). We also use Theorem
8.2
instead of
[
11
, Theorem 17.2]
.
∎

For
p\leq 1
, we have the following analogous result.

Proposition 8.9
.

Let
p\in\left(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+1}\,,\,1\right]
. If
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
, then
u(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
satisfies the estimate

\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}
, and

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|\begin{bmatrix}A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u\\
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}-(A_{\perp\perp}H^{1/2}f)(x)\\
\nabla_{\mu}f(x)\end{bmatrix}\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}
and
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)
, then the reverse estimate also holds.

Proof.

We follow the proof of the previous proposition, using
[
11
, Proposition 8.27]
in the range
p\leq 1
, as well as the norm equivalences of Figure
1
. It then remains to prove the estimate
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
, which we claim holds for all
\frac{n}{n+1}<p\leq 1
, under the assumption that
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}
and
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)
.
We follow and adapt the third step of the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.7]
. Recall the function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
and, for fixed
t>0
, the function
\Phi^{t}
from the proof of Proposition
8.4
.
For
t>0
, we let
\varphi(tH_{0}^{1/2})
act componentwise on
\nabla_{\mu}f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+1})
. By the intertwining relations of Proposition
2.15
, we note that
u(t,\cdot)\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)=e^{-t\tilde{M}^{1/2}}\left(\nabla_{\mu}f\right)
, and therefore
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)=e^{-t\tilde{M}^{1/2}}\left(\nabla_{\mu}f\right)\to\nabla_{\mu}f
as
t\to 0
, in the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
norm. We can therefore follow the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 17.7]
to obtain that for all fixed
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
it holds that

\displaystyle\left|\varphi(tH_{0}^{1/2})\left(\nabla_{\mu}f\right)(x)\right|\leq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}}\left|\partial_{s}\Phi^{t}\;\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(s,y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}}\left|\partial_{s}u\;\nabla_{\mu,y}^{\parallel}\Phi^{t}(x,y,s)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.

Since
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}
, we can apply the pointwise estimate (
8.5
) and conclude the proof as in
[
11
, Proposition 17.7]
to obtain (recall Lemma
3.5
)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert\mathcal{M}_{V}(\nabla_{\mu}f\,,\,\varphi)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

The conclusion follows from the assumption
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)
(see Section
4.2
).
∎

8.2.3.
Uniform estimates and strong continuity

Finally, we follow
[
11
, Proposition 17.8]
to obtain uniform boundedness and strong continuity at
t=0
.

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee 1\,,\,q_{+}(H))
with
p\leq 2q
. If
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
, then
u(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
has the following properties:

(i)

It holds that
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
, with

\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\quad\textup{and}\quad\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert t^{k}\partial_{t}^{k}\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim k^{k}e^{-k}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all integers
k\geq 1
.

(ii)

If
p<n
and
V\not\equiv 0
, then
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
and
u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})
, with

\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}.

Proof.

Since
f\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the intertwining relations (Lemma
2.15
) imply that
u(t,\cdot)\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for each
t\geq 0
, with

\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)=\nabla_{\mu}\left(e^{-tH^{1/2}}f\right)=e^{-t\widetilde{M}^{1/2}}(\nabla_{\mu}f).

The argument of the proof of Proposition
8.7
shows that, in the range of exponents
p
considered, it holds that
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{\widetilde{M}}}\eqsim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
for all
g\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{\widetilde{M}}\cap\mathsf{R}(\nabla_{\mu})
. Moreover, it shows that
\nabla_{\mu}f\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{\widetilde{M}}
. We can therefore apply the abstract theory of
[
11
, Propositions 8.10 and 8.13]
to the sectorial operator
\widetilde{M}
, as we did in the proof of Proposition
8.5
, in order to obtain the first item.

Next, if we assume that
p<n
and
V\not\equiv 0
, then Proposition
2.7
shows that there is a continuous embedding
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
.
The first item in the proposition means that
u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p})
with the boundary limit
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}u(t,\cdot)=f
in the
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}
norm. Together with the embedding this implies the required regularity.
Moreover, since
u(t,\cdot)\to f
in
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
as
t\to 0
, we obtain
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\leq\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}
.
In addition, it follows from Proposition
4.4
and Theorem
6.1
that
\mathbb{H}^{p^{*}}_{H}=\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
with equivalent
p^{*}
-norms. Therefore, the sectorial counterpart of
[
11
, Proposition 8.10]
shows that
\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}
, uniformly for all
t>0
. We also note that the embedding
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
implies that
\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
for all
t>0
.
∎

In the case
p\leq 1
, we have the following analogous result.

Proposition 8.11
.

Let
p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+1},1]
. If
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}
, then
u(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
has the following properties:

(i)

It holds that
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
and
u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})
, with

\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}.

(ii)

If
p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2},1]\cap\,\mathcal{I}(V)
, then
u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}})
, with

\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}\quad\textup{and}\quad\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert t^{k}\partial_{t}^{k}u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}\lesssim k^{k}e^{-k}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}

for all integers
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

The first item follows from the identification
\mathbb{H}^{p^{*}}_{H}=\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
, as in the proof of the second part of Proposition
8.10
above. The second item uses the identification
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}=\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
with equivalent
p
-(quasi)norms (see Corollary
6.11
), along with the abstract theory of
[
11
, Propositions 8.10 and 8.13]
for the sectorial operator
H
, as in the proof of Proposition
8.5
.
∎

9.
Solvability and estimates for Theorems
1.2
and
1.3

With the estimates of Section
8
at hand, we shall now be able to prove the existence results for the Dirichlet and Regularity problems. Throughout this section, we assume that
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\left\{\frac{n}{2},2\right\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

9.1.
The Dirichlet problem

The following result contains solvability and estimates for Theorem
1.2
. We follow the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 1.1]
outlined in
[
11
, Section 17.3]
. For notational convenience, we introduce
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with the corresponding norm, for
p\in(1,\infty)
. This is in accordance with Lemma
3.3
.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Suppose that
p\geq 1
and
p\in(p_{-}(H),p_{+}(H)^{*})
. If either
p>1
and
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}
, or
p=1
and
f\in b\textup{H}^{1}_{V}
, then the Dirichlet problem
\left(\mathcal{D}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
for data
f\in b\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
admits a weak solution
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, with the following additional properties:

(i)
