There is comparability:
\left\lVert N_{*}(u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
.
If either
p>1
, or
p=1
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}
, then the reverse non-tangential maximal function bound holds.

(ii)

The non-tangential convergence improves to
\mathrm{L}^{2}
averages:

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)-f(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0\text{ for almost every }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(iii)

If
p<p_{+}\left(H\right)
, then
b^{-1}u\in C_{0}\left(\left[0,\infty\right);\textup{H}^{p}_{V}\right)\cap C^{\infty}\left((0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V}\right)
, where
t
is the distinguished variable, and
u(0,\cdot)=f
, with
\sup_{t\geq 0}\left\lVert b^{-1}u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
.

(iv)

If
p\geq p_{+}(H)
, then for all
T>0
and all compact subsets
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it holds that
u\in C\left([0,T];\mathrm{L}^{2}(K)\right)
, with
u(0,\cdot)=\left.f\right|_{K}
, and
\sup_{0<t\leq T}\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(K)}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
,
where the implicit constant depends on
T
and
K
.

Proof.

Let
f\in b\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
. If
1<p<\infty
, then
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and we pick a sequence
(f_{k})_{k}\subset\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
such that
f_{k}\to f
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}
. If
p=1
, we choose a sequence
(f_{k})_{k}\subset b\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
b^{-1}f_{k}\to b^{-1}f
in
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, hence also in
\mathrm{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(see the definition of
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}
in Section
3.1.2
). For each
k\geq 1
, we obtain a weak solution
u_{k}\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
corresponding to the boundary data
f_{k}
, given by the Poisson extension
u_{k}(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f_{k}
(see Theorem
7.2
). For all
j,k\geq 1
, it follows from linearity of
e^{-tH^{1/2}}
that

u_{k,j}:=u_{k}(t,\cdot)-u_{j}(t,\cdot)=e^{-tH^{1/2}}(f_{k}-f_{j}).

Consequently, it follows from Propositions
8.1
and
8.4
that for all
j,k\geq 1
it holds that

\displaystyle\left\lVert u_{k}-u_{j}\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert N_{*}(u_{k}-u_{j})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert b^{-1}(f_{k}-f_{j})\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert t\nabla_{\mu}(u_{k}-u_{j})\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}.

It follows that the sequence
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
and that the sequence
(t\nabla_{\mu}u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\textup{T}^{p}
. By completeness, there are
u\in\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
and
v\in\textup{T}^{p}
such that
u_{k}\to u
in
\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
and
t\nabla_{\mu}u_{k}\to v
in
\textup{T}^{p}
. Both convergences also hold in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
. Lemma
7.1
shows that
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
is a weak solution in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
, and that
u_{k}\to u
in the
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
topology. Consequently,
v=t\nabla_{\mu}u
. We can therefore pass to the limit in the estimates to obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}}=\left\lVert N_{*}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert S\left(t\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}=\left\lVert t\nabla_{\mu}u\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{p}}.

The last statement of Proposition
8.4
shows that the reverse estimate
\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert N_{*}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
holds if
p>1
, and it also holds for
p=1
if
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}
. This proves (i).

To prove (iii), let us now assume that
p<p_{+}(H)
. By Proposition
8.5
, for each
k,j\geq 1
, it holds that
b^{-1}(u_{k}-u_{j})\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}})\cap\,C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}})
in the variable
t
, with

\displaystyle\left\lVert b^{-1}(u_{k}-u_{j})\right\rVert_{C_{0}\left([0,\infty);\,\textup{H}^{p}_{V,\textup{pre}}\right)}:=\sup_{t\geq 0}\left\lVert b^{-1}(u_{k}(t,\cdot)-u_{j}(t,\cdot))\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert b^{-1}(f_{k}-f_{j})\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}.

Since
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is complete, the space
C_{0}([0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V})
is a Banach space, hence there is
w\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V})
such that
b^{-1}u_{k}\to w
in
C_{0}([0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V})
. Since
p\geq 1
, this implies that
b^{-1}u_{k}\to w
in the
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
topology as well, hence
w=b^{-1}u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V})
.
Moreover, taking the limit as
k\to\infty
in the equivalence
\left\lVert b^{-1}u_{k}\right\rVert_{C_{0}([0,\infty);\;\textup{H}^{p}_{V})}\eqsim\left\lVert b^{-1}f_{k}\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
yields that
\left\lVert b^{-1}u\right\rVert_{C_{0}([0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V})}\eqsim\left\lVert b^{-1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}
, and that
b^{-1}u(0,\cdot)=b^{-1}f
in
\textup{H}^{p}_{V}
(by Proposition
8.5
).
We obtain in a similar manner that
u\in C^{\infty}\left((0,\infty);\textup{H}^{p}_{V}\right)
, and this proves (iii).

To prove (iv), let us now assume that
p\geq p_{+}(H)
. Let
T>0
and
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be compact. It follows from Proposition
8.6
that
u_{k}\in C([0,T];\mathrm{L}^{2}(K))
for all
k\geq 1
, and that

\left\lVert u_{k}-u_{j}\right\rVert_{C([0,T];\;\mathrm{L}^{2}(K))}:=\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\lVert u_{k}(t,\cdot)-u_{j}(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(K)}\leq c_{K,T}\left\lVert f_{k}-f_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all
j,k\geq 1
, where
c_{K,T}
is a constant depending on
K
and
T
. It follows from completeness of
C([0,T];\mathrm{L}^{2}(K))
that there exists some
w\in C([0,T];\mathrm{L}^{2}(K))
such that
\left\lVert u_{k}-w\right\rVert_{C([0,T];\mathrm{L}^{2}(K))}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. Since
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, this implies that
u|_{(0,T]\times K}=w
and therefore
u\in C((0,T];\mathrm{L}^{2}(K))
. We can pass to the limit as
k\to\infty
to obtain the estimate

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(K)}\leq c_{K,T}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

This proves (iv). Finally, we obtain the non-tangential almost everywhere convergence in (ii) using the well-known density argument (recalled in
[
11
, Section 17.3]
) relying on the estimate

\left\lVert N_{*}(u-u_{k})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert b^{-1}(f-f_{k})\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{p}_{V}}

and the identity (
8.4
) applied to
u_{k}
.
∎

9.2.
The Regularity problem

Before proving the solvability and estimates for Theorem
1.3
, we provide justification for the statement made in the introduction that
[
51
, Theorem 1.1]
already implies that the Regularity problem
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{2}
is well-posed if
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\left\{\frac{n}{2},2\right\}
,
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
V\not\equiv 0
. The reason we need an extra argument is because the type of convergence of the solution towards the boundary data that was obtained in
[
51
, Theorem 1.1]
is
a priori
different from the one considered in the problem
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{2}
stated in the introduction.

To this end, we first note that Proposition
2.7
shows that any
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
belongs to
\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and therefore belongs to the space
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
defined in
[
51
, Section 2.1]
(recall Remark
2.4
). Now, let
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
be the solution to the regularity problem provided by
[
51
, Theorem 1.1]
for data
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since
N_{*}(\nabla_{t,x}u)\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we claim that a.e. convergence of
\mathrm{L}^{2}
-Whitney averages
\scriptstyle{N_{*}}
-
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)=\nabla_{\mu}f
and the
\mathrm{L}^{2}
convergence
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)-\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=0
obtained in
[
51
, Theorem 1.1]
together imply the convergence

(9.1)

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)-f(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0,

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. To see this, we note that
[
11
, Proposition A.5]
with
q=r=p=2
shows that there exists some
u_{0}\in\dot{\textup{W}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

(9.2)

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)-u_{0}(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\right)^{1/2}\lesssim tN_{*}(\nabla_{t,x}u)(x)

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
t>0
. In addition,
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{t/2}^{2t}u(s,\cdot)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s=u_{0}
in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The later identity implies that
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{t/2}^{2t}\langle\nabla_{x}u(s,\cdot),\varphi\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s=\langle\nabla_{x}u_{0},\varphi\rangle
for all
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})
, which combined with the convergence
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\left\lVert\nabla_{x}u(t,\cdot)-\nabla_{x}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=0
shows that
\nabla_{x}u_{0}=\nabla_{x}f
so there is
c\in\mathbb{C}
such that
u_{0}=f+c
. To see that
c=0
, we note that (
9.2
) and Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem imply that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|V^{1/2}(y)u(s,y)-V^{1/2}(x)u_{0}(x)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x,t)}V\right)^{1/2}tN_{*}\left(\nabla_{t,x}u\right)(x)+\left|u_{0}(x)\right|\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x,t)}\left|V^{1/2}(y)-V^{1/2}(x)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\right)\to 0

as
t\to 0^{+}
, for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. When combined with the non-tangential convergence
\scriptstyle{N_{*}}
-
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}V^{1/2}u(t,\cdot)=V^{1/2}f
, this shows that
V^{1/2}(x)u_{0}(x)=V^{1/2}(x)f(x)
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since
V\not\equiv 0
, it follows that
c=0
, and this proves (
9.1
), so
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{2}
is well-posed.

The following result contains the solvability and estimates for Theorem
1.3
in the range
p>1
. We follow the proof of
[
11
, Theorem 1.2]
outlined in
[
11
, Section 17.3]
.

Theorem 9.2
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
{p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee 1,q_{+}(H))}
and
p\leq 2q
. If
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
, then the Regularity problem
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
for data
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
admits a weak solution
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
with the following additional properties:

(i)

The following estimates hold:

\left\lVert S(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\gtrsim\left\lVert A_{\perp\perp}H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}},

where the square root
H^{1/2}
extends by density to
{H^{1/2}:\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\to\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
.

(ii)

For almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
t>0
it holds that

\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)-f(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\right)^{1/2}\lesssim tN_{*}\left(\nabla_{t,x}u\right)(x),

hence
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)-f(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Moreover,

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}u(t,\cdot)=f\text{ in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right).

(iii)

For almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
it holds that

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|\begin{bmatrix}A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u\\
\nabla_{\mu}^{\|}u\end{bmatrix}\left(s,y\right)-\begin{bmatrix}(-A_{\perp\perp}H^{1/2}f)(x)\\
\left(\nabla_{\mu}f\right)(x)\end{bmatrix}\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0.

(iv)

It holds that
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\in C_{0}\left(\left[0,\infty\right);\mathrm{L}^{p}\right)\cap C^{\infty}\left((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p}\right)
, where
t
is the distinguished variable, and
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\left(0,\cdot\right)=\nabla_{\mu}f
, with

\sup_{t\geq 0}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\left(t,\cdot\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

(v)

If
p<n
, then
u\in C_{0}\left(\left[0,\infty\right);\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}\right)\cap C^{\infty}\left((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}\right)
, and
u(0,\cdot)=f
, with

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\leq\sup_{t\geq 0}\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}.

(vi)

If
p>p_{-}(H)
, then
\partial_{t}u\in C_{0}\left([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p}\right)
and

\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\partial_{t}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\sup_{t\geq 0}\left\lVert\partial_{t}u\left(t,\cdot\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert A_{\perp\perp}H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

Proof.

If
V\equiv 0
, then this is
[
11
, Theorem 1.2]
. We can therefore assume that
V\not\equiv 0
. Let
p\in\left(p_{-}(H)_{*}\;,q_{+}(H)\right)\cap(1,2q]
. We first treat the case when
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The identifications of Theorem
6.1
(and
\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})=\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
) then imply that
f\in\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{D}(H^{1/2})
. It follows from Figure
1
that
H^{1/2}f\in\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}\cap\mathsf{R}(H^{1/2})
with the estimates

(9.3)

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}=\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}}\eqsim\left\lVert H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}}\gtrsim\left\lVert H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

The last inequality follows from the continuous inclusion
\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{p}\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}
for
p\in(1,p_{+}(H))
, which follows from Proposition
6.6
and Proposition
6.12
, as well as the fact that
p<q_{+}(H)<p_{+}(H)
(Proposition
4.4
). Since
1<p\leq 2q
, Proposition
2.9
and the estimate (
9.3
) show that
H^{1/2}
extends by density to a bounded mapping from
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
As before, Theorem
7.2
shows that
u(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
. Also, note that

\partial_{t}u=-H^{1/2}e^{-tH^{1/2}}f=e^{-tH^{1/2}}(-H^{1/2}f)=e^{-tH^{1/2}}g,

where
g:=-H^{1/2}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
by (
9.3
).

Now let
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be arbitrary. By Proposition
2.9
again, we can find a sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}
such that
f_{k}\to f
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
k\to\infty.

We now consider two cases:

Case 1.

Let us first assume that
p<n
. Then it follows from Proposition
2.7
that
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
also converges to
f
in the
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}
norm. For each
k\geq 1
, let
u_{k}(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f_{k}
be the associated weak solution. For all
j,k\geq 1
, we have that
{(u_{k}-u_{j})(t,\cdot)=e^{-tH^{1/2}}(f_{k}-f_{j})}
, and it follows from Proposition
8.10
that

\left\lVert u_{k}-u_{j}\right\rVert_{C_{0}([0,\infty);\,\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})}\eqsim\left\lVert f_{k}-f_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}.

This implies that
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})
, and consequently also in
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
. By Lemma
7.1
, there is a weak solution
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
such that
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
. Moreover,
u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})
with
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{C_{0}([0,\infty);\,\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}}
.
Next, we observe that for all
j,k\geq 1
, by Proposition
8.8
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}u_{k}-\nabla_{\mu}u_{j}\right\rVert_{\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}}=\left\lVert N_{*}(\nabla_{\mu}(u_{k}-u_{j}))\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f_{k}-f_{j}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}.

Consequently, the sequence
(\nabla_{\mu}u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
and there is
v\in\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
such that
\nabla_{\mu}u_{k}\to v
in
\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
. In particular, convergence also holds in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
. Since
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, we obtain that
v=\nabla_{\mu}u\in\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
, with
\left\lVert N_{*}(\nabla_{\mu}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}
.
Next, we note that
[
51
, Lemma 4.2]
shows that
\partial_{t}u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
since it is also a weak solution (by
t
-independence of the coefficients). Proposition
8.7
shows that the sequence
(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\textup{T}^{p}
, so there is
w\in\textup{T}^{p}
such that
t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u_{k}\to w
in
\textup{T}^{p}
as
k\to\infty
. Convergence also holds in the
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
topology. The estimate of
[
51
, Lemma 4.2]
can be applied to obtain that

\displaystyle\iint_{W}\left|\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}(u_{k}-u)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\frac{1}{\ell(W)^{2}}\iint_{2W}\left|\nabla_{x,t}(u_{k}-u)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

for all Whitney cubes
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
. The convergence
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
therefore implies that
\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u_{k}\to\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
. We conclude that
w=t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u\in\textup{T}^{p}
and we obtain the estimate

\left\lVert S(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}.

We now prove strong continuity of the solutions in the variable
t
. It follows from Proposition
8.10
that
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u_{k}-\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u_{j}\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
with
\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u_{k}-\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u_{j}\right\rVert_{C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})}\eqsim\left\lVert f_{k}-f_{j}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}
, and
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u_{k}-\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u_{j}\in C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})

with
\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u_{k}-\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u_{j}\right\rVert_{C^{m}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})}\lesssim_{m}\left\lVert f_{k}-f_{j}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}
for all
m\geq 1
. There is
v\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
such that
\nabla_{\mu}u_{k}\to v
in
C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
. Convergence also holds in
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
and therefore
v=\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
, with
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(0,\cdot)=\nabla_{\mu}f
and
\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\right\rVert_{C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}
.
Similarly, we obtain that
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\in C^{m}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
for all
m\geq 1
, with the corresponding estimates, whence
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\in C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
. The continuous embedding
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
implies that
u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})
.
Since
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}u(t,\cdot)=f
in
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, this trivially implies that
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}u(t,\cdot)=f
in the distributional sense.

Now, to prove (iii), we use the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
9.1
, relying instead on the second part of Proposition
8.8
and the estimates

(9.4)

\left\lVert N_{*}(\nabla_{\mu}u-\nabla_{\mu}u_{k})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f-f_{k}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}\gtrsim\left\lVert H^{1/2}f-H^{1/2}f_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

for all
k\geq 1
. Item (ii) is proved as in
[
11
, Section 17.3]
, once we note that
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})\subseteq\textup{W}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, and that

\left\lVert N_{*}(\nabla_{t,x}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\leq\left\lVert N_{*}(\nabla_{\mu}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}<\infty.

Let us now treat item (vi) and assume that
p>p_{-}(H)
. Then, we can use Propositions
8.4
and
8.5
to obtain that
\partial_{t}u_{k}=e^{-tH^{1/2}}(-H^{1/2}f_{k})\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
for all
k\geq 1
, with

\displaystyle\left\lVert N_{*}(\partial_{t}u_{k}-\partial_{t}u_{j})\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}

\displaystyle\eqsim\sup_{t\geq 0}\left\lVert\partial_{t}u_{k}(t,\cdot)-\partial_{t}u_{j}(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert H^{1/2}f_{k}-H^{1/2}f_{j}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f_{k}-f_{j}\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}

for all
k,j\geq 1
. We have used Theorem
1.1
in addition to (
9.4
) to obtain the last equivalence. The sequence
(\partial_{t}u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
, so there is
v\in\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
such that
\partial_{t}u_{k}\to v
in
\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
. Convergence also holds in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, and thus
v=\partial_{t}u\in\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
, with
\left\lVert N_{*}(\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}
. In addition, the sequence
(\partial_{t}u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
, hence there exists
w\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
such that
\partial_{t}u_{k}\to w
in
C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
. Convergence also holds in
\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, whence
w=\partial_{t}u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
, with the estimate
\left\lVert\partial_{t}u\right\rVert_{C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}\eqsim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}
.

Case 2.

Let us now assume that
p\geq n
. We can still find a sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset\mathcal{V}^{1,2}\cap\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}
such that
f_{k}\to f
in
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}
, but, in contrast with Case 1, convergence no longer holds in the
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
norm. As usual, we let
u_{k}(t,\cdot):=e^{-tH^{1/2}}f_{k}
be the weak solution associated to the data
f_{k}
. As in Case 1, it follows from the first estimate of Proposition
8.8
that the sequence
(\nabla_{\mu}u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
, hence we can find some
v\in\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
such that
\nabla_{\mu}u_{k}\to v
in
\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
, whence also in the
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
topology.
Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
0<a<b
such that
b-a=\ell(Q)
.
For arbitrary
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, the local Fefferman–Phong inequality for the
t
-independent extension
V\in\textup{RH}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
and the cube
(a,b)\times Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
yields

\min\left\{\ell(Q)^{-1},\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}V\right)^{1/2}\right\}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}((a,b)\times Q)}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}((a,b)\times Q)}.

Since
V
is not identically zero, for all sufficiently large cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the constant
C(Q,V):=\min\left\{\ell(Q)^{-1},\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{Q}V\right)^{1/2}\right\}
is strictly positive. Since
(\nabla_{\mu}u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
converges in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, this shows that
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is Cauchy in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
. Let
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
be such that
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
as
k\to\infty
. It follows from Lemma
7.1
that
u
is a weak solution and that
u_{k}\to u
in
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, whence
v=\nabla_{\mu}u\in\textup{T}^{0,p}_{\infty}
, with the estimate
\left\lVert N_{*}(\nabla_{\mu}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}
.
All properties of the solution
u
follow as in the case
p<n
, except for (ii), where we have explicitly used the fact that
u\in C_{0}([0,\infty),\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}})
. We must proceed differently. Since
p\geq n\geq 2>p_{-}(H)
, (vi) can be applied to obtain that
\partial_{t}u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. Moreover, it follows from
[
51
, Lemma 4.2]
that
u\in C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, hence for a fixed compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and for all
0<t_{0}<t_{1}\leq 1
we can use the fundamental theorem of calculus to estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert u(t_{1},\cdot)-u(t_{0},\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(K)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\left\lVert\partial_{t}u(s,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(K)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\leq\left\lvert K\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\left\lVert\partial_{t}u(s,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert K\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\left|t_{1}-t_{0}\right|^{1/2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left\lVert\partial_{t}u(s,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2},

where we have used Minkowski’s inequality. This shows that
\left\{u(t,\cdot)\right\}_{0<t\leq 1}
is a Cauchy net in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Consequently, there is some
F\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}u(t,\cdot)=F
in the
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
topology, so
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\nabla_{x}u(t,\cdot)=\nabla_{x}F
in the distributional sense. Since we know that
\nabla_{x}u(t,\cdot)\to\nabla_{x}f
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as
t\to 0
, we find that
\nabla_{x}F=\nabla_{x}f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}
. In particular, there is a
c\in\mathbb{C}
such that
F=f+c
. For all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t>0
, we can therefore decompose

\displaystyle cV(x)^{1/2}=V(x)^{1/2}(F(x)-u(t,x))+V(x)^{1/2}(u(t,x)-f(x)).

Thus, by Hölder’s inequality, we obtain for all
t>0
and all compact subsets
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\left|c\right|\int_{K}V^{1/2}\leq\left(\int_{K}V\right)^{1/2}\left\lVert u(t,\cdot)-F\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(K)}+\left\lvert K\right\rvert^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)-\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

Letting
t\to 0
implies that
\left|c\right|\int_{K}V^{1/2}=0
. Since
V
is not identically zero, choosing
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
sufficiently large implies that
c=0
. Consequently,
F=f
and
\lim_{t\to 0}u(t,\cdot)=f
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
We can finally apply
[
11
, Proposition A.5]
as in the case
p<n
to find a non-tangential trace
u_{0}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{t/2}^{2t}u(s,\cdot)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s=u_{0}
in the distributional sense. The
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
convergence implies that the limit above also holds in the
\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
topology. It follows that
u_{0}=f
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and this finishes the proof.∎

We conclude this section with the solvability and estimates for Theorem
1.3
in the range
p\in(\frac{n}{n+1},1]
.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
and
V\!\in\!\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Suppose that
p\!\in\!\left(p_{-}(H)_{*}\!\vee\!\frac{n}{n+1},1\right]
. If
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then the Regularity problem
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
for data
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}
admits a weak solution
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
with the following additional properties:

(i)

The following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\left\lVert S(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}\quad\textup{and}\quad\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}.

Moreover, if
{p\in\left(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2},1\right]\cap\mathcal{I}(V)}
, then the reverse square function estimate holds,
and if
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}
and
p\in\mathcal{I}(V)
, then the reverse non-tangential maximal function estimate holds.

(ii)

Properties
(ii)
and
(v)
from Theorem
9.2
hold.

(iii)

If
p\in\left(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2},1\right]\cap\,\mathcal{I}(V)
, then
u\in C_{0}\left(\left[0,\infty\right);\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}\right)\cap C^{\infty}\left((0,\infty);\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}\right)
, where
t
is the distinguished variable, and
u\left(0,\cdot\right)=f
, with
\sup_{t\geq 0}\left\lVert u\left(t,\cdot\right)\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}}
.

Proof.

Again, if
V\equiv 0
, this follows from
[
11
, Theorem 1.2]
, and we can therefore assume that
V\not\equiv 0
. Let
p\in\left(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+1},1\right]
and
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By definition (recall Section
3.2.2
), we have
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and there exists a sequence
(f_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
f_{k}\to f
in both
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathrm{L}^{p^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We can then follow the proof of Theorem
9.2
(in Case 1, i.e.
p<n
), using Propositions
8.7
,
8.9
and
8.11
in the range
\frac{n}{n+1}<p\leq 1
.
∎

10.
Uniqueness for Theorems
1.2
and
1.3

In this last section, we prove uniqueness results for solutions of the Dirichlet problem
\left(\mathcal{D}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
and of the Regularity problem
\left(\mathcal{R}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
. We apply the strategy used in
[
11
, Chapter 21]
, which originates in
[
10
]
, to prove the following two theorems, which complete the proofs of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
. Throughout this section, we assume that
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\left\{\frac{n}{2},2\right\}
and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Theorem 10.1
.

Suppose that
V\not\equiv 0
and
p\in(p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+1}\,,p_{+}(H))
.
If either
p\leq 1
and
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, or
p>1
and
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then the modified Regularity problem for data
f

\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}\hskip 14.22636pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathscr{H}u=0\text{ weakly in }\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+};\\
N_{*}(\nabla_{t,x}u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\\
\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)-f(x)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0\text{ for a.e. }x\in\mathbb{R}^{n};\end{array}\right.

admits at most one solution in
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
.

Note that only the non-tangential estimate on the gradient of the solution is required to obtain uniqueness, and not the full control
N_{*}(\nabla_{\mu}u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}
.

Theorem 10.2
.

Suppose that
p\in[1,\infty)\cap(p_{-}(H),p_{+}(H)^{*})
. If either
p>1
and
f\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, or
p=1
and
f\in b\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then the Dirichlet problem
\left(\mathcal{D}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
admits at most one solution in
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
.

10.1.
Further estimates and representations for solutions

In order to prove these theorems, first note that since the equation
\mathscr{H}u=0
is linear, it suffices to prove that a solution
u
to
\mathscr{H}u=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
with zero boundary data vanishes identically (almost everywhere) on
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
. The first basic result in this direction is the following.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
be a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
such that

(10.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
\langle u,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})}=0
for all
G=\partial_{t}\widetilde{G}
,
\widetilde{G}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, then
u=0
almost everywhere.

Proof.

Since
u\in\textup{W}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, we can integrate by parts to find that
\langle\partial_{t}u,\tilde{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})}=0
for all
\tilde{G}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
. It follows that
\partial_{t}u=0
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
. Since
u\in C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
(iterate
[
51
, Lemma 4.2]
as in the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 16.9]
), there exists
g\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
u(t,\cdot)=g
for all
t>0
. It follows from (
10.1
) and the equality

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.44173pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.81091pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.2152pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.04697pt}}\!\int_{B(x,t)}\left|g(y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

that
g(x)=0
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, by Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem.
∎

We now introduce the Hilbert space

\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}):=\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}_{+}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}),

with the inner-product
\langle u,v\rangle_{\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}}:={\langle\nabla_{\mu}u,\nabla_{\mu}v\rangle}_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
. Again,
2^{*}_{+}=\frac{2(n+1)}{(n+1)-2}
is the upper Sobolev exponent in dimension
n+1
. Note that
\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
coincides with the space
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
defined in
[
51
, Section 2]
, which shows that
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
is dense in
\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, so using the argument outlined at the start of Section
7
, the following ellipticity estimate holds for all
u\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
:

(10.2)

\operatorname{Re}{{\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}u,\nabla_{\mu}u\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})}}}\geq\lambda\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})}^{2}.

Recall the definition of
\mathcal{A}
in (
7.1
), and that
V
and
A
are extended to
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}
by
t
-independence.

We now consider the sesquilinear form
\dot{h}:\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})\times\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})\to\mathbb{C}
,
defined as
\dot{h}(u,v):=\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}u,\nabla_{\mu}v\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})}
for all
u,v\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
.
For fixed
u\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, we define
\dot{\mathscr{H}}u\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})^{*}
by

(10.3)

\langle\dot{\mathscr{H}}u,v\rangle:=\dot{h}(u,v)

for all
v\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, where the angled brackets represent the action of the dual space
\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})^{*}
.
By the ellipticity (
10.2
), we have
\operatorname{Re}{{\dot{h}(u,u)}}\gtrsim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}=\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})}^{2}
, so the Lax–Milgram theorem shows that the mapping
\dot{\mathscr{H}}:\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})\to\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})^{*}
is a (bounded) linear isomorphism. In particular, we can consider the inverse operator

\dot{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}:\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})^{*}\to\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
.

Let us note that any test function
G\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
is naturally identified with an element of the dual
\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})^{*}
, as the linear functional

(10.4)

\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})\to\mathbb{C}:\varphi\mapsto\langle\varphi,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})}.

Continuity of the functional follows by a Sobolev embedding in
1+n>2
dimensions as follows:

\displaystyle\left|\langle\varphi,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2n+2}{n+3}}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2n+2}{n-1}}}\lesssim\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2n+2}{n+3}}}\left\lVert\nabla_{t,x}\varphi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\lesssim\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}}.

We shall need the following analogue of
[
10
, Lemma 3.1]
for Schrödinger operators.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
G\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
. If
u:=\dot{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(G)\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, then the following properties hold for all integers
k\geq 1
:

(i)

\partial_{t}^{k}u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, and
\dot{\mathscr{H}}(\partial_{t}^{k}u)=\partial_{t}^{k}G
.

(ii)

\partial_{t}^{k}u\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, with
t\in\mathbb{R}
being the distinguished variable.

Proof.

We first prove (i). This is similar to the proof of
[
51
, Lemma 4.2]
, except that
G
is not identically zero.
Let
e_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{1+n}
be the unit vector in the positive
t
direction. We consider the difference quotient
\mathbb{D}_{0}^{h}u:\mathbb{R}^{1+n}\to\mathbb{C}
, defined as

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{0}^{h}u(s,y):=h^{-1}\left[(u((s,y)+he_{0})-u((s,y))\right]

for all
(s,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{1+n}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
.
By
t
-independence of
\mathcal{A}
, we get
\dot{\mathscr{H}}\left(\mathbb{D}^{h}_{0}u\right)=\mathbb{D}^{h}_{0}G
for all
h\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
.
It follows from Caccioppoli’s inequality (
7.2
) that for all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{1+n}
we have

\displaystyle\iint_{Q}\left|\nabla_{\mu}(\mathbb{D}^{h}_{0}u)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(Q)^{-2}\iint_{2Q}\left|\mathbb{D}^{h}_{0}u\right|^{2}+\left(\iint_{2Q}\left|\mathbb{D}^{h}_{0}u\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\iint_{2Q}\left|\mathbb{D}^{h}_{0}G\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ell(Q)^{-2}\left\lVert\partial_{t}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\lVert\partial_{t}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\partial_{t}G\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}},

where we have used
[
35
, Lemma 7.23]
. Letting
\ell(Q)\to\infty
, this yields

(10.5)

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}}\left|\nabla_{\mu}(\mathbb{D}_{0}^{h}u)\right|^{2}\lesssim\left\lVert\partial_{t}u\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\left\lVert\partial_{t}G\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

uniformly for all
h\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
.
Now, since
u\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
and
V
is
t
-independent, we clearly have that
\mathbb{D}^{h}_{0}u\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, with
\nabla_{\mu}\left(\mathbb{D}^{h}_{0}u\right)=\mathbb{D}^{h}_{0}(\nabla_{\mu}u)
.
It follows from this, (
10.5
) and the Sobolev difference quotient characterisation (see, e.g.,
[
35
, Lemma 7.24]
) that
\nabla_{\mu}u
has a weak
\partial_{t}
-derivative in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, denoted
\partial_{t}\nabla_{\mu}u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
.
It follows from the proof of
[
51
, Lemma 4.2]
that
\partial_{t}u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, with

(10.6)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\left(\partial_{t}u\right)=\partial_{t}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}),

from which it follows that
\dot{\mathscr{H}}(\partial_{t}u)=\partial_{t}G
.
Since
\partial_{t}^{k}G\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
for all
k\geq 1
, we can proceed by induction to obtain that
\partial_{t}^{k}u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
with
\nabla_{\mu}(\partial_{t}^{k}u)=\partial_{t}^{k}(\nabla_{\mu}u)
and
\dot{\mathscr{H}}(\partial_{t}^{k}u)=\partial_{t}^{k}G
.
The proof of (ii) follows as in the proof of
[
10
, Lemma 3.1]
from the vector-valued Sobolev embedding
\textup{W}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R};X)\subseteq C_{0}(\mathbb{R};X)
for the Banach space
X=\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
∎

We shall now use the qualitative information provided by the previous lemma to derive a representation formula for solutions
u
of
\dot{\mathscr{H}}u=G\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, which is the analogue of
[
11
, Corollary 20.3]
in our context. Recall that
b=A_{\perp\perp}^{-1}
.

Lemma 10.5
.

If
\widetilde{G}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
and
G=\partial_{t}\widetilde{G}
, then for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
it holds that

\dot{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(G)(t,\cdot)=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\textup{sgn}(t-s)e^{-\left|t-s\right|H^{1/2}}\left(b\widetilde{G}(s,\cdot)\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

as an absolutely convergent
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-valued Bochner integral.

Proof.

Let
u:=\dot{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(G)
, where
G=\partial_{t}\widetilde{G}
for some
\widetilde{G}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, and consider
F:=\begin{bmatrix}A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u\\
\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2})
. The proof of Lemma
10.4
shows that
F
can identified with an element of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n+2}))
, where
t
is the distinguished variable. We claim that
F_{t}:=F(t,\cdot)\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(D)}\cap\mathsf{D}(DB)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and that it solves the first-order system

(10.7)

\partial_{t}F_{t}+DBF_{t}=-\begin{bmatrix}G(t,\cdot)\\
0\end{bmatrix}

in the strong sense for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
(recall that
B\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{n+2}))
is defined in (
2.17
)).

Let us first prove that
F_{t}\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(D)}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. To this end, note that
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}_{+}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
can be identified with an element of
\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}_{+}}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}_{+}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
via Fubini’s theorem and therefore
u\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. By Lemma
10.4
(and by one-dimensional Sobolev embeddings, see also
[
11
, Corollary 16.9]
), we deduce that
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. It then follows from the smoothness (or just the continuity) of both
u
and
F
in the variable
t
that
u(t,\cdot)\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\nabla_{\mu}\left(u(t,\cdot)\right)=\left(\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\right)(t,\cdot)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since our assumptions on
V
imply that
\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subseteq\mathrm{L}^{2^{*}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by Proposition
2.7
, it follows from (
2.16
) that
F_{t}\in\overline{\mathsf{R}(D)}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, as claimed.

We now show that
BF_{t}\in\mathsf{D}(D)=\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathsf{D}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*})
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that the argument from the previous paragraph shows that

BF_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot)\\
A_{\parallel\parallel}\nabla_{x}u(t,\cdot)\\
aV^{1/2}u(t,\cdot)\end{bmatrix}.

We already know from Lemma
10.4
that
\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot)\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
For the other components, we let
A_{\parallel,\mu}:=\begin{bmatrix}A_{\parallel\parallel}&0\\
0&a\end{bmatrix}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{n+1}))
,
and we note that for
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the function
(\phi\varphi)(t,x):=\phi(t)\varphi(x)
satisfies
\nabla_{\mu}(\varphi\phi)(t,x)=\begin{bmatrix}\varphi(x)\phi^{\prime}(t)\\
\phi(t)\nabla_{\mu}\varphi(x)\end{bmatrix}
. It follows from an integration by parts in
t
that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\langle A_{\parallel,\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot),\nabla_{\mu}\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}-\langle A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}^{2}u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\right)\overline{\phi(t)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle A_{\parallel,\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot),\nabla_{\mu}\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\overline{\phi(t)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\langle A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\overline{\phi^{\prime}(t)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}}\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}u\cdot\overline{\nabla_{\mu}(\varphi\phi)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\langle\dot{\mathscr{H}}u,\phi\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}G(t,\cdot)\overline{\varphi}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)\overline{\phi(t)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.

By the fundamental lemma of the calculus of variations, and smoothness in the variable
t
, it follows that

\displaystyle\langle A_{\parallel,\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot),\nabla_{\mu}\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\langle G(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}+\langle A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}^{2}u(t,\cdot),\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. This means that
A_{\parallel,\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)\in\mathsf{D}(\nabla_{\mu}^{*})
, with
\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\left(A_{\parallel,\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u(t,\cdot)\right)=G(t,\cdot)+A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}^{2}u(t,\cdot)
. Consequently,
BF_{t}\in\mathsf{D}(D)
and by (
10.6
) we are justified in writing

\displaystyle DBF_{t}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&-\nabla_{\mu}^{*}\\
-\nabla_{\mu}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{t}u\\
A_{\parallel,\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}u\end{bmatrix}_{t}=\begin{bmatrix}-A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}^{2}u(t,\cdot)-G(t,\cdot)\\
-\nabla_{\mu}^{\parallel}(\partial_{t}u)\end{bmatrix}=-\partial_{t}F_{t}-\begin{bmatrix}G(t,\cdot)\\
0\end{bmatrix}

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, which proves (
10.7
). We can then follow the Proof of Proposition 4.5 in
[
10
, Section 7]
, along with that of
[
11
, Proposition 20.2]
to obtain (
10.5
).
∎

Our next goal is to be able to relate
\dot{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(G)(t,\cdot)
to the boundary value
\dot{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(G)(0,\cdot)
. This requires the following boundedness properties of the Poisson semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH^{1/2}}\right\}_{t>0}
, analogous to
[
11
, Proposition 12.5]
.

Lemma 10.6
.

If
p_{-}(H)\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}<p\leq r<p_{+}(H)
, then the Poisson semigroup
\left\{e^{-tH^{1/2}}\right\}_{t>0}
is
b\textup{H}^{p}_{V}-b\textup{H}^{r}_{V}
-bounded. If in addition
p_{-}(H^{\sharp})<1
, then it is
b\textup{H}^{p}_{V}-\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-bounded.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
11
, Proposition 12.5]
, using the fact that the Riesz transform
R_{H}
is
\mathrm{L}^{2_{*}}
-bounded by Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
4.5
. We also rely on Corollary
6.11
and Proposition
3.21
to prove the first part.
For the second part, we proceed as in the aforementioned reference, except that for
0<\alpha<n\left(\frac{1}{p_{-}(H^{\sharp})}-1\right)
, we pick
\rho\in(p_{-}(H^{\sharp})\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta/2},1)
such that
\alpha=n\left(\frac{1}{\rho}-1\right)
. Then, we proceed by similarity and duality by using Lemma
3.23
(noting that
\frac{n}{n+1\wedge\delta}\leq\frac{n}{n+\delta/2}
). We conclude with the interpolation inequality of
[
11
, Lemma 12.6]
and the fact that
\Lambda^{\alpha}_{V}
embeds continuously into the space of
\alpha
-Hölder continuous functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
∎

The previous two lemmas allow us to obtain the following analogue of
[
11
, Lemma 20.4]
.

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
\widetilde{G}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, and consider
G:=\partial_{t}\widetilde{G}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
. Let
F:=\dot{\mathscr{H}}^{-1}(G)\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}).
Assume that
\textup{supp}\left(\widetilde{G}\right)\subseteq[\beta^{-1},\beta]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
\beta>1
. Let
f:=F(0,\cdot)\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then, for
r\in(p_{-}(H)\vee 1,p_{+}(H))
, there exists
\gamma>0
such that the following estimates hold for all
t>0
:

\left\lVert F(t,\cdot)-e^{-tH^{1/2}}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}\lesssim t\wedge t^{-\gamma}\quad\textup{and}\quad\left\lVert\partial_{t}\left(F(t,\cdot)-e^{-tH^{1/2}}f\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{r}}\lesssim 1\wedge t^{-1-\gamma},

where the implicit constants also depend on
\beta
. If in addition
p_{-}(H^{\sharp})<1
, then both estimates also hold for
r=\infty.

Finally, we shall need a version of the technical lemma
[
11
, Lemma 21.3]
. In our setting, it trivially extends to the following result.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
0<p<r\leq 2
and let
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
be a weak solution of
\dot{\mathscr{H}}u=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
such that
\left\lVert N_{*}(\nabla_{t,x}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}<\infty
. Then,

\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}}\left|\nabla_{t,x}u\right|^{r}t^{n(\frac{r}{p}-1)}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{1/r}\lesssim\left\lVert N_{*}(\nabla_{t,x}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

If in addition
p>\frac{n}{n+1}
and the boundary limit (
10.1
) holds, then also

\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}}\left|u\right|^{r}t^{n(\frac{r}{p}-1)-r}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{1/r}\lesssim\left\lVert N_{*}(\nabla_{t,x}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

Proof.

Apply
[
11
, Proposition A.5]
and
[
11
, Lemma A.3]
along with the reverse Hölder estimates (
7.3
) for weak solutions.
∎

10.2.
The Dirichlet and Regularity problems

We now follow the method described in
[
11
, Chapter 21]
(or, equivalently,
[
10
]
) to simultaneously establish uniqueness of solutions for the problems
(\mathcal{D})^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
and
(\mathcal{R})^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
, using the results collected in the previous section.

Proof of Theorems
10.1
and
10.2
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
be a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
. Let
\widetilde{G}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
, and consider
G:=\partial_{t}\widetilde{G}
. By linearity we only need to show that
\langle u,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})}=0
provided
u
has some interior control (either
N_{*}(u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}
or
N_{*}(\nabla_{t,x}u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}
for some
p
) and vanishes at the boundary in the sense of (
10.1
), since then Lemma
10.3
shows that
u=0
almost everywhere.

As in
[
11
, Chapter 21]
, in order to compute
\langle u,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})}
, we introduce a real-valued Lipschitz continuous cut-off function
\theta
with compact support in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
, such that
\theta=1
on
\textup{supp}\left(G\right)
. We consider the operator
\dot{\mathscr{H}}^{*}:\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})\to\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})^{*}
defined as in (
10.3
) above with
(A^{*},a^{*})
in place of
(A,a)
. By definition and (
10.4
), the function
F:=(\dot{\mathscr{H}}^{*})^{-1}(G)\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
satisfies

(10.8)

\langle\mathcal{A}^{*}\nabla_{\mu}F,\nabla_{\mu}\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\langle G,\varphi\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

for all
\varphi\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
.
Observe that
\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})\subset\mathrm{L}^{\frac{2n+2}{n-1}}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
by Sobolev embeddings on bounded smooth domains (see, e.g.,
[
35
, Theorem 7.26]
). As a consequence,
u\theta\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, and it can be used as a test function in (
10.8
) to obtain

(10.9)

\displaystyle\langle u,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle u,G\theta\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\langle u\theta,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\overline{\langle\mathcal{A}^{*}\nabla_{\mu}F,\nabla_{\mu}(u\theta)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}}=\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}(u\theta),\nabla_{\mu}F\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.

Similarly, note that
F\theta\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n})
, with

(10.10)

\nabla_{\mu}(F\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}F\nabla_{t,x}\theta\\
0\end{bmatrix}+\theta\nabla_{\mu}F.

Since
F\theta
has compact support, there is a sequence of suitably supported functions
(F_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
such that
\nabla_{\mu}F_{k}\to\nabla_{\mu}(F\theta)
in
\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
(see the proof of
[
51
, Lemma 2.2]
). As
\mathscr{H}u=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
(in the sense of
1.7
), we get

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{A}(\nabla_{\mu}u)\theta,\nabla_{\mu}F\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}u,\nabla_{\mu}(F\theta)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}-\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}u,\begin{bmatrix}F\nabla_{t,x}\theta\\
0\end{bmatrix}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=-\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}u,\begin{bmatrix}F\nabla_{t,x}\theta\\
0\end{bmatrix}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.

Using (
10.9
), and applying (
10.10
) with
u
instead of
F
, this implies that

(10.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle u,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}&=\langle\mathcal{A}\begin{bmatrix}u\nabla_{t,x}\theta\\
0\end{bmatrix},\nabla_{\mu}F\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}-\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}u,\begin{bmatrix}F\nabla_{t,x}\theta\\
0\end{bmatrix}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}\\
&=\langle Au\nabla_{t,x}\theta,\nabla_{t,x}F\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}-\langle A\nabla_{t,x}u,F\nabla_{t,x}\theta\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.\end{split}

By Lemma
10.5
, we can consider the trace
f:=F(0,\cdot)\in\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and its Poisson extension
F_{1}(t,\cdot):=e^{-t(H^{\sharp})^{1/2}}f
for
t>0
. We know from Theorem
7.2
that
F_{1}\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{2})\cap C^{\infty}((0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{2})
, with
F_{1}(0,\cdot)=f
, and that
F_{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}^{*}F_{1}=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+}
(in the sense of
1.7
). As above, we can use the fact that both
u\theta
and
F_{1}\theta\in\dot{\mathcal{X}}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
have compact support and that
u
and
F_{1}
are solutions to write

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{\mu}(u\theta),\mathcal{A}^{*}\nabla_{\mu}F_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}(u\theta),\nabla_{\mu}F_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{A}\begin{bmatrix}u\nabla_{t,x}\theta\\
0\end{bmatrix},\nabla_{\mu}F_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}+\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}u,\nabla_{\mu}(F_{1}\theta)\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}-\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\mu}u,\begin{bmatrix}F_{1}\nabla_{t,x}\theta\\
0\end{bmatrix}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle Au\nabla_{t,x}\theta,\nabla_{t,x}F_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}-\langle A\nabla_{t,x}u,F_{1}\nabla_{t,x}\theta\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}},

where we have used (
10.10
) twice. We can therefore subtract this quantity from
\langle u,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}
in (
10.11
) to obtain the following:

\displaystyle\langle u,G\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}=\langle Au\nabla_{t,x}\theta,\nabla_{t,x}(F-F_{1})\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}-\langle A\nabla_{t,x}u,(F-F_{1})\nabla_{t,x}\theta\rangle_{\mathrm{L}^{2}}.

This is precisely the same identity as in
[
11
, Equation (21.4)]
. We can proceed exactly as in
[
11
, Chapter 21]
, assuming that
\widetilde{G}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
is supported on
[\beta^{-1},\beta]\times B(0,\beta)
for some
\beta>0
and picking a cut-off function
\theta=\theta_{\varepsilon,M,R}
for suitable parameters
\varepsilon,M,R>0
.
In particular, using Lemma
10.8
, the reverse Hölder estimate (
7.3
) and Caccioppoli’s inequality (
7.2
) for weak solutions, as well as the estimates of Lemma
10.7
instead of
[
11
, Lemma 20.4]
, we obtain uniqueness of solutions to the Regularity problem
(\mathcal{R})^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
in the range
p_{-}(H)_{*}\vee\frac{n}{n+1}<p<p_{+}(H)
, and uniqueness of solutions to the Dirichlet problem
(\mathcal{D})^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
in the range
1\leq p<p_{+}(H)^{*}
if
p_{-}(H)<1
, or in the range
p_{-}(H)<p<p_{+}(H)^{*}
if
p_{-}(H)\geq 1
. Let us observe that we only need the non tangential control of the gradient
\nabla_{t,x}u
of the solution
u
, i.e.
\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{t,x}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}<\infty
, and not the full control
\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}<\infty
to obtain uniqueness.
This concludes the proof.
∎

11.
The Neumann problem and Theorem
1.4

In this final section, we combine Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
with identifications from Theorem
6.1
to prove well-posedness of the Neumann problem
\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
and Theorem
1.4
. The cornerstone of our argument is the following lemma, which enables us to go from Neumann-type boundary data to Regularity-type boundary data. Note that the inverse of the mapping
T_{p}
below is related to the so-called Neumann to Dirichlet map (see, e.g.,
[
14
, Section 3]
).

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
q\geq\max\{\frac{n}{2},2\}
, and
V\in\textup{RH}^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. If
p\in(p_{-}(H),q_{+}(H))\cap(1,2q]
, then the linear mapping
-A_{\perp\perp}H^{1/2}:\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
extends to an isomorphism

T_{p}:\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

If
p_{-}(H)<1
and
1\in\mathcal{I}(V)
, then it extends to an isomorphism

T_{1}:\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Proof.

Let us first consider the case when
p\in(p_{-}(H),q_{+}(H))\cap(1,2q]
. It then follows from Theorem
6.1
and Figure
1
that for all
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
there holds

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{1,p}_{H}}\eqsim\left\lVert H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{p}_{H}}\eqsim\left\lVert-A_{\perp\perp}H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

This estimate and Proposition
2.9
show that
-A_{\perp\perp}H^{1/2}
can be extended to a bounded mapping
T_{p}:\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfying
\left\lVert T_{p}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}
for all
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The invertibility of
T_{p}
follows from the density of
\mathsf{R}(H^{1/2})\cap\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in
\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(see Lemma
4.1
).

Let us now suppose that
p_{-}(H)<1
and
1\in\mathcal{I}(V)
. The self-improvement property from Lemma
2.1
.(i) shows that there exists
q_{0}>\frac{n}{2}
such that
V\in\textup{RH}^{q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. As a consequence, we have that
1\in\mathcal{I}(V)\cap(p_{-}(H)\vee\frac{n}{n+\delta_{0}/2},1]
, where
\delta_{0}:=2-\frac{n}{q_{0}}>0
, and it again follows from Theorem
6.1
and Figure
1
that for all
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
there holds

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{1,1}_{H}}\eqsim\left\lVert H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\mathbb{H}^{1}_{H}}\eqsim\left\lVert-A_{\perp\perp}H^{1/2}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}.

By completeness of
\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and density of
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in
\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(see Sections
3.1.2
and
3.2.2
), this allows us to extend
-A_{\perp\perp}H^{1/2}
to a bounded mapping
T_{1}:\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfying
\left\lVert T_{1}f\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}}
for all
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. As above, the invertibility of
T_{1}
follows from the density of
\mathsf{R}(H^{1/2})\cap\left(-A_{\perp\perp}^{-1}\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)
in
-A_{\perp\perp}^{-1}\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(when equipped with the natural norm mentioned at the start of Section
6
), which itself follows from the fact that
\mathbb{H}^{1}_{H}=-A_{\perp\perp}^{-1}\textup{H}^{1}_{V,\textup{pre}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with equivalent norms (see Theorem
6.1
) and that
\mathsf{R}(H^{1/2})\cap\mathbb{H}^{1}_{H}
is dense in
\mathbb{H}^{1}_{H}
(see
[
11
, Lemma 8.7]
).
∎

We may now prove the existence and uniqueness of a solution to the Neumann problem.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Let us first prove the existence of a solution with the desired properties. We first consider the case when
p\in(p_{-}(H),q_{+}(H))\cap(1,2q]
. Let
g\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and let
f:=T_{p}^{-1}g
, where
T_{p}:\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is as in Lemma
11.1
above. Applying Theorem
9.2
with the boundary data
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
yields a weak solution
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
of
\mathscr{H}u=0
such that

\left\lVert S(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}}\eqsim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

Items (iii) and (vi) of the same theorem also yield the non-tangential convergence

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|(A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u)(s,y)-g(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\right)^{1/2}=0

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and the strong continuity
A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u\in C_{0}([0,\infty);\mathrm{L}^{p})
, where
t
is the distinguished variable, along with the estimate

\left\lVert N_{*}(\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\sup_{t>0}\left\lVert A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}.

We also obtain the convergence
A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u(t,\cdot)\to g
in the
\mathrm{L}^{p}
norm, as
t\to 0^{+}
.

We now treat the case when
p=1
. Let
g\in\textup{H}^{1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and let us apply Theorem
9.3
with the boundary data
f:=T_{p}^{-1}g\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
T_{p}
has the same meaning as in the previous lemma. This provides us with a weak solution
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
of
\mathscr{H}u=0
, satisfying

\left\lVert S(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}\eqsim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}}\eqsim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}\gtrsim\left\lVert N_{*}\left(\nabla_{\mu}u\right)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{1}}.

Moreover, we note that the reverse estimate on the non-tangential maximal function also holds provided that
V\in\textup{RH}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
We observe that the proof of item (iii) of Theorem
9.2
now goes through to show that the solution
u
satisfies the non-tangential convergence

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|(A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u)(s,y)-(T_{1}f)(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\right)^{1/2}=0

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is the desired conclusion.

Let us now consider the question of uniqueness. Let
p\in(p_{-}(H),q_{+}(H))\cap[1,2q]
and let
u\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{1+n}_{+})
be a solution to the Neumann problem
\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
with zero boundary data. In other words,
u
is a weak solution of
\mathscr{H}u=0
with the nontangential control
N_{*}(\nabla_{\mu}u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and it satisfies the nontangential convergence

(11.1)

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|(A_{\perp\perp}\partial_{t}u)(s,y)\right|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=0

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
It follows from the
t
-independence of the coefficients that
\mathscr{H}(\partial_{t}u)=0
(see, e.g.,
[
51
, Lemma 4.2]
). Moreover, we have the nontangential control
N_{*}(\partial_{t}u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since
A_{\perp\perp}^{-1}
is bounded, these observations and (
11.1
) imply that
\partial_{t}u
solves the Dirichlet problem
(\mathcal{D})^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
with zero boundary data. It follows from Theorem
10.2
that
\partial_{t}u=0
. As a consequence, we know that there is some
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
u(t,x)=f(x)
for almost every
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see the proof of Lemma
10.3
). Since
N_{*}(\nabla_{t,x}u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
[
11
, Proposition A.5]
shows that there exists a nontangential trace
u_{0}\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(i.e.
u_{0}\in\mathrm{L}^{1}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\nabla_{x}u_{0}\in\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
) such that

(11.2)

\lim_{t\to 0}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-6.23328pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-5.58163pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-4.09418pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{-}\mkern-3.5mu{-}$}}\kern-3.32918pt}}\!\iint_{W(t,x)}\left|u(s,y)-u_{0}(x)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\right)^{1/2}=0

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. It follows from Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem that
u_{0}(x)=f(x)
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and therefore
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).
In addition, the nontangential control
N_{*}(V^{1/2}u)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the
t
-independence of
V
and
u
imply that the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator satisfies
\mathcal{M}(V^{1/2}f)\in\mathrm{L}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and therefore
V^{1/2}f\in\textup{H}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(see
[
59
, Chapter II, Section 2.1 and Chapter III, Theorem 1]
).
If
p>1
, this means that
f\in\dot{\mathcal{V}}^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and that
u
solves the Regularity problem
(\mathcal{R})^{\mathscr{H}}_{p}
with boundary data
f
. It follows from Theorem
1.3
that
\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}f\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}\eqsim\left\lVert S(t\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{t}u)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{p}}=0
, hence
f
is constant and therefore so is
u
, and that constant is zero if
V\not\equiv 0
.
∎

Remark 11.2
.

Let us remark that, in the context of the previous proof in the case when
p=1
, we can only infer that
f\in\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\textup{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is such that
\nabla_{\mu}f\in\textup{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{n+1}
. If this was enough to imply that
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then the same argument as in the case
p>1
would yield uniqueness for
\left(\mathcal{N}\right)^{\mathscr{H}}_{1}
. In this regard, we note that every
g\in\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\nabla_{\mu}g\in\textup{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{n+1}
satisfies

(11.3)

\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}_{V}}\lesssim\left\lVert\nabla_{\mu}g\right\rVert_{\textup{H}^{1}},

which follows from the assumption
1\in\mathcal{I}(V)
and Corollary
3.13
. Consequently, if one could prove existence of a sequence
(g_{k})_{k\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{V}^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\nabla_{\mu}g_{k}\to\nabla_{\mu}f
in
\textup{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{n+1}
, then (
11.3
) and Proposition
2.7
would yield the desired conclusion
f\in\dot{\textup{H}}^{1,1}_{V}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
